# A Practical Guide to Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback: Using Human Signals to Align AI Models

**Welcome to Packt Early Access**. We’re giving you an exclusive preview of this book before it goes on sale. It can take many months to write a book, but our authors have cutting-edge information to share with you today. Early Access gives you an insight into the latest developments by making chapter drafts available. The chapters may be a little rough around the edges right now, but our authors will update them over time.You can dip in and out of this book or follow along from start to finish; Early Access is designed to be flexible. We hope you enjoy getting to know more about the process of writing a Packt book.

# Chapter 1: Introduction to Reinforcement Learning

## Join our book community on Discord

![A qr code with a square in the middle Description automatically generated](media/file0.png)

[https://packt.link/EarlyAccessCommunity](https://packt.link/EarlyAccessCommunity)

This book is about aligning AI agents with human intent. Capabilities of AI have improved exponentially over the period of last two decades, and some milestones in disciplines such as machine learning, computer vision, natural language processing, deep learning, and **reinforcement learning** (**RL**) played a prominent role in this rise. Some of these capabilities and applications demonstrate how well AI scales and impacts almost everything. This makes it very important that the behavior or actions taken by AI align with human intent, and this very necessity is also the path towards safety of using this technology. In this book, we will delve into **Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback** (**RLHF**), which played a key role in the success of generated pretrained models and brought it to mainstream technology usage. The first part of the book introduces RLHF from a general perspective and develops the readers understanding of core technological components necessary for applying RLHF to any application. The second part dives into applying RLHF for language modeling especially large language modeling. The final part provides the readers with current trends and what lies ahead for this technology. In this chapter, we introduce RL to the readers by first covering the essentials of RL and key concepts for understanding RL algorithms by explaining their categories at a high level. Then we introduce a specific simple classic environment called the grid world, and show how **Q-learning**, a fundamental RL algorithm be implemented. Using the basic implementation for comparison and context, we then detail how reward functions can be designed, and how transfer learning can be implemented to accelerate RL training. Finally, we show how **Human Feedback** can help the RL based training, with a hands-on implementation on the same example, so that readers can clearly compare improvements over classic Q-learning implementation. This gives readers an idea of how RLHF works in practice and sets the stage for rest of the chapters to delve into more involved algorithms and architectures.We will cover the following main headings in this chapter:

*   Essentials of Reinforcement Learning
*   Understanding RL Algorithms
*   Navigating RL Environments: A Grid World Environment
*   Designing Reward Functions and Transfer Learning
*   Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback

## Technical requirements

For this chapter, we have kept all implementation related dependencies minimal. All implementation is in python. Specific version recommendations and the minimal required libraries that need to be installed are:

*   Python version >= 3.9
*   Numpy == 1.26.4
*   Matplotlib == 3.8.4

To get started, clone this git repository: [https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback](https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback), and change directory to `chapter1`.

## Essentials of Reinforcement Learning

RL is a paradigm of machine learning focused on how agents can learn to make decisions based on their interactions with the environment, to maximize specified cumulative rewards. In the context of artificial intelligence, an agent is any entity that perceives the environment and acts upon that environment, by making decisions autonomously. The agent learns through trial and error, receiving feedback in the form of rewards or penalties based on the states it reaches or remains in as a result of its actions. Over time, through this iterative process, the agent hones its decision-making abilities to maximize cumulative rewards.RL is based on the **state-action-reward framework**, where agents interact with their environment to learn optimal decision-making strategies. Perception of the environment, its present state, is conveyed to the agent through sensors, enabling it to make informed decisions. **States** represent various attributes of the environment, providing crucial information for decision-making.For instance, humans perceive the world through senses like vision, while robots may use cameras. These perceptions form the basis of states, encompassing objects, positions, and other environmental attributes. However, not all states are directly observable; hidden objects contribute to the present state but remain unobserved. Observations, therefore, represent the perceived states that agents utilize for decision-making. For the sake of conciseness, in our discussion, we will be using states as observations, since observations are expected to have all relevant states needed for decision making.Consider a robot navigating an environment based on camera inputs. It interprets images to decide **actions** such as moving right, left, or straight. These actions are executed using mechanisms like limbs or wheels. The agent employs decision-making processes, from simple rules to complex machine learning algorithms, to choose actions based on its perception of the environment.In RL, agents aim to maximize cumulative **rewards** by selecting actions that lead to favorable outcomes. Rewards serve as feedback, guiding the agent's learning process. Through repeated interactions, the agent refines its decision-making abilities, leveraging the state-action-reward framework to achieve optimal performance in diverse environments. In *Figure 1.1*, we show the **agent-environment** interaction using the state-action-reward framework.

![Figure 1.1 Agent-Environment Interaction](media/file1.png)

Figure 1.1 Agent-Environment Interaction

At a given iteration or time, based on the **initial state** of the environment , the agent takes action to change the environment, transitioning it to state s[t+1]. For each iteration, this change in state, if desirable, is assigned a higher positive reward , incentivizing the agent to take such actions. The decision-making strategy that causes an agent to take the actions based on the current state, is called the **policy**, usually notated as *π(s)*. The actions are input to the environment and cause the environment to alter or transition its states to the next state , the transition dynamics is usually represented as *.* The transition dynamics is dictated by the environment and is not in our or agent’s control. Depending upon the environment, it can be stochastic or deterministic.The estimate of expected cumulative reward over a sequence of states, starting from the initial state after following a particular policy is the **Value Function**, usually defined as *V(s),* and is a function of states. When Value Function is for a particular action taken for a particular state, it is termed as **Action Value Function** or **Q function**.

## Understanding RL Algorithms

The main task of RL algorithms is to optimize the policy so that the agent takes optimal actions leading to desired states. To optimize the actions, RL algorithms employ a variety of innovative techniques, but at the core, they use a reward associated with the agent being in a particular state, to drive the policy to come up with actions that are desirable. The differences in algorithms are mainly related to how states, actions, and rewards are used, stored, processed, and applied to come up with optimal policies, and how agent interacts with the environment to come up with those policies. RL algorithms can be classified based on several criteria. Some common categorization are:

*   **Model Based VS Model Free**: Model based algorithms involve learning a model of the environment and using it for planning actions. The model of the environment can be based on dynamic programming, monte carlo methods, or learning a transition model. On the contrary, model free algorithms do not model the environment, but directly learn by interaction with the environment. Examples of model free algorithms are **Q-learning**, **SARSA** and **Deep Q Networks** (**DQN**).
*   **Value-based vs Policy-based**: Value based algorithms estimate the value of being in a particular state and taking a particular action, and aim at maximizing the value function. Policy based algorithms directly parameterize the policy and learn optimal policy using gradient ascent approaches. A hybrid method, combining value and policy based algorithms, is called actor-critic algorithm. It maintains an actor (policy) and a critic (value function), and uses both of them to learn from experiences, balancing the advantages of both.
*   **Temporal-Difference vs Monte-Carlo vs Dynamic Programming**: In temporal difference learning the value function is updated based on difference between current and future rewards. Conversely, in **Monte Carlo approach**, the values are estimated based on total observed returns over the course of entire episode (sequence of states in a single run), and values are updated once every episode. Considering that entire trajectory of episode needs to be completed before value is updated, this is a known weakness of the Monte Carlo approach. Dynamic programming involves decomposing the problem into subproblems, and iteratively solving the problems. While dynamic programming algorithms are fundamental in building algorithms and are useful for known domains and finite state-action spaces, their computational complexity, time and memory requirements, are not scalable to large state-action spaces, and not suitable for environments with uncertainties.
*   **On-policy vs off-policy algorithms**: On-policy algorithms immediately follow the policy (take action), as the policy is being learned and updated. In case of off-policy algorithms, a target-policy is learned, while a separate policy (behavioral policy) is used for following the policy (by taking action).
*   **Single-agent vs Multi-agent algorithms**: Single-agent algorithms involve a single-agent interacting in the environment learning to achieve a goal. Multi-agent algorithms involve multiple agents interacting with each other in the environment collaborating, competing against or contributing independently towards a goal or a set of goals. There is coordination and communication between these agents, and sometimes it is an integration of multiple single agents.
*   **Tabular RL vs Function Approximation RL**: Tabular RL stores values and policies in tables explicitly, while in function approximation RL function approximators or models like neural networks are used to estimate value functions or policies, enabling them to handle large or continuous state and action spaces efficiently. Tabular RL are generally good for developing RL algorithms or understanding and gaining intuition, but are limited to smaller state-action spaces due to nature of their table storing property.
*   **Batch vs Online RL**: In Batch RL or Offline-RL, the agent learns from a fixed dataset collected offline, and experiences are from some past interactions, but there is no interaction with the environment during learning. Online-RL is typical case where the agent learns by interacting with the environment and consistently updating policy or value function as part of the learning process, as the observations are gathered during the experience.
*   **Exploration-Exploitation Strategies**: RL algorithms can also be categorized based on how the agent balances exploration and exploitation during training. When an agent tries new actions, discovers new scenarios, and how these new actions affect evolution of states and resulting rewards, it is called exploration. When an agent tries to maximize actions that are known to yield high reward, without taking risk with new actions, it is called exploitation. Some examples are **epsilon-greedy**, **Boltzmann exploration**, **Upper Confidence Bound**, and **Thompson Sampling**.

While the categorization of RL algorithms can be exhaustive, many of the algorithms fit within several of the categories above. For the purpose of **RLHF**, we will focus on a few key base algorithms, e.g. Q-learning in this chapter for value-based algorithm approach, and later introduce policy-based approach also in following chapters. Before, we go through a complete example, it is important to understand the concept of value function. Value function is used to evaluate the quality of states or state-action pairs within an environment. They provide a way to quantify the expected return or utility associated with being in a particular state or taking a specific action in each state *s* and following a given policy. This helps with knowing how desirable the state (and taking a specific action in that state) is over long-term. There are two ways to define value functions:

*   **State Value function**: It is the expected return (cumulative discounted reward) starting from a particular state s and following a given policy. Mathematically, the expected sum of discounted future rewards can be represented as: .
*   E is the expectation operator denoting expectation over possible future trajectories, *γ* is the discount factor (between 0 and 1) discounting future rewards to give more weight to immediate rewards, and is the reward received at time .
*   **Action Value function**: It is the expected return starting from a state *𝑠*, taking an action *a*, and then following a given policy. Mathematically, this expected sum of discounted future rewards can be represented as:
*   ,
*   with similar notations as for state value function.

From a system level and architectural perspective of the RLHF based AI system, it is easy to replace algorithms and consider states-actions as input-output variables that interact with rest of the AI system. For readers interested in diving deeper into these algorithms, there are several books theoretical as well as practical in nature (see the references at the end of the chapter). To understand the AI system and the agent-world interaction, we will use a classic grid world environment, and show how Q-learning algorithm works. Next, we will show how reward models come into play, to make the learning process more efficient.

## Navigating RL Environments: A Grid World Environment

In RL, agent-environment interaction is the scenario that needs to be optimized. In order to optimize this scenario, agents take optimal actions leading to desirable states. The sequence of actions that lead to optimal states is the policy.To train an agent, real world environments could be used. But, they can be dangerous, expensive, and sometimes inefficient. Training an agent to learn sufficiently complex policies can require millions of iterations. This is where simulations of real world environments can be helpful. While simulations may not capture the real world completely, only capturing relevant information and environmental properties that lead to state transitions relevant in real world are sufficient in many industrial and real world problems. In some cases, simulation scenarios and real world have a gap and pose a difficulty during real-world implementation, often termed as **sim2real gap**. This is more significant where simulation model involves physics and three-dimensional geometry based assets, and less of a problem for text, image, or applications where upon deployment of agents, the end environment is still a digital world.In this chapter, we introduce a grid world representation of the real world. The simplified environment will help us with the focus needed in learning the RL related concepts, without needing the cognitive overhead of a complex domain.The environment consists of a grid of cells. Each cell can be either accessible or inaccessible, forming a layout of the world. The agent is positioned in one of the cells within the grid. The agent can take actions to move from one cell to another. Using the state-action-reward framework, we define them in context of the grid world problem.

*   **States**: Each cell in the grid represents a state of the environment. The state of the environment changes as the agent moves around the grid.
*   **Actions**: The agent can take actions to move around the grid. Typically, the agent can move up, down, left, or right. However, depending on the variant of the problem, some actions may be unavailable due to obstacles or boundaries.
*   **Rewards**: Each cell in the grid may have a reward associated with it. The agent's objective is usually to maximize its total reward over time. Rewards can be positive, negative, or zero.
*   **Terminal States**: Some cells in the grid may be designated as terminal states. When the agent enters a terminal state, the episode ends, and the agent may either receive a final reward or not, depending on the problem setting.
*   **Policy**: The policy determines the agent's behavior in the environment. It maps states to actions, guiding the agent on how to select actions in different states to achieve its goal, which is often to maximize cumulative rewards.
*   **Transition Dynamics**: Moving from one state to another may not always be deterministic. There could be stochasticity involved, such as wind blowing the agent off course or the agent slipping in icy terrain.

To create a gridworld representation of the world, we first create a `GridWorld` class in python. Here are steps for building the gridworld environment.

1.  Initializing the environment: The `__init__` method initializes the grid world environment with its parameters such as grid size, start and goal positions, and obstacle positions. Number of states is initialized to the size of the grid. It also initializes **Q-values** for each state-action pair to zero.

```
    def __init__(self, grid_size, obstacle_positions):
        self.grid_size = grid_size
        self.start_position = (2, 2)
        self.goal_position = (5,5) #(grid_size[0] - 1, grid_size[1] - 1) for generalizing
        self.obstacle_positions = obstacle_positions
        self.actions = [(0, 1), (0, -1), (1, 0), (-1, 0)]  # right, left, down, up
        self.action_symbols = ['→', '←', '↓', '↑']  # Action symbols for visualization
        self.num_actions = len(self.actions)
        self.num_states = np.prod(self.grid_size)
        self.Q_values = np.zeros((self.num_states, self.num_actions))
```

1.  Methods for constructing the simulator: Then, we add an additional method to construct the grid world simulator. Below are methods for converting `state (i, j)` to its corresponding index in the Q-values array for that state, and vice-versa given index in the Q-values array to its corresponding `state (i, j)`.

```
def state_to_index(self, state):
        return state[0] * self.grid_size[1] + state[1]
def index_to_state(self, index):
        return (index // self.grid_size[1], index % self.grid_size[1])
```

The method `state_to_index(state)` is used to convert a state (a tuple representing the position in the grid) to its corresponding index in the Q-values array. Let's break down the expression `state[0] * self.grid_size[1] + state[1]`:

*   `state[0]`: Represents the row index of the state in the grid.
*   `self.grid_size[1]`: Represents the number of columns in the grid. Since `grid_size` is a tuple `(rows, columns)`, `self.grid_size[1]` gives the number of columns.
*   `state[1]`: Represents the column index of the state in the grid.

When you multiply `state[0]` by `self.grid_size[1]`, you are essentially converting the row index to a linear index that spans across all rows. For example, if the grid size is `(6, 6)` and you're at position `(2, 3)`, the calculation would be *2 \* 6 + 3 = 15*. This converts the row index 2 to its linear equivalent in a flattened representation of the grid.Then, you add `state[1]`, the column index, to this linear index. This effectively maps the 2D coordinates of the state to a single index in a 1D array, which is suitable for accessing elements in the Q-values array.This approach ensures that each state is uniquely mapped to an index in the Q-values array, allowing for efficient storage and retrieval of Q-values during the Q-learning process. The method `index_to_state(self, index)` in the `GridWorld` class is responsible for converting an index in the Q-values array back to its corresponding 2D state coordinates `(i, j)` in the grid.The expression `index // self.grid_size[1]` calculates the row index of the state in the grid.

*   `index // self.grid_size[1]` performs integer division to find how many full rows are present before the index. This gives us the row index of the state.

The expression `index % self.grid_size[1]` calculates the column index of the state in the grid.

*   `index % self.grid_size[1]` calculates the remainder when dividing the index by the number of columns (`self.grid_size[1]`). This gives us the column index of the state.

The method returns a **tuple (row\_index, column\_index)**, representing the 2D coordinates of the state in the grid. For example,

*   Let's say we have an index **index = 20**, and the grid size is **(6, 6)**.
*   **row\_index = 20 // 6 = 3** (integer division)
*   **column\_index = 20 % 6 = 2** (remainder)

So, the corresponding state in the grid would be **(3, 2)**.This method is useful when you have an index in the Q-values array and need to retrieve the corresponding state coordinates in the grid. It allows you to map linear indices back to their original 2D positions, facilitating interpretation and visualization of Q-values and policies.

1.  Check validity of states: We are considering obstacles in the grid world, the states in which agent can exist are firstly within the boundaries of the grid world, but also not coinciding with the obstacles. To ensure that the states of agent are valid, we add a method called `is_valid state` that Checks if a state is within the grid boundaries and not an obstacle.

```
    def is_valid_state(self, state):
        return 0 <= state[0] < self.grid_size[0] and 0 <= state[1] < self.grid_size[1] \
               and state not in self.obstacle_positions
```

1.  Advancing state to next state: For advancing to next state, we add the method `get_next_state()` method: Given a state and an action, calculate the next state. If the action leads to an invalid state, it stays in the current state. 
    *   The expression `(state[0] + action[0], state[1] + action[1])` calculates the next state by adding the changes specified by the action to the current state.
    *   `state[0] + action[0]` calculates the new row index by adding the change in the row direction specified by `action[0].`
    *   `state[1] + action[1]` calculates the new column index by adding the change in the column direction specified by `action[1]`.
    *   The method then checks if the next state is a valid state using the `is_valid_state` method. If the next state is valid (within the grid boundaries and not an obstacle), it returns the next state. Otherwise, it returns the current state, indicating that the agent cannot move to the next state and should stay in its current state.

```
def get_next_state(self, state, action):
        next_state = (state[0] + action[0], state[1] + action[1])
        if self.is_valid_state(next_state):
            return next_state
        return state  
```

The method returns the next state if it is valid, otherwise, it returns the current state. This method is used within the Q-learning algorithm to determine the next state based on the current state and the action chosen by the agent. It ensures that the agent's movements adhere to the grid's boundaries and avoid obstacles.

### Q-learning

The above methods help with defining the grid world environment, states, actions, and how states can be updated. This covers the environment part, and medium for agent to interact with the environment. For the agent to use the states and come up with a policy to take actions, the q-learning algorithm is implemented in below method.Firstly, the algorithm iterates through several episodes to explore various policies, and a loop is started. It first assigns the state to starting position, initializes rewards, steps, and starts episode loop. It checks that the state is not goal state, each episode iterates through steps the agent takes to change the state. The agent takes one step of action changing the state, per iteration. To learn the environment, here the algorithm is using exploration-exploitation by following epsilon-greedy policy. That means, with a small epsilon probability the agent takes random action, and for rest of the time takes action to maximize the reward, the Q values. Every step taken is given a -1 reward. If next step taken is goal state a high reward of 10 is assigned. If `next_step` is overlapping with obstacle position, then -5 reward is assigned to discourage such a move. Then, a target is calculated as *Target = r +gamma \*Qmax*, which represents the sum of the immediate reward and the discounted maximum Q-value of the next state. This target value is used to update the Q-value of the current state-action pair. The Q-value represents the expected cumulative future reward of taking action a in state s, *Q\_values = Q\_t +learning\_rate\*(target – Q\_t)*. Total reward, `next_state`, , and steps are updated every iteration, as shown in the q-learning function.`Q_learning` method updates Q-values based on the rewards received. It iterates over a specified number of episodes, each consisting of state transitions until reaching the goal state. It uses epsilon-greedy exploration for action selection here, so sometimes it will take sub-optimal actions, but Q values are stored and represent long term prospects. Below code shows steps involved in implementing Q-learning using python.

*   **Function Definition and Initialization**

This snippet defines the `q_learning` function and initializes the necessary variables: rewards to store the total rewards per episode, `steps_per_episode` to store the number of steps per episode, and `policy_progress` to track the policy at specified checkpoints. It also sets default values for the parameters if they are not provided.

```
def q_learning(self, num_episodes=1000, learning_rate=0.1, discount_factor=0.9, epsilon=0.1, policy_checkpoints=None):
    rewards = []
    steps_per_episode = []
    policy_progress = []  # Store optimal policy at different checkpoints
    if policy_checkpoints is None:
        policy_checkpoints = [num_episodes // 1000, num_episodes // 100, num_episodes // 50, num_episodes - 1]
```

*   **Episode Loop:** This snippet starts the main loop for the episodes. For each episode, it initializes the state to the start position, sets the total reward and steps to zero, and creates a list to track the visited states.

```
    for episode in range(1, num_episodes+1):
        state = self.start_position
        total_reward = 0
        steps = 0
        visited_states = [state]
```

*   **Action Selection:** This code snippet implements the action selection mechanism using an epsilon-greedy strategy. With probability epsilon, a random action is chosen (exploration), and with probability (1 - epsilon), the best-known action is selected (exploitation).

```
        while state != self.goal_position:
            if np.random.rand() < epsilon:
                action = np.random.choice(self.num_actions)
            else:
                action = np.argmax(self.Q_values[self.state_to_index(state)])
```

*   **State Transition and Reward Assignment:** This snippet computes the next state based on the current state and chosen action. It assigns a reward based on the outcome: a positive reward of `10` for reaching the goal, a negative reward of `-5` for hitting an obstacle, and a default penalty of `-1` for each step taken.

```
            next_state = self.get_next_state(state, self.actions[action])
            reward = -1
            if next_state == self.goal_position:
                reward = 10
            elif next_state in self.obstacle_positions:
                reward = -5
```

*   **Update Q-values:** Here we update the Q-value for the current state-action pair using the Q-learning update rule. The target value is the reward plus the discounted maximum future reward, and the Q-value is updated to reduce the difference between the target and the current estimate.

```
            target = reward + discount_factor * np.max(self.Q_values[self.state_to_index(next_state)])
            self.Q_values[self.state_to_index(state), action] += learning_rate * (target - self.Q_values[self.state_to_index(state), action])
```

1.  **State transition, update and tracking:** This snippet updates the total reward and steps for the current episode, transitions to the next state, and tracks the visited states.

```
            total_reward += reward
            state = next_state
            steps += 1
            visited_states.append(state)
```

1.  **Tracking Reward and Steps:** The below snippet adds total reward and the number of steps for the current episode to their respective lists for later analysis.

```
            rewards.append(total_reward)
            steps_per_episode.append(steps)
```

1.  **Policy Checkpointing for Evaluation:** This snippet stores the current policy at specified checkpoints. It uses the `get_optimal_policy` method for evaluation. It also prints progress information every 20 episodes for monitoring purposes.

```
            if episode in policy_checkpoints:
                policy_progress.append((self.get_optimal_policy(), visited_states, episode))
            if episode % 20 == 0:
                print(f"Episode {episode}/{num_episodes}, Total Reward: {total_reward}, Steps: {steps}")
```

1.  **Return Output**: Once all episodes are completed, the below line returns the collected rewards, steps per episode, and policy progress.

```
        return rewards, steps_per_episode, policy_progress
```

1.  **Keeping Track of Optimal Policy**: In order to help logging and checkpointing policy, `a get_optimal_policy` method is added. Here, every grid cell is assigned a argmax of Q-value, as optimal policy, with invalid actions such as those when state is in goal position or obstacle position as `-1`. This function efficiently calculates the optimal policy for a grid-based environment considering the constraints of goal and obstacle states. The snippet below defines the `get_optimal_policy` function and initializes a matrix `optimal_policy` with the same dimensions as the grid. The matrix is filled with zeros and will store the optimal actions for each state.

```
def get_optimal_policy(self):
        optimal_policy = np.zeros(self.grid_size, dtype=int)
```

Then, the function iterates through each cell in the grid, treating each cell as a state. The nested loops ensure that every state in the grid is processed.

```
        for i in range(self.grid_size[0]):
            for j in range(self.grid_size[1]):
                state = (i, j)
```

It then checks if the current state is the goal state or an obstacle state. If so, it sets the corresponding entry in `optimal_policy` to -1, indicating that these states have no applicable actions.

```
                if state == self.goal_position:
                    optimal_policy[i, j] = -1  
                elif state in self.obstacle_positions:
                    optimal_policy[i, j] = -1  
```

Once check for goal or obstacle state is cleared, the function collects valid actions by storing them in `valid_actions` list. It iterates through all possible actions, calculates the resulting next state, and checks if the next state is valid.

```
                else:
                    valid_actions = []
                    for action in range(self.num_actions):
                        next_state = self.get_next_state(state, self.actions[action])
                        if self.is_valid_state(next_state):
                            valid_actions.append(action)
```

To find the optimal policy, it uses the Q-values to find the action with the highest value. If there are no valid actions, it sets the entry to -1. It then returns the optimal policy.

```
                    if valid_actions:
                        optimal_policy[i, j] = np.argmax([self.Q_values[self.state_to_index(state), a] for a in valid_actions])
                    else:
                        optimal_policy[i, j] = -1  # No valid action
        return optimal_policy
```

### Visualization and Tracking Progress

In order to track and visualize learning progress, three plotting methods are also added that show reward per episode, and number of steps taken by agent to reach goals state per episode and the policy progress.

1.  **Rewards vs Episode:** The below code plots rewards vs episode showing the performance of Q-learning algorithm.

```
def plot_rewards(self, rewards):
    plt.plot(rewards)
    plt.xlabel('Episode')
    plt.ylabel('Total Reward')
    plt.title('Q-learning Performance')
    plt.show()
```

The plot below in *Figure 1.2* shows Q-learning performance over all training episodes executed. Here x-axis is episodes, and y- axis is total episode reward. Initial sharp rise shows that policy converges in about 50 episodes.

![Figure 1.2 Total rewarfd over 1000 episodes, showing learning progress and performance](media/file2.png)

Figure 1.2 Total rewarfd over 1000 episodes, showing learning progress and performance

1.  **Number of steps per episode:** Steps needed to reach the goal per episode can be plotted by implementing the code below. This visualization is to track how the number of iterations required to reach the goal reduces as the agent learns.

```
def plot_steps_per_episode(self, steps_per_episode):
    plt.plot(steps_per_episode)
    plt.xlabel('Episode')
    plt.ylabel('Steps')
    plt.title('Steps per Episode')
    plt.show()
    plt.tight_layout()
```

Visualization of the number of steps required to reach the goal for every episode,is as shown in *Figure 1.3* below. Initially the agent takes more steps up to 200 steps to reach the goal, but after the policy converges, number of steps required is also converged to <10 steps.

![Figure 1.3 Number of steps taken by the agent in the grid world environment to reach goal vs episode.](media/file3.png)

Figure 1.3 Number of steps taken by the agent in the grid world environment to reach goal vs episode.

1.  **Visualize Policy Progress:** To visualize clearly the trajectory of the agent as it takes steps towards the goal, until it reaches the goal, we implement the **plot\_policy\_progress** method. Here, the grid world is plotted with the entire trajectory traced by the agent. Such a trajectory can be visualized for every episode. But, only a few snapshots of episodes are enough to visualize progress. The below definition of the function and input, sets up the figure with subplots, one for each checkpoint in the `policy_progress `list, by assigning `num_checkpoints`.

```
def plot_policy_progress(self, policy_progress):
    num_checkpoints = len(policy_progress)
    fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, num_checkpoints, figsize=(6*num_checkpoints, 6))
```

*   **Loop Through Checkpoints:** Then, it starts a loop to process each checkpoint in `policy_progress`. It sets the title of each subplot to indicate the episode number.

```
    for i, (policy, visited_states, episode) in enumerate(policy_progress):
        ax = axes[i]
        ax.set_title(f'Policy at Episode {episode}')
```

*   **Visual Representation of States:** The function then iterates through each state in the policy grid. It sets the visual representation for the goal position, obstacles, start position, and other states, marking them with appropriate symbols and colors or styles.

```
        for y in range(policy.shape[0]):
            for x in range(policy.shape[1]):
                state = (y, x)
                if state == self.goal_position:
                    ax.text(x, self.grid_size[0] - y - 1, 'G', va='center', ha='center', fontsize=20)
                    ax.add_patch(plt.Rectangle((x - 0.5, self.grid_size[0] - y - 1 - 0.5), 1, 1, color='lightgreen'))
                elif state in self.obstacle_positions:
                    ax.text(x, self.grid_size[0] - y - 1, 'X', va='center', ha='center', fontsize=20)
                    ax.add_patch(plt.Rectangle((x - 0.5, self.grid_size[0] - y - 1 - 0.5), 1, 1, color='lightgray'))
                elif state == self.start_position:
                    ax.text(x, self.grid_size[0] - y - 1, 'S', va='center', ha='center', fontsize=20, color='red')
                    ax.add_patch(plt.Rectangle((x - 0.5, self.grid_size[0] - y - 1 - 0.5), 1, 1, color='lightblue'))
                else:
                    action = policy[y, x]
                    ax.text(x, self.grid_size[0] - y - 1, self.action_symbols[action], va='center', ha='center', fontsize=20)
```

*   **Plotting the Agent Path and Configuring the Grid:** The function then plots the path taken by the agent for each checkpoint, using lines to connect consecutive states. It also configures the grid lines and labels for the subplot to make the plot more readable.

```
        for j in range(len(visited_states) - 1):
            x1, y1 = visited_states[j][1], self.grid_size[0] - visited_states[j][0] - 1
            x2, y2 = visited_states[j + 1][1], self.grid_size[0] - visited_states[j + 1][0] - 1
            ax.plot([x1, x2], [y1, y2], color='blue', linewidth=3)
            ax.set_xticks(np.arange(-0.5, policy.shape[1], 1))
            ax.set_yticks(np.arange(-0.5, policy.shape[0], 1))
            ax.grid(color='black', linestyle='-', linewidth=1)
            ax.set_xticklabels([])
            ax.set_yticklabels([])
            ax.set_xlabel('X')
            ax.set_ylabel('Y')
```

*Figure 1.4* shows policy progress, trajectories taken by agent at progressive episodes, after executing 1000 episodes of training, snapshots of episodes 1, 10, 20 and 999 are recorded. The agent has to wander and retrace some paths, taking a longer trajectory to reach the goal in episode 1. By the 10th episode, there is some progress, and by 20^(th) episode there is marked improvement in the trajectory taken by the agent. On 999^(th) episode the agent is already taking optimal path. As an exercise, modify the number of episodes in q-learning to 200 or 500 episodes only, and change the number of checkpoints.

![](media/file4.png)![](media/file5.png)![](media/file6.png)![Figure 1.4. Progression of the policy for four representative episodes during training.](media/file7.png)

Figure 1.4. Progression of the policy for four representative episodes during training.

Execution code to instantiate the `GridWorld` class and run q-learning training can be as below.

```
# Adjust hyperparameters for experimentation
obstacle_positions = [(2, 3), (3, 1), (4, 3)]
grid_world = GridWorld(grid_size=(6, 6), obstacle_positions=obstacle_positions)
rewards, steps_per_episode, policy_progress = grid_world.q_learning(num_episodes=1000, learning_rate=0.1, discount_factor=0.95, epsilon=0.1)
```

In the above q-learning implementation, the agent was able to learn how to reach the goal in a finite number of episodes. Visualization methods shown help to track learning progress. We use three obstacles and a grid size of 6 x 6, in this example. The training episodes required for convergence are ~50 for this problem, but this number can increase for a larger grid size or number of obstacles. Note that the `GridWorld` class allows parametrizing grid size and obstacle positions, making it convenient to modify the environment. We will use this parametrization in the following sections to explore strategies to improve learning progress.

## Designing Reward Functions and Transfer Learning

For the agent to learn desirable policy, in the Q-learning algorithm discussed in the preceding section, positive rewards are assigned to agents for desirable behavior, and negative rewards are assigned for undesirable behavior. In case of the gridworld problem, for every step taken by the agent, a negative reward `-1` assigned to the agent discourages it from taking more steps. Similarly, a large negative reward of `-5` assigned for every step agent takes that overlaps with the obstacles. A large negative reward helps with learning to avoid the obstacles. Finally, a large positive reward of `10` for reaching the goal encourages reaching the goal state. The reward assignment is itself the reward function, in this case, as restated below:

```
reward = -1
if next_state == self.goal_position:
    reward = 10
elif next_state in self.obstacle_positions:
    reward = -5
```

Notice that the reward function is discrete and works well for the grid world problem. There are cases where the reward function may need to be continuous. For example, if the grid has many cells e.g. *1000 x 1000*, with a few obstacles, a continuous reward with gradual increment as the agent gets closer to the goal will help in providing a meaningful result. If all rewards are `-1` except at the goal, the agent will wander longer. In such a case, we can redesign the reward assignment. One example is given below:

```
reward = -1 + np.exp(-0.1 * np.linalg.norm(np.array(state) - np.array(self.goal_position)))
if next_state in self.obstacle_positions:
    reward = reward – 5
```

`reward = -1 + np.exp(-0.1 * np.linalg.norm(np.array(state) - np.array(self.goal_position)))`, this line calculates the reward for the current state. It starts with a base reward of `-1`. Then, it adjusts this base reward based on the distance between the current state and the goal position. The distance between the current state and the goal position is calculated using the Euclidean distance formula implementation in numpy: `(np.linalg.norm(np.array(state) - np.array(self.goal_position)))`. This distance is then scaled by a factor of `-0.1` and passed through an exponential function `np.exp`. Scaling by a negative value ensures that the reward decreases as the distance increases, and the exponential function ensures that the decrease is gradual rather than linear.Then, if the next state (the state the agent will transition to after taking an action) is in the list of obstacle positions (`self.obstacle_positions`), the reward is further decreased by 5. This penalty encourages the agent to avoid obstacles, as transitioning into an obstacle state results in a larger negative reward.

### Transfer Learning

**Transfer learning** (**TL**) in context of RL involves leveraging knowledge gained from solving one task to improve learning or performance on a related but different task. To demonstrate TL for the grid world problem, we modify the code in `q_learning` function we discussed in the previous section. We add a few methods to the class as described below:The `q_learning_transfer` method is intended for TL. It takes pre-learned Q-values from a smaller grid world (e.g., **6x6**) and transfers them to a larger grid world (e.g., **8x8**). The transferred Q-values are then fine-tuned in the new environment using Q-learning.

```
def q_learning_transfer(self, pre_learned_Q_values,epsilon=0.01):
        # Initialize Q-values with pre-learned values
        self.Q_values[:pre_learned_Q_values.shape[0], :pre_learned_Q_values.shape[1]] = pre_learned_Q_values
        print(f"Q learning with Transferred Q-values")
        # Perform Q-learning in the new environment
        rewards_transfer, steps_per_episode, policy_progress = self.q_learning(epsilon=0.01)
        return rewards_transfer, steps_per_episode, policy_progress
```

The `q_learning_scratch` method performs Q-learning from scratch in the new environment, by first initializing Q-values.

```
    def q_learning_scratch(self):
        self.Q_values = np.zeros((self.num_states, self.num_actions))  # Or initialize with other initial values if needed
        print(f"Q learning from Scratch")
        rewards_scratch, steps_per_episode, policy_progress = self.q_learning()
        return rewards_scratch, steps_per_episode, policy_progress
```

Finally, after training both transfer learning and from-scratch models, the two trainings can be compared. The below method `plot_rewards_comparison` compares their performance, total reward as metric is computed and then smoothed using moving average before plotting them. We show snippets that are part of this method below.The below code defines the function and calculates the convergence speed for both the transfer learning and training from scratch scenarios. It finds the first episode where the reward reaches 90% of the maximum reward achieved in the respective scenario.

```
def plot_rewards_comparison(self, rewards_transfer, rewards_scratch):
        convergence_speed_transfer = next((i for i, reward in enumerate(rewards_transfer) if reward >= 0.9 * max(rewards_transfer)), len(rewards_transfer))
        convergence_speed_scratch = next((i for i, reward in enumerate(rewards_scratch) if reward >= 0.9 * max(rewards_scratch)), len(rewards_scratch))
```

The code follows by summing up the rewards for each episode to calculate the total reward for both transfer learning and training from scratch.

```
        total_reward_transfer = sum(rewards_transfer)
        total_reward_scratch = sum(rewards_scratch)
```

For better visualization, a moving average is used to smooth the reward curves. The window size for the moving average is set to 10.

```
        window_size = 10
        rewards_transfer_smoothed = np.convolve(rewards_transfer, np.ones(window_size)/window_size, mode='valid')
        rewards_scratch_smoothed = np.convolve(rewards_scratch, np.ones(window_size)/window_size, mode='valid')
```

Below code plots the smoothed reward curves for both scenarios, using different colors and styles for clarity, with appropriate labeling. You can change the colors and style for your liking.

```
        plt.plot(np.arange(window_size//2, len(rewards_transfer_smoothed) + window_size//2), rewards_transfer_smoothed, label='Transfer Learning', linestyle='--', color='blue', alpha=0.7)
        plt.plot(np.arange(window_size//2, len(rewards_scratch_smoothed) + window_size//2), rewards_scratch_smoothed, label='Training from Scratch', linestyle='--', color='orange', alpha=0.7)
```

We then highlight specific epochs related to transfer learning on the plot using vertical lines.

```
        transfer_epochs = [episode for _, _, episode in policy_progress]
        for epoch in transfer_epochs:
            plt.axvline(x=epoch, color='gray', linestyle='--', linewidth=0.8)
```

To make the plot more informative, we add text annotations to the plot to show the convergence speeds and total rewards for both scenarios.

```
        plt.text(0.5, 0.6, f"Convergence Speed (Transfer): {convergence_speed_transfer}", transform=plt.gca().transAxes)
        plt.text(0.5, 0.55, f"Total Reward (Transfer): {total_reward_transfer}", transform=plt.gca().transAxes)
        plt.text(0.5, 0.5, f"Convergence Speed (Scratch): {convergence_speed_scratch}", transform=plt.gca().transAxes)
        plt.text(0.5, 0.45, f"Total Reward (Scratch): {total_reward_scratch}", transform=plt.gca().transAxes)
```

This final snippet of the function `plot_rewards_comparison` sets the labels, title, and legend for the plot, then displays it.

```
        plt.xlabel('Episode')
        plt.ylabel('Total Reward')
        plt.title('Q-learning Performance Comparison')
        plt.legend()
        plt.show()
```

The method `plot_comparison_metrics` below compares the reward metrics for Q-learning with TL, where pretrained Q-values are transferred, with reward metrics for Q-learning from scratch.

```
def plot_comparison_metrics(self, rewards_transfer, rewards_scratch, steps_transfer, steps_scratch):
        fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(12, 6))
        ax1 = axes[0]
        ax1.plot(rewards_transfer, label='Transfer Learning')
        ax1.plot(rewards_scratch, label='Training from Scratch')
        ax1.set_xlabel('Episode')
        ax1.set_ylabel('Total Reward')
        ax1.set_title('Total Reward Comparison')
        ax1.legend()
        ax2 = axes[1]
        ax2.plot(steps_transfer, label='Transfer Learning')
        ax2.plot(steps_scratch, label='Training from Scratch')
        ax2.set_xlabel('Episode')
        ax2.set_ylabel('Steps per Episode')
        ax2.set_title('Steps per Episode Comparison')
        ax2.legend()
        plt.tight_layout()
        plt.show()
```

Example execution showing policy training and TL steps are shown below. To see how the TL works, we first instantiate the modified `GridWorld` class and create a **6 x 6** grid world with three obstacles to obtain a trained policy. Run the `q_learning()` method to come up with optimal policy and Q-values for the **6 x6** environment. We are going to use these Q-values for TL. These are lines of code below:

```
obstacle_positions = [(2, 3), (3, 1), (4, 3)]
grid_world = GridWorld(grid_size=(6, 6), obstacle_positions=obstacle_positions)
rewards, steps_per_episode_6x6, policy_progress_6x6 = grid_world.q_learning()
grid_world.plot_policy_progress(policy_progress_6x6)
```

The policy progress for training using Q-learning is obtained above as `policy_progress_6x6` can be visualized as in *Figure 1.5*, where the trajectories of agent at three representative episodes, **6, 20** and **100** is plotted. For episode 6 and 20, the agent is still learning the policy, but at episode 100 the agent has already learned the optimal path of reaching the goal.

Figure 1.5 Progress in learning shown at various episodes for the 6 x 6 grid with trajectories taken by the agent to reach the goal.

For  TL, the Q-values from this **6x6** environment are transferred to a more complex but similar environment with **8 x 8** cells and **5** obstacles (**instead of 3**). Here we compare training the agent from scratch and training with transferred Q-values, by comparing their policy progress. Note that we don’t compare the policy progress for TL with Q-learning from scratch for the **6x6** environment, but train again the **8 x8** environment with **5** obstacles using method `q_learning_scratch`. Execution code is as below:

```
obstacle_positions_5 = [(2, 3), (3, 1), (4, 3), (5, 2), (2, 5)]
new_grid_world = GridWorld(grid_size=(8,8), obstacle_positions=obstacle_positions_5)
rewards_transfer, steps_per_episode_transfer, policy_progress_transfer = new_grid_world.q_learning_transfer(grid_world.Q_values)
new_grid_world.plot_policy_progress(policy_progress_transfer)
rewards_scratch, steps_per_episode, policy_progress = new_grid_world.q_learning_scratch()
new_grid_world.plot_policy_progress(policy_progress)
```

The policy progress for the agent training based on TL is plotted using the `plot_policy_progress` method. Below, in *Figure 1.6*, you can see the progress in learning shown at various episodes for the 8 x 8 grid with trajectories taken by the agent to reach the goal, when Q-values are transferred from training on 6 x6 grid with 3 obstacles. For episode 6, the agent is still learning the policy, but at episode 20 the agent has already learned the optimal path of reaching the goal, at episode 100, it just explored another optimal path.

Similarly, policy progress for agent training on **8x8** grid environment with **5** obstacles from scratch is plotted in *Figure 1.7*. Compare the episodes 6, 20 and 100 for both the cases. For episode 6, the agent is still learning the policy, at episode 20, it has improved, but it is at episode 100 that the agent has already learned the optimal path of reaching the goal.

To further compare transfer-learning based training progress with training from scratch, we execute the comparison metrics functions as below.

```
grid_world.plot_rewards_comparison(rewards_transfer, rewards_scratch)
grid_world.plot_comparison_metrics(rewards_transfer, rewards_scratch,  steps_per_episode_transfer,steps_per_episode)
```

In *Figure 1.8*, comparison of Q-learning performance from plotting total reward (averaged/smoothed over a window of 10 episodes) vs episodes for agent trained with transferred Q-values vs agent trained from scratch for the 8x8 grid, shows transfer learning accelerates training convergence. After looking at promising improvements using transfer learning, we can be optimistic of using human feedback also, which we will realize in the next section.

## Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback

In the preceding section, we saw that training an agent by transferring Q-values pretrained on an environment (**6 x 6** grid with **3** obstacles) similar to target environment (**8 x 8** grid with **5** obstacles), can be effective in speeding up the learning progress. There is an important caveat to this. The environment should have reasonable similarity for benefiting from the policies learned. If the environment is significantly different, or worse—conflicting in goals (or reward incentives to reach goals), transfer learning may prove to be ineffective. I suggest you experiment with obstacles and goals that are very different from those used in getting the Q-values of the policy to be transferred. How do we accelerate training in such scenarios? One approach is to incorporate human feedback into the training of policy. Human feedback can take various forms, such as explicit rewards, evaluations, or demonstrations of desired behavior.

*   **Explicit Rewards:** The human feedback provides explicit rewards or penalties based on the agent's actions. For example, in the `gridworld` scenario, the human might assign positive rewards for moving towards a goal and negative rewards for hitting obstacles.
*   **Evaluations:** The human evaluates the agent's actions or policies and provides feedback based on their performance. For instance, they might provide feedback like "That was a good move" or "You should have gone left instead of right.", objective thumbs up or down, “yes” or “no”.
*   **Demonstrations:** The human demonstrates the desired behavior by showing the agent how to solve tasks in the environment. This can be in the form of trajectories or sequences of actions leading to successful outcomes.

The agent uses the human feedback to improve its decision-making process. This can involve various learning algorithms tailored to incorporate human feedback effectively. In case of grid world example, if the agent makes a move in the grid world, the human might provide positive feedback (reward) if the move is good or negative feedback (penalty) if the move is bad. Instead of exploring the environment to learn this policy, the agent uses the human-provided feedback to update its policy. Positive feedback encourages the agent to repeat similar actions in similar situations, while negative feedback guides the agent to avoid those actions. Over time, through iterative learning from human feedback, the agent refines its policy to make better decisions in the grid world.To realize how human feedback accelerates learning, we continue using the grid world problem. We add a new method called `q_learning_with_human_feedback `to the `GridWorld` class, which is a modification of the `q_learning` method. The additional input `human_feedback `provides a dictionary of rewards assigned by a human for specific scenario change from a current state to next state. For example, if the agent is in current state **(2,2)** and next state is **(2,3)**, which is closer to goal **(5,5)**, human would give a reward of **+1**. On the contrary, if current state of the agent is **(3,4)** and agent moves to next state of **(2,4)**, which is farther from the goal **(5,5)**, human would give a reward of **\-1**. This human feedback based reward is added to the reward assigned in q learning algorithm. Below is the method:We first define the function and initialize the necessary variables. Note that it is similar to `q_learning` method, but has additional input `human_feedback` as shown in snippet below.

```
    def q_learning_with_human_feedback(self, num_episodes=1000, learning_rate=0.1, discount_factor=0.9, epsilon=0.1, policy_checkpoints=None, human_feedback=None):
```

Since this method is a modification of previous method with significant overlap, please refer to the *Q\_learning* section and snippets discussed earlier. The specific snippets and their explanations that follow as before are: 1) **Function Definition and Initialization** (modified here), followed by 2) **Episode Loop**, 3) **Action Selection**, and 4) **State Transition and Reward Assignment**.**Incorporating Human Feedback:** After reward assignment as in snippets discussed previously, in 4) **State Transition and Reward Assignment**, it is at a point to update the reward by incorporating human feedback. First, the code checks if human feedback is provided. If feedback for the transition from the current state to the next state is available, it updates the reward accordingly.

```
                if human_feedback is not None:
                    if state in human_feedback and next_state in human_feedback[state]:
                        reward += human_feedback[state][next_state]
```

Here, we simply add the human feedback to the reward, but there can be numerous ways to incorporate human feedback, which we will see in the next chapter. After updating the reward, we continue rest of the code similar to q\_learning, as before, until the end of the method where we return output.To allow comparison between Q-learning and Q-learning with human feedback, the previous plotting methods, `plot_reward_comparison()` and `plot_comparison_metrics()` used in the TL section are reused and added to the `GridWorld` class . The labels and legends are updated accordingly. Execution code to instantiate and run the modified class and compare human feedback based training and training from scratch with just q learning is as below. In this example, we are collecting human feedback in a dictionary, where the keys are state tuples, and the values are dictionaries mapping next states to feedback values.

```
obstacle_positions = [(2, 3), (3, 1), (4, 3)]
grid_world = GridWorld(grid_size=(6, 6), obstacle_positions=obstacle_positions)
rewards, steps_per_episode, policy_progress = grid_world.q_learning(num_episodes=200, learning_rate=0.1, discount_factor=0.95, epsilon=0.1)
grid_world.plot_policy_progress(policy_progress)
human_feedback = {
    (2, 2): {(2, 3): 1},  # Positive feedback for transitioning from (2, 2) to (2, 3)
    (2, 3): {(2, 4): 1},  # Positive feedback for transitioning from (2, 3) to (2, 4)
    (2, 4): {(2, 5): 1},  # Positive feedback for transitioning from (2, 4) to (2, 5)
    (3, 4): {(2, 4): -1},  # Negative feedback for transitioning from (3, 4) to (2, 4)
    (4, 4): {(3, 4): -1},  # Negative feedback for transitioning from (4, 4) to (3, 4)
    (4, 5): {(5, 5): 1},  # Positive feedback for transitioning from (4, 5) to (5, 5)
}
rewards_hf, steps_per_episode_hf, policy_progress_hf = grid_world.q_learning_with_human_feedback(num_episodes=200, learning_rate=0.1, discount_factor=0.95, epsilon=0.1, human_feedback=human_feedback)
grid_world.plot_policy_progress(policy_progress)
grid_world.plot_rewards_comparison(rewards_hf, rewards)
grid_world.plot_comparison_metrics(rewards_hf, rewards,  steps_per_episode_hf,steps_per_episode)
```

The following figure, *Figure 1.10*, shows agent trajectory during specific episodes when the agent is navigating a 6x6 grid. The policy is progressing in learning, but on 2 and 20th episode the agent is still learning the policy. On 100th episode we see optimal path followed by the agent, indicating learning.

![Figure 1.10 Agent trajectories shown at various episodes for the 6 x 6 grid for q-learning.](media/file13.png)

Figure 1.10 Agent trajectories shown at various episodes for the 6 x 6 grid for q-learning.

Comparing Q-learning with Q- learning + human feedback using policy progress on grid, Figure 1.10 and Figure 1.11, shows that with human feedback, optimal policy is already taken by the agent.

![Figure 1.11 Agent trajectories for q learning with human feedback shown at various episodes for the 6 x 6 grid.](media/file14.png)

Figure 1.11 Agent trajectories for q learning with human feedback shown at various episodes for the 6 x 6 grid.

To see this more clearly, *Figure 1.12* shows comparison of total reward performance over episodes. While Q-learning only takes reasonable amount of episodes to reach achievable reward, and convergence (orange plot), upon addition of **Human Feedback**, the total reward is already converged in just *2nd* episode. Note that in *Figure 1.12*, the total reward is smoothed (filtered) to average over every 10 iterations.The following figure compares total reward per episodes smoothed over a window-size of 10 episodes, for agent training using human feedback (in darker line) with Q-learning from scratch, the plots show faster convergence in case of human feedback with q learning based training, 2 episodes vs 18 episodes. Total training reward also is better 1022 (with human feedback)> -549 (without human feedback). Note that starting policy at episode **0** is not plotted . The reward for the case with human feedback is the sum of reward for q learning and reward assigned by human (+1 or -1), and agent is still following exploration vs exploitation strategy (epsilon greedy approach) here.

![Figure 1.12 Total reward per episodes averaged, for agent training using human feedback (in darker line) with Q-learning from scratch.](media/file15.png)

Figure 1.12 Total reward per episodes averaged, for agent training using human feedback (in darker line) with Q-learning from scratch.

In the left plot of the following figure, *Figure 1.13* we are comparing raw total reward per episode, while on the right we see steps taken by agent to reach the goal for every episode.

![Figure 1.13 Human Feedback + Q learning (blue/darker line flat) vs Q learning only shown by raw total reward per episode (left) and steps taken by agent to reach the goal for every episode (right).](media/file16.png)

Figure 1.13 Human Feedback + Q learning (blue/darker line flat) vs Q learning only shown by raw total reward per episode (left) and steps taken by agent to reach the goal for every episode (right).

Human Feedback + Q learning (blue/darker line flat) in this case show improvement in learning to take less steps to reach the goal state almost instantly. The few peaks and valleys in the blue flatter plots are indicating some unlearning or exploration-exploitation strategy at play, but on average the learning with human feedback has higher reward and lower number of steps to reach the goal.

## Summary

In this chapter, we introduced you to main concepts of RL, provided a simple grid world example to help understand key concepts using one of the fundamental algorithms, Q learning, then demonstrated how transfer learning and human feedback can help accelerate learning. While this is good for introduction, there are also more complex examples where Q-values may not be stored in tables but a neural network or similar function approximator might be necessary. Q-learning can become unstable, when scaled to continuous state-action space, and other policy learning algorithms will be necessary. We will cover some of these cases in following chapters. Note also that while we managed to get learning accelerated by using explicit reward feedback from human, on a large scale this can be expensive, and adequate tools for gathering human feedback might be necessary. More on that in the following chapters, but before we discuss that, we will begin by understanding the role of human feedback in RL from a general perspective, in the next chapter.
