# Chapter 2: Role of Human Feedback in Reinforcement Learning

## Join our book community on Discord

![A qr code with a square in the middle Description automatically generated](media/file0.png)

[https://packt.link/EarlyAccessCommunity](https://packt.link/EarlyAccessCommunity)

In this chapter, our focus shifts towards the role of human feedback in reinforcement learning, taking a broader perspective on it. In the previous chapter, we illustrated how **RL with human feedback** (**RLHF**) can significantly improve and expedite policy training compared to traditional reinforcement learning methods such as Q-learning. Incorporation of human feedback in reinforcement learning has evolved across various applications in both practical settings and research literature, experiencing a surge in popularity in recent years, especially in large language models. While our current focus remains broad, we won't delve into specific applications of human feedback in large language modeling just yet. Instead, our aim is to explore its overarching significance and implications within the broader context of reinforcement learning. To accommodate readers interested in diving deeper, some relevant references have also been provided.In conventional RL methodology, as discussed in the previous chapter, a state-action-reward framework is utilized, necessitating the design of a reward function to define specific objectives. This function incentivizes the agent to maximize these objectives through its behavior. While it is feasible to design such functions for specific tasks, this capability is limited to well-defined tasks. As the scope of requirements and objectives expands, accurately defining the reward function becomes increasingly challenging. Inaccuracies within these functions or scenarios marked by distributional shifts can result in undesirable agent behavior, where the agent prioritizes achieving the objective regardless of consequences or takes actions leading to unforeseen outcomes in unseen scenarios. Such side-effects or unforeseen consequences may incur costs, potentially leading to safety hazards. Human intervention and alignment of models play crucial roles in addressing these challenges. Human feedback during training aids in developing policies resilient to distributional shifts in training data and mitigates the consequences of reward function inaccuracies.This chapter covers the following topics:

*   Enhancing Learning Efficiency with Human Feedback in RL
*   Incorporating Human Expertise
*   Types of Human Feedback in RL
*   Incorporating Human Preferences into RL
*   Bias and Other Challenges

## Enhancing Learning Efficiency with Human Feedback in RL

While training a reinforcement learning agent to achieve a particular objective, the agent must undergo a vast number of iterations until it converges to a high reward. This involves going through several trial-and-error state-action sequences or trajectories that would not be optimal. The number of such trajectories can be very high if the agent must navigate through complex environments. Collecting experience of low reward trajectories can turn out to be costly. The challenges encountered by RL agents, such as navigating complex environments and undergoing numerous trial-and-error iterations, underscore the need for more efficient learning strategies , the integration of human feedback offers a promising solution to enhance learning efficiency. For example, in training autonomous vehicles, human feedback can help the agent quickly learn traffic rules, safe driving behaviors, and respond appropriately to various road conditions, instead of going through every condition that requires avoiding these scenarios. Human feedback can also help in overcoming exploration-exploitation trade-offs. By providing hints or nudges towards promising actions or states, humans can guide the agent to explore more efficiently, focusing on regions of the state-action space that are more likely to yield valuable information.Incorporating human feedback allows RL agents to learn from expert demonstrations or preferences, enabling them to make more informed decisions. For example, in healthcare settings, RL algorithms can learn treatment policies by incorporating feedback from medical professionals regarding the effectiveness and safety of different interventions. Thus, rather than spending several training cycles of figuring out valuable trajectories, using human expert guidance can save time by simply specifying human preferences or intention. In scenarios where simulators or models are imperfect representations of the real world, human feedback can help bridge the gap between simulation and reality, leading to faster convergence and improved generalization.Let's revisit the reinforcement learning problem where an agent needs to learn to navigate a grid world to reach a goal state while avoiding obstacles. In the example from previous chapter, the agent receives a reward of +10 upon reaching the goal state, a reward of -5 upon hitting an obstacle, and -1 for every step taken in order to penalize taking too long to reach the goal. The goal is to learn a policy that maximizes cumulative rewards over time. The specific assignment of these rewards in the grid space is reward design, but not a form of human feedback as during training no human input is provided. Without human feedback, the agent might use exploration strategies like epsilon-greedy to randomly explore the grid world, gradually learning which actions lead to higher rewards through trial and error. For smaller state spaces, this may not appear to be a problem, however, this approach can be slow and inefficient, in large state spaces.Now, let's incorporate human feedback into the learning process. The human supervisor can observe the agent's actions and provide feedback in the form of preferences or evaluations of the agent's behavior. In the grid world example from previous chapter, the human evaluates or provides binary feedback indicating whether each action taken by the agent was good (+1) or bad (-1). This is captured for a series of grid cells, based on its current state and resulting next state based on action taken. This type of human feedback is **evaluative feedback**, as the goal is known clearly and objectively, and direct feedback is possible.If we model the RL problem as a Markov decision process, where current state s and action taken, a, lead to next state s’, such that transition probabilities of next state depend only on current state and action taken P(s’|s,a), and reward are assigned for each of these sequences R(s,a,s’). The goal of the agent is to maximize cumulative returns, which is the action value function Q(s,a). If rt is reward at time t, then cumulative reward or total return discounted over future rewards by γ is .The way we incorporate human feedback based reward is by adding human feedback to environmental reward. The augmented reward function can be defined as R\*(s,a,s’) = R(s,a,s’)+ H(s,a,s’). where H(s,a,s’) represents the human feedback for the action a taken in state s leading to next state s’. This feedback can be utilized to guide the agent's learning process by reinforcing actions that lead to positive outcomes and discouraging actions that lead to negative outcomes, ultimately accelerating the learning process and improving the agent's decision-making abilities.

![Figure 2.1 - RLHF schematic showing integration of reward model with the environment-agent system](media/file18.png)

Figure 2.1 - RLHF schematic showing integration of reward model with the environment-agent system

In the last section of the *Chapter 1*, we used human feedback that mapped rewards to current state and next state, so the feedback is specific to the action taken and its consequences in the next state. We then empirically compare training the agent from scratch with the training using human feedback and demonstrate that using human feedback accelerates training significantly. There are some other ways to integrate human feedback depending upon the scenario:

*   **State-Action feedback** H(s,a): this form of feedback provides information about the quality of actions directly in the current state. It doesn't consider the specific outcome or next state resulting from the action. It's useful when the quality of the action can be evaluated without observing its consequences.
*   **State feedback** H(s): Human feedback can also be provided at the state level, indicating the desirability or appropriateness of the current state. This form of feedback can guide exploration by directing the agent towards states that are more likely to lead to desirable outcomes.
*   **Cumulative feedback** H(τ): In some cases, human feedback may be provided over entire trajectories (τ), capturing the overall quality of the agent's behavior over a sequence of actions and states. This type of feedback can be useful for evaluating long-term performance and encouraging consistency or convergence towards desired behaviors. This is especially helpful when environment is complex and we want to limit human feedback to trajectories rather than asking explicit feedback over a large state-action space.
*   **Preference feedback** H(a1>a2): Instead of providing explicit rewards or evaluations, human feedback can be in the form of preferences or comparisons between pairs of actions. This type of feedback is common in preference-based reinforcement learning, where the agent learns from comparisons between expert demonstrations or actions.

Therefore, human feedback approach in implemented and discussed in previous chapter is only one of the many ways in which human feedback can be integrated into reinforcement learning training for accelerating the learning process. We will discuss more on how human expertise can be incorporated next.

## Incorporating Human Expertise

Leveraging domain knowledge in **reinforcement learning** (**RL**) can significantly enhance performance and decision-making by providing a structured framework for understanding the problem space and guiding the learning process. Domain knowledge refers to expertise and insights specific to the problem domain, which can come from various sources such as subject matter experts, prior research, or practical experience. **Problem formulation**: Domain knowledge helps in framing the RL problem by identifying relevant states, actions, and rewards, thus reducing the search space and making the learning process more efficient. For example, in robotics, understanding the dynamics of the environment and the physical constraints of the robot can help design more effective state representations and action spaces. The list below explains how domain knowledge can help define each of the components in RL framework.

*   Defining states: Including the important states as part of the agent observation is critical to the training efficiency of the agent. Too many unnecessary states can add noise and overhead, and unnecessarily higher capacity requirement to a function approximator such as a neural network can slow training, or worse—learn some unnecessary correlations. On the contrary, missing some important states can lead to agent having to learn and model some additional relationships between states that could have been simply specified. In case of a robot (e.g. a robot arm or a mobile robot), not providing velocities, and only providing position information can lead to inefficient learning.
*   Defining actions: Subject matter expertise can help define what actions can be available and how they are constrained. For example, a robot arm may move only in a particular range of angles, and subject matter expert knowledge on constraints on the action defines action space.
*   Defining rewards: In complex industrial scenarios, the region of desirable and undesirable state-action space may not be obvious to lay person, and subject matter experts can help specify these regions, and shape the reward functions more accurately.
*   Episode lengths and termination criteria: Human expertise can also help determine when a particular trajectory should be terminated, and episode ended, so that agent can abort a trajectory navigating in an unsafe region. This can avoid areas of exploration that are not useful to learn, in turn making training more efficient.

Note that all the above incorporation of human expertise can be useful in conventional RL applications. In RLHF, evaluative feedback rewards from experts can be incorporated in addition to defining the original reward from environment. RLHF uses human feedback to train AI systems to appear more human-like. In RLHF, human feedback is used to evaluate the quality of the agent's responses or actions and guide the learning process. The goal of RLHF is to make the AI system more accurate and human-like in its responses.The key difference between the two approaches is the role of human expertise. In incorporating human expertise in RL, the expertise is used to define the problem and guide the learning process, whereas in RLHF, human feedback is used to evaluate and improve the agent's performance. In other words, incorporating human expertise in RL is about defining the problem and setting the goals, while RLHF is about providing feedback to achieve those goals.To illustrate the difference, consider a robotics example. In incorporating human expertise in RL, a subject matter expert might define the states, actions, and rewards for a robot arm, such as the joint angles, motor speeds, and distance to target. In RLHF, a human might provide feedback on the quality of the robot's movements, such as rating the smoothness or accuracy of the arm's motion, and this feedback would be used to update the reward function and improve the robot's performance. In practice, both approaches can be combined. Considering the robot arm example, experts can define states, actions and rewards, and collect robot motion trajectories that can be used for rating or scoring using human feedback. Based on this feedback on the quality of robot’s trajectories, agent training can be refined by building a reward model.To deal with dynamic environments and changes, human expertise can be incorporated in design and algorithms to counter changing environmental factors, by training adaptive RL systems. Human feedback can further help with refining policy decisions during unforeseen scenarios or real-time situations emerging in the environment. We will now discuss the types of human feedback used in RL, in order to refine our understanding further.

## Types of Human Feedback in RL

The idea of incorporating human feedback into RL has been tried in several different ways in the past, and has gained traction in recent years, especially with the emergence of large language models and training of foundation models. Use of human signals for training agents has multiple forms. Broadly, human demonstration and human feedback are classified as different categories of using human signals for training agents. In RL, reward function design and engineering can be subjective, and getting it right can be tricky. Using human demonstration to define reward function is studied and practiced in the subdomain of inverse RL. Similarly, supervised learning using human demonstration and a direct mapping from states to actions based on the demonstration, is called behavioral cloning, which can be beneficial when defining reward functions is hard even for experts e.g. for complex environments. Both, inverse RL and behavioral cloning are methods within the class of **learning from demonstration** (**LfD**). Imitation learning is similar to LfD and includes behavioral cloning and inverse RL, but it can also extend to learning from any form of expert behavior, whether provided explicitly through demonstrations or implicitly through interactions. While LfD primarily focuses on direct demonstrations, it can also incorporate learning from the consequences of actions to refine the learned behavior. Therefore, both LfD and IL can involve learning from the consequences of actions, and the distinction between them lies more in their scope and methods rather than the type of feedback used. Readers interested in inverse RL can read more here: *A Survey of Inverse Reinforcement Learning: Challenges, Methods and Progress*: [https://arxiv.org/abs/1806.06877](https://arxiv.org/abs/1806.06877), *Guided Cost Learning: Deep Inverse Optimal Control via Policy Optimization*: [https://arxiv.org/pdf/1603.00448](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1603.00448) and *Generative Adversarial Imitation Learning*: [https://arxiv.org/pdf/1606.03476](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1606.03476), and for behavioral cloning, and imitation learning in general, refer here: *An Algorithmic Perspective on Imitation Learning*: https://arxiv.org/pdf/1811.06711 and *CS 285* (berkeley.edu): [https://rail.eecs.berkeley.edu/deeprlcourse/](https://rail.eecs.berkeley.edu/deeprlcourse/). This brings us to incorporating human feedback, which aims at minimizing the subjectivity and effort in reward design and engineering. Human feedback approach in reinforcement learning has evolved over the last two decades. The current usage of reinforcement learning from human feedback encompasses several methods, which can be broadly categorized as below:

*   **Learning from Human Reward or Evaluative Reinforcement**: Direct evaluative feedback impacting the agent's rewards.
*   **Preference-Based RL**: Feedback in the form of preferences between actions or outcomes to guide policy development.
*   **Human Feedback for Defining Reward Models**: Using human feedback to construct or refine a reward model.
*   **Semi-Supervised RL with Absolute Feedback on a Subset of Experiences**: Combining supervised learning with RL through feedback on specific experiences.

### Learning from Human Reward or Evaluative Reinforcement

This category involves direct human evaluative feedback impacting the agent’s rewards or learning process. It includes scalar rewards; humans provide scalar feedback (positive or negative rewards) based on the agent's actions or performance. This direct feedback helps shape the agent's behavior over time. The feedback is usually binary; simplified evaluative feedback where humans indicate whether an action or outcome is good or bad, often represented as +1 (positive) or -1 (negative). The readers already realized this approach in the last section from previous chapter. Some papers and resources for further reading are Learning from human-generated reward : https://repositories.lib.utexas.edu/items/20b9e8a1-a78d-4844-816f-3c0b0a4c848a, and TAMER: Training an Agent Manually via Evaluative Reinforcement | IEEE Conference Publication | IEEE Xplore: https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/4640845 .

### Preference-Based RL

This category focuses on human feedback in the form of preferences rather than absolute rewards. Humans indicate their preferences between different actions or outcomes, guiding the agent’s policy development. It usually requires:

*   **Pairwise Preferences:** Humans provide feedback by comparing pairs of trajectories or outcomes, indicating which one they prefer. The agent uses this preference information to learn a reward function or policy that aligns with human preferences. Given two robot motion trajectories that reach the end goal, one of the trajectories might be safer or less awkward than another. A human preference on this is much easier to learn than providing complex features in a reward function. Similarly, asking users to provide feedback on two movies A and B, about their preference is much easier than deducing it from usage.
*   **Ranking:** Extends pairwise preferences to ranking multiple trajectories or outcomes, providing more detailed preference information for the agent to learn from. Ranking robot trajectories based on human feedback are easier to incorporate compared to extracting complex features and inferring from them. Similarly, direct feedback in the form of user rating and ranking simplify the objectives and reduce the possibility of errors due to developing complex reward functions accommodating all possible trajectories or outcomes.

Preference-based RL usually involves using preferences to directly optimize policy as in *Reward-rational (implicit) choice: A unifying formalism for reward learning* :[https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper/2020/hash/2f10c1578a0706e06b6d7db6f0b4a6af-Abstract.xhtml](https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper/2020/hash/2f10c1578a0706e06b6d7db6f0b4a6af-Abstract.xhtml) and *A Survey of Preference-Based Reinforcement Learning Methods* (jmlr.org): https://jmlr.org/papers/v18/16-634.xhtml, but can sometimes involve reward function modification as can be seen in Deep Reinforcement Learning from Human Preferences : https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper/2017/hash/d5e2c0adad503c91f91df240d0cd4e49-Abstract.xhtml.

### Human Feedback for Defining Reward Models

In this category, human feedback is used to construct or refine the reward model that the RL agent aims to optimize, as detailed in A Survey of Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback : [https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.14925](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.14925). It requires

*   **Reward Model Training:** Humans provide feedback on the quality of outcomes or trajectories, which is used to train a reward model. This model then guides the agent's learning process. In training a self-driving car, human experts might review and rate different driving scenarios, such as lane changes or braking decisions. Their feedback on these scenarios helps build a reward model that informs the car's driving policy to ensure safety and adherence to traffic laws.
*   **Interactive Reward Specification:** Humans iteratively provide feedback to refine the reward model, ensuring it aligns with desired objectives and values. This can include corrections or adjustments based on observed agent behavior. In developing an AI for a video game, developers might initially set up a reward model based on generic goals like winning levels or scoring high. As the AI plays the game, human feedback helps tweak the reward model to better capture subtleties like strategic play or player enjoyment, ensuring that the AI's behavior matches the intended game experience.
*   **Direct Reward Feedback:** Humans provide explicit feedback on the agent's performance in terms of desired outcomes, and this feedback is used to construct a reward model. In training a chatbot, human evaluators might review interactions and provide feedback on how well the bot handles customer queries. This feedback directly influences the reward model, helping the bot learn to respond more effectively and provide satisfactory customer service.

This type of feedback and methodology is more scalable, and we will focus on more, in general.

### Semi-Supervised RL with Absolute Feedback on a Subset of Experiences

This approach combines elements of supervised learning with RL and is detailed in Semi-supervised reinforcement learning | by Paul Christiano | AI Alignment (ai-alignment.com): https://ai-alignment.com/semi-supervised-reinforcement-learning-cf7d5375197f, where humans provide absolute feedback on a subset of the agent's experiences:

*   **Labeling Experiences**: Humans label specific experiences (state-action pairs) with absolute feedback, such as correct or incorrect actions, which the agent uses to learn more efficiently. For a robotic arm trained to sort objects into different bins, humans can review a subset of the arm’s sorting actions (e.g., placing objects into bins) and label them as correct or incorrect based on whether the objects are sorted into the right bins.
*   **Bootstrapping with Partial Supervision:** The agent receives detailed feedback on a subset of its experiences and uses this information to bootstrap learning on unlabeled experiences through RL algorithms. In algorithmic trading, an RL agent might receive detailed feedback on specific trading actions (e.g., whether a trade was profitable or not). This feedback helps the agent understand the impact of various strategies and bootstrap learning for trades that were not explicitly labeled.
*   **Hybrid Approaches:** Combining absolute feedback with other forms of human feedback (like evaluative rewards or preferences) to enhance learning efficiency and effectiveness. An autonomous vehicle might receive absolute feedback on specific driving scenarios (e.g., "this lane change was safe") as well as evaluative feedback (e.g., "overall driving behavior is aggressive"). Combining these types of feedback helps the vehicle improve its driving policy more comprehensively.

In the following section, we will focus on how human preferences can be incorporated into RL strategy.

## Incorporating Human Preferences into RL

Incorporating human preferences and signals into RL training process or architecture involves several steps, as outlined in *Figure 2.2*. The first step is how the human preferences are collected—are they collected as binary inputs per step or per episode of the agent training, or a segment of trajectory of sequences of agent? Are humans using choices of multiple actions or resulting states or trajectories of states? Next step is to decide on how the human preferences are utilized to guide the development of agent’s policy. Is policy updated directly or value function is updated? Is reward function modified based on human preferences? Or, is a reward model built and trained, and then used for training the agent? The third and final step of incorporating human preferences is related to ethical considerations and safety. Processes mitigating bias, improving fairness, maintaining transparency and ensuring long-term alignment of AI with human need to be build and integrated into the training flow. We will analyze each step in the following subsections.

![Figure 2.2 - RLHF Process and Considerations for incorporating human feedback.](media/file19.png)

Figure 2.2 - RLHF Process and Considerations for incorporating human feedback.

### Collecting Human Preferences

Collection of human signals or preferences involves choice from implicit set of options that the human can make, and a function or model that maps these choices made by human to agent behavior. The type of human feedback or choice is termed **reward-rational implicit choice**, if given a grounding function it can be modeled as a choice that maximizes reward as elaborated her Reward-rational (implicit) choice: A unifying formalism for reward learning : https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper/2020/hash/2f10c1578a0706e06b6d7db6f0b4a6af-Abstract.xhtml. The form of human feedback can be many: comparison, demonstration, correction, improvement, correction, improvement, turning off, proxy rewards, reward and punishment (similar to what we used in previous chapter), initial state and credit assignments. The choice depends on the feedback type, for example during comparison, identity of feedback input chosen is enough, while during proxy reward, value of reward output is used, and a positive or negative assignment in case of reward and punishment type feedback.

### Utilizing human preferences

The collected human preference can be used for guiding the policy using some utility, map or a grounding function. The grounding function or model is dependent on not only the choices made, but also on the domain and scenario. The grounding function is dependent on not only the choices made, but also on the domain and scenario. It could simply be a +1 or -1 reward assignment similar to what we did in previous chapter, or it could be a proxy reward, simply picking between options provided to user ( provided unique identity is assigned to each choice).

### Evaluating and auditing human preferences

Evaluating and auditing human preferences in RL systems is crucial for ensuring that the system accurately reflects user values and operates fairly and effectively. Assessing how well the RL system incorporates human preferences and identifying potential areas for improvement, involves ensuring accuracy RL system by logging human preferences and how they are used within the training process. Tracking performance metrics is crucial for evaluating the effectiveness of an RL system. Key metrics include:

*   Alignment Metrics: These measure how well the RL system’s decisions align with user preferences. This can be assessed through:
*   Preference Satisfaction Scores
*   User Satisfaction Ratings
*   Adherence to Stated Preferences
*   Diversity Metrics: These evaluate the range of preferences accommodated by the system. Ensuring diversity is important for handling a wide array of user values and can be measured by examining:
*   The variety of user preferences satisfied
*   The system’s ability to cater to different user values

In addition to tracking metrics, regular audits can be conducted for in-production systems. This can include conducting periodic reviews with automated checks and human oversight. Engaging independent third parties to conduct unbiased audits of the system can provide an external perspective and enhance credibility. Feedback mechanisms such as ongoing user feedback can help identify any discrepancies between their preferences, the system’s actions, and inputs used for training (e.g. reward model). This may involve developing interactive platforms, where users can interactively report issues, suggest improvements, and provide direct feedback on the system’s decisions.In this section, we discussed what is needed for incorporating human preferences into RL system, specifically the important steps of collecting human preferences, utilizing human preferences by using a grounding function or model, and evaluation and auditing of the human preferences to ensure correct alignment and coverage with actual intent. While these steps provide assurance about quality of collection, utilization and temporal discrepancies, there are still several challenges with human feedback data. Next we will discuss the bias and other challenges with human feedback.

## Bias and Other Challenges

Human feedback, while valuable in RLHF (reinforcement learning with human feedback), can introduce various challenges, inaccuracies and biases. These can be broadly categorized into technological and ethical challenges and can be handled in technical and legal ways.Human preferences are inherently subjective, varying greatly between individuals. What one person values highly, another might consider unimportant. Even for the same person, preferences can change over time, influenced by new experiences, changing contexts, and evolving needs. Hence, it is necessary to do a thorough evaluation of human preferences, and integrate upon considering these imperfections in human signals. Some ways of handling diversity of human preferences are:

*   Aggregating multiple preferences using consensus methods such as majority voting, average ranking, or weighted scoring to aggregate preferences from multiple users, aiming to find a balance that reflects a broader consensus .
*   Grouping users with similar preferences into clusters and create tailored RL policies for each group.
*   Designing RL algorithms that can perform well across a range of possible preference scenarios, by creating policies that are not overly sensitive to specific preference variations.
*   Apply regularization techniques to prevent overfitting to specific preferences. This helps in creating more generalized policies that can adapt to varying preferences. For example, in \[2301.11774\] Reinforcement Learning from Diverse Human Preferences: [https://arxiv.org/abs/2301.11774](https://arxiv.org/abs/2301.11774), reward learning is stabilized by regularizing and also correcting predictions in latent space where inputs of reward models are mapped, applying a strong distribution constraint to inhibit inconsistencies in preferences.
*   Implement systems that continually learn and adapt to changing preferences through ongoing feedback loops. This ensures that the RL system remains aligned with user preferences even as they evolve. One example would be to create user interfaces and telemetry data collection processed that allow anonymized user activity and feedback to be collected, in order to update the data used for reward modeling. This would help retrain or fine-tune reward models to out-of-distribution data, if user preferences change.
*   Using models that can dynamically update based on new inputs, allowing the RL system to remain flexible and responsive to changes in user preferences.

Other issues that can arise that are not directly related to training policy or process are ethical considerations such as:

*   **Anonymization**: making sure human feedback is anonymized of human identity,
*   **Empowering Feedback Providers**: tooling for providing enough control to humans providing feedback to modify their preferences is in place,
*   **Transparency of Data Usage**: transparency of how data would be used needs to be incorporated and displayed to human feedback providers, and appropriate logging and traceability of data from the time human information is taken, processed to consumed is in place.
*   Biases and unfairness in human preferences, if data is not diverse enough.

### Anonymization

Anonymization of human feedback is a critical process that ensures the privacy and confidentiality of individuals providing feedback. It aims to render personal data nonpersonal, by removing any information that could directly or indirectly identify an individual. While pseudonymization addresses only direct risks from data, anonymization considers both direct and indirect risks. Anonymization aims at minimizing the risk of reidentification and protects individuals’ identities. The challenge involves balancing utility (usefulness of data) with privacy. The **Anonymization Decision-making Framework** (**ADF**) ensures that this feedback can be shared or analyzed without compromising privacy. ADF involves following considerations:

*   Technical: How to transform data while preserving utility.
*   Legal: Compliance with data protection laws (e.g., GDPR).
*   Ethical: Balancing privacy and utility.
*   Policy: Aligning with organizational policies and guidelines.

There are some challenges that are undertaken by ADF:

*   Utility vs. Privacy: Striking the right balance.
*   Context Dependency: Anonymization effectiveness varies based on context.
*   Emerging Risks: Advances in reidentification techniques require ongoing vigilance.

Here are some examples of Anonymization Techniques:

*   Aggregation: Grouping data to reduce granularity.
*   Generalization: Replacing specific values with ranges (e.g., age groups).
*   Randomization: Introducing noise or perturbation.
*   Masking: Hiding certain attributes (e.g., blurring faces in images).

In summary, anonymization is a multifaceted process that involves technical, legal, ethical, and policy considerations. It ensures that feedback providers can contribute candidly while safeguarding their privacy. Readers looking for more depth and guidance can refer to data specific references such as in here: [https://theodi.github.io/ukan-course/assets/odi-data-anonymization.pdf](https://theodi.github.io/ukan-course/assets/odi-data-anonymization.pdf).

### Empowering Feedback Providers

Giving feedback providers with more control can enhance their satisfaction, trust and engagement, which will in turn help in improving feedback quality. In cases where feedback providers are end users of the AI product, it also helps in improving product satisfaction scores and trust. Examples of human feedback provider control can be similar to user control provided in online products we use every day: preference settings: Users can adjust notification preferences, language settings, or privacy options, opt-out mechanisms: Users can choose not to participate in certain features (e.g., data collection, surveys), customization: allowing users to personalize their experience (e.g., themes, layouts), add granularity to feedback signals. Some examples of control to feedback providers might not necessarily be active control by providers directly, but organizational policy based or security based control for example security control may involves authorization: users should have appropriate permissions to modify settings, authentication: confirming the user’s identity prevents unauthorized changes, access controls: limiting who can modify preferences.

### Transparency of Data Usage

Besides empowering human feedback providers by actively designing user control mechanisms, providing information on how data will be used will allow humans interacting with AI with enough information to take the right decisions to help with the RL training, and at the same time be entrusted to act without privacy concerns. Transparency can be applied by considering these features in UI design:

*   Clear Notices: Display data collection notices prominently (e.g., pop-ups, banners).
*   Settings: Provide a dedicated section for privacy settings.
*   Consent Flow: Obtain explicit consent for specific data uses.

Some geographic locations have particular compliance requirements for how data is used, and transparency is mandatory, some examples of these are General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) and California Consumer Privacy Act (CCPA)

### Biases and Unfairness

While humans can be biased in providing their feedback, the collection process or entire process of collecting, preprocessing, utilizing feedback data requires bias prevention mechanisms.

#### Techniques for Removing Bias in Data Collection

Data collection process can be a source of bias, depending upon how the data is collected. For example, if data is collected from limited sources of similar types, even large amount of data will be biased. The following list shows some techniques that help in removing bias:

*   **Diversity in Data Sources:** Collection of preference data should be from a diverse range of human individuals under diverse scenarios across various demographics, backgrounds, time of day, week or year, and experiences. This helps ensure the preferences reflect a broader population and reduces the influence of any single group's bias.
*   **Balanced Sampling:** Actively sampling data to ensure representation from underrepresented groups can help reduce bias. This might involve setting quotas or employing weighting techniques during data collection.
*   **Blinding Techniques:** Implementing blind data collection methods by blinding the human feedback providers from signals that are known to cause decision bias can be effective. For example, minimizing the influence of factors like race, gender, or age on preference selection, for use cases where these factors are known to create bias. For example, have humans evaluate options without identifying information.

#### Techniques for Data Preprocessing

While improving data collection processes can help with avoiding and minimizing sources of biased data, it is possible that bias is still introduced or is pre-existing, for example in historical data. To counteract such cases, preprocessing of the collected data to identify bias and mitigate it can be effective. Some techniques for data preprocessing to handle bias are as below.

*   **Detecting Bias:** Use statistical tests or fairness metrics to identify potential biases in the collected data. Techniques like anomaly detection or examining correlations between preferences and demographics can help uncover biases. Once identified, consider removing biased data points or adjusting their weights.
*   **Data Augmentation:** Artificially generate additional preference data to address imbalances or underrepresentation in specific categories. This can involve techniques like data mirroring (flipping existing data points) or generating synthetic data based on existing preferences. This can be done during the phase of reward model building also, for more details see *Aligning Large Multimodel Models with Factually Augmented RLHF*: [https://arxiv.org/pdf/2309.14525](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2309.14525)

#### Algorithmic Techniques

In addition to handling bias during pre-processing phase, bias can also be handled during the training phase using algorithms and learning frameworks. During the learning process of the agent, certain algorithmic techniques can help with minimizing bias.

*   **Debiasing Algorithms:** Certain algorithms can be used to mitigate bias in preference data during the learning process. These algorithms might down-weight the influence of potentially biased preferences or adjust the reward function to counteract bias. Reward models are biased towards longer outputs, to align training to human intent, an ensemble method approach called Product-of-Experts is employed in \[2310.05199\] Loose lips sink ships: Mitigating Length Bias in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback: [https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.05199](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.05199) to improve in identifying and mitigating the length bias.
*   **Fairness-Aware Learning Frameworks:** Frameworks that incorporate fairness constraints into the learning process can be implemented 2401.15270 SimFair: Physics-Guided Fairness-Aware Learning with Simulation Models: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2401.15270. These frameworks might penalize the model for making decisions that favor specific groups or ensure the model's outputs are statistically fair across different demographics.

#### Human Feedback Calibration

While bias can emanate during data collection, preprocessing and algorithmic stages, it can also be due to human compliance or cognitive bias. To counter this source of bias due to human inputs and inherent human experience and behavior, calibration can be necessary.

*   **Calibrated Annotations:** Train annotators to be aware of common cognitive biases and provide guidelines to minimize their influence on preference selection. This might involve standardized training procedures or providing clear evaluation criteria.
*   **Active Learning:** Interactively query annotators for preferences on specific data points where the model is most uncertain or where potential bias might be present. This helps focus human feedback on areas where it can have the most impact on reducing bias.

Above challenges and mitigation techniques to handle data and human bias, and necessity of complying to data regulatory requirements are some guidelines and examples that are necessary. In addition to above, there may be local requirements and custom application specific data and human requirements that can be handled by working with domain experts.

## Summary

In this chapter, we covered role of RLHF and how it enhances learning efficiency. We also learned what it means to incorporate human expertise into RL training and how it is done. Further, we discussed various types of human feedback in RL, details of processes involved in incorporating human preferences into RL, and real world challenges in application of RLHF. With this understanding, in the following chapter, we will discuss reward modeling based on human feedback data.
