*   `ipywidgets == 8.1.5`

In addition to the above libraries and installation, we will also use google colab for fine-tuning using GPUs (more on that in the section on Fine Tuning).To get started, clone this git repository: [https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback](https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback), and change directory to `chapter 5`.

## Introduction to Language Models

Traditionally, **language models** (**LM**) are probabilistic models designed to predict the most likely next word in a sentence based on available input words preceding in the sentence. Predicting next word in a text can be useful in several applications such as text generation, translation, summarization and sentiment analysis. From a broader perspective, it predicts the likelihood of a sequence of words and can be used to estimate how probable a given sentence is, or to generate coherent text based on a prompt.

### Early Language Models: N-grams

The earliest language models relied on **n-gram** approaches, which calculate the probability of a word based on the preceding n−1 words. For example, a bigram model (where n=2) would look at the probability of a word given the previous word. While simple and easy to implement, n-grams have significant limitations:

*   **Sparsity**: As the size of n increases, the number of possible word combinations grows exponentially, leading to sparse data issues.
*   **Context Limitations**: They can only consider a limited context (the last n−1words), which often leads to a loss of meaningful relationships in longer texts.
*   **<>**

### Statistical and Neural Language Models

To address the limitations of n-grams, researchers turned to **statistical language models**, which employed techniques like **Hidden Markov Models (HMMs)** and **Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA)** to capture more complex relationships in text. However, these models were still constrained by their reliance on handcrafted features and assumptions about language structure.The introduction of **neural networks** marked a significant turning point. Early neural language models, such as those using feedforward architectures, began to incorporate embeddings (like Word2Vec) to represent words as dense vectors in a continuous space. This approach allowed for capturing semantic relationships between words (e.g., “king” - “man” + “woman” - “queen”).

### Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs)

**RNNs** and their advanced versions, **Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM)** networks, were developed to handle sequential data better. They could maintain a hidden state to remember previous words, allowing for more context-aware predictions. However, RNNs struggled with long-range dependencies due to issues like vanishing gradients.

### The Emergence of Transformers

The introduction of the **transformer architecture** in 2017, as detailed in the paper “*Attention is All You Need*” by Vaswani et al: [https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.03762](https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.03762) https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.03762, revolutionized language modeling. Transformers use *self-attention mechanisms* to weigh the importance of different words in a sequence, regardless of their distance from one another. This allows them to capture long-range dependencies effectively and parallelize training, significantly speeding up the process.

### Large Language Models (LLMs)

Building on transformers, large language models like **BERT**, **GPT-3**, and others emerged. These models are characterized by:

*   **Scale**: They are trained on vast amounts of text data (often hundreds of gigabytes) and contain billions of parameters, enabling them to learn intricate patterns in language.
*   **Transfer Learning**: Many LLMs are pre-trained on a wide array of tasks and then fine-tuned on specific applications, allowing them to generalize well across various NLP tasks.
*   **Versatility**: They can perform zero-shot or few-shot learning, enabling them to adapt to new tasks with minimal examples.

Considering that transformer models are state-of-the-art models that led to revolutionizing the NLP research and practice, we will focus on this model only. In the next section, we will go through the transformer architecture and its components, and show implementation snippets.

## The Transformer Architecture

Transformers emerged from the need to handle long-range dependencies in sequential data, which traditional **recurrent neural networks** (**RNNs**) struggled with, particularly in **natural language processing** (**NLP**). The landmark paper "*Attention is All You Need*" by Vaswani et al. (2017), mentioned earlier, introduced the transformer architecture, which replaced recurrent layers with attention mechanisms. This shift allowed for better parallelization during training and improved performance on tasks involving large datasets.The Transformer is a model designed to understand and generate sequences of data, like sentences. Instead of processing words one by one in order, it looks at all the words at once. The core idea behind it is *self-attention*, which allows the model to focus on different parts of the sentence based on their relevance to each other.

### Embedding the Input

When a Transformer processes text, it starts by converting words into numbers (vectors) that the model can understand. This step is called **embedding**.For example, let’s say we have a sequence of words: , where each ​ represents a word in the sequence. Since words are represented as one-hot vectors (large vectors full of zeros with a single 1 indicating the word), we use an **embedding matrix** ​ to convert these one-hot vectors into dense, meaningful vectors. As a side note, for practical considerations, each ​, can be a token (a defined splitting of word). But, for purpose of understanding, we are considering each word as a token.Mathematically, if ​, where V is the size of the vocabulary and is the model’s hidden dimension, each word embedding is: . The result is a sequence of embeddings: , where each ​ is a dense vector of size ​. **Intuition**: Think of embeddings as a way to represent words that captures their meaning. Words with similar meanings will have similar embeddings, which helps the model understand relationships between words.

![Figure 5. 1 Embedding the input involves converting text words or tokens to numeric vectors using an embedding model.](media/file28.png)

Figure 5. 1 Embedding the input involves converting text words or tokens to numeric vectors using an embedding model.

### Positional Encoding

Transformers process entire sequences in parallel, but this creates a challenge: how does the model know the order of the words in a sentence? For example, "The sun rises from the east" is very different in meaning and semantics from "The east rises from the sun," even though the words are the same.To solve this, the Transformer adds something called *positional encoding* to each word embedding. This encoding injects information about the position of each word in the sequence. The positional encoding for each position *t* is defined mathematically as:This creates a unique positional vector ​ for each word’s position. The model then adds this vector to the word embeddings:This new sequence is now the input to the transformer’s encoder-decoder architecture, starting with self-attention aspect. A simple component of the architecture is shown below:

![Figure 5. 2 Adds position encoding to input embedding, outputting a new sequence Z that accounts for position of the words or tokens relative to other words/tokens.](media/file29.png)

Figure 5. 2 Adds position encoding to input embedding, outputting a new sequence Z that accounts for position of the words or tokens relative to other words/tokens.

Positional encodings help the model know the order of words. Without this, it would treat " The sun rises from the east " and " The east rises from the sun " the same. Using pytorch these can be implemented as shown in below snippet:

```
import torch
from torch import nn
class PositionalEncoding(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, d_model, dropout=0.1, max_len=5000):
        super(PositionalEncoding, self).__init__()
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(p=dropout)
        pe = torch.zeros(max_len, d_model)
        position = torch.arange(0, max_len, dtype=torch.float).unsqueeze(1) 
        div_term = torch.exp(torch.arange(0, d_model, 2).float() * -(torch.log(torch.tensor(10000.0)) / d_model)) 
        pe[:, 0::2] = torch.sin(position * div_term) 
        pe[:, 1::2] = torch.cos(position * div_term) 
        pe = pe.unsqueeze(0)  # Shape (1, max_len, d_model)
        self.register_buffer('pe', pe)
    def forward(self, x):
        x = x + self.pe[:, :x.size(1), :]
        return self.dropout(x) of shape
```

In the above snippet, we `create a matrix of zeros of shape (max_len, d_model) to ensure that has appropriate dimensions. The dimension of d_model accomodates for including both the sine and cosine components, hence it is double of the term productizing in their argument position*div_term and. Since both the arguments within sine and cosine components use position as the first term, it is of shape (max_len, 1) . Similarly, the second div_term is of shape (d_model/2), adding up to d_model, half for sine and half for cosine. In d_model, the sine and cosine are applied on even and odd indices respectively, as can be seen from how pe[:, 0::2] and pe[:,1::2] are implemented. We add all these in the __init__ method of the PositionalEncoding class. The forward method adds positional encoding to the input tensor, and returns after applying dropout for regularization, in order to avoid overfitting.`Now that we know how positional encoding can be added to input embedding, and importance of word position is incorporated, we will visit the self-attention mechanism that ties the learning of word relationships.

### Self-Attention: Learning Word Relationships

The **self-attention** mechanism is the heart of the Transformer. It allows the model to focus on different words in a sentence when processing each word. For example, in the sentence " The sun rises from the east," when the model processes "rises," it should focus on both "sun" (the subject) and "east" (the object) to understand the meaning.For each word, the model creates three vectors:

*   A **query** Q,
*   A **key** K,
*   A **value** V.

These vectors are calculated by multiplying the input Z with learned weight matrices:To figure out how much attention each word should give to other words, the model computes a similarity score between the query of one word and the keys of all the words. This is done using the dot product and scaling:The **softmax** function ensures that the scores add up to 1, turning them into probabilities. This gives us a weighted combination of the value vectors, where words that are more important get more weight.Self-attention lets each word "look at" other words in the sentence and focus on the ones that matter most to understanding the meaning. It is like highlighting the most important words for each part of the sentence.

![Figure 5. 3 Schematic showing self attention (scaled dot product) and multihead attention.](media/file30.png)

Figure 5. 3 Schematic showing self attention (scaled dot product) and multihead attention.

### Multi-Head Attention: Seeing from Multiple Perspectives

Rather than just using one attention mechanism, the Transformer uses several **attention heads** to capture different aspects of the relationships between words. This is called **multi-head attention**.Each head computes its own self-attention using different learned weight matrices . The outputs of all the heads are then concatenated and projected back into a single matrix into the final output of the multi-head attention layer:Each head might focus on different relationships, such as one head focusing on word-order relationships and another on synonyms or grammatical roles. This helps the model understand word relationships from multiple perspectives. Think of multi-head attention as looking at a sentence through different lenses. Each lens might focus on a different relationship, such as one head looking at subject-object relationships, while another focuses on modifiers like adjectives. The multi-head attention output is combined and passed into the **feed-forward network**.A code snippet on how readers can implement this is given below. The `MultiHeadAttention` class inherits from `torch.nn.Module`, which is the base class for all neural network modules in PyTorch. The constructor initializes the attention mechanism with the input size (`d_model`) and the number of attention heads (`num_heads`). `dense` is the dimensionality per head, computed as `d_model // num_heads`. This splits the overall embedding size into smaller sections for each attention head.

```
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
class MultiHeadAttention(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, d_model, num_heads):
        super(MultiHeadAttention, self).__init__()
        self.num_heads = num_heads
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.depth = d_model // num_heads
        self.wq = nn.Linear(d_model, d_model)
        self.wk = nn.Linear(d_model, d_model)
        self.wv = nn.Linear(d_model, d_model)
        self.dense = nn.Linear(d_model, d_model)
```

The `split_heads` function reshapes the input tensor into multiple heads. It first splits the embedding dimension (`d_model`) into multiple heads of size depth for parallel attention computation. Then, it permutes the dimensions to reorganize the tensor shape for multi-head processing: **(batch\_size, seq\_len, d\_model)** **→ (batch\_size, num\_heads, seq\_len, depth).**The snippet implementing split\_heads function is as follows.

```
    def split_heads(self, x, batch_size):
        # Split the embedding into multiple heads
        x = x.view(batch_size, -1, self.num_heads, self.depth)
        return x.permute(0, 2, 1, 3)  # (batch_size, num_heads, seq_len, depth)
```

The `forward` method processes the input queries (q), keys (k), and values (v). It also takes a mask, which is typically used to prevent the model from attending to certain positions (e.g., padding tokens in sequences). It takes following steps:Apply the linear projections (wq, wk, wv) to compute queries, keys, and values.Split the result into multiple heads using split_heads.Perform **scaled dot-product attention** (details below).Combine the heads back into a single tensor using view and dense.

```
    def forward(self, q, k, v, mask):
        batch_size = q.size(0)
        # Linear projections
        q = self.wq(q)
        k = self.wk(k)
        v = self.wv(v)
        # Split heads
        q = self.split_heads(q, batch_size)
        k = self.split_heads(k, batch_size)
        v = self.split_heads(v, batch_size)
        # Scaled dot-product attention
        scaled_attention, _ = self.scaled_dot_product_attention(q, k, v, mask)
        scaled_attention = scaled_attention.permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous()
        concat_attention = scaled_attention.view(batch_size, -1, self.d_model)
        return self.dense(concat_attention)
```

The `scaled_dot_product_attention` method called above, is constructed as follows. The query (q) is multiplied by the key's transpose (`k.transpose(-2, -1)`), resulting in a similarity score between queries and keys. This gives a tensor of shape (`batch_size, num_heads, seq_len, seq_len`). The similarity score is scaled by the square root of `dk` (dimension of the key), which helps to prevent extremely large values and stabilize gradients. If a mask is provided, positions in the attention logits that should not be attended to (such as padding tokens) are assigned a very large negative value, ensuring that their softmax value will be close to zero. The softmax function is applied to the attention logits along the last dimension (sequence length), turning the scores into probabilities that sum to 1. Finally, the attention weights are used to weight the values (v), yielding the output of the attention mechanism.

```
    def scaled_dot_product_attention(self, q, k, v, mask):
        matmul_qk = torch.matmul(q, k.transpose(-2, -1))  # (batch_size, num_heads, seq_len, seq_len)
        dk = k.size(-1)
        scaled_attention_logits = matmul_qk / torch.sqrt(torch.tensor(dk, dtype=torch.float32))
        if mask is not None:
            scaled_attention_logits += (mask * -1e9)
        attention_weights = nn.functional.softmax(scaled_attention_logits, dim=-1)
        output = torch.matmul(attention_weights, v) 
        return output, attention_weights
```

The outputs of the scaled\_dot\_product\_attention are output and attention weights, with outputs having dimension of **(batch\_size, num\_heads, seq\_len, depth).**

### Feed-Forward Network

After attention, the model applies a **feed-forward network** to each word’s representation independently. This network consists of two linear transformations with a ReLU activation in between:The FFN operates on each word independently but helps capture more complex interactions within each word’s updated representation from the attention mechanism. The output from the FFN is passed back to the **multi-head attention** layer for additional iterations. Multiple layers of attention and FFN are stacked on top of each other to deepen the model’s understanding.

### Residual Connections and Layer Normalization

To help the model learn more effectively, the Transformer uses **residual connections** and **layer normalization**. These mechanisms ensure that the model retains the original information at each step and helps stabilize learning. To help the model learn effectively, the Transformer adds **residual connections** around the attention and feed-forward layers. This means the input to each layer is added to the layer’s output:Each time the data passes through self-attention or the feed-forward network, the input is added back to the output (residual connection), and the result is normalized. This keeps the information flowing through the network and helps with training stability. Residual connections are added around both the self-attention and feed-forward layers to maintain data integrity. These normalized outputs move between each of the stacked layers.The combined multihead attention with feed-forward network and layer normalization can be implemented similar to below snippet, where \``self.fnn`` is the feed-forward network and `self.layernorm1` and `self.layernorm2` are layer normalizations over attention output and feedforward network outputs.

```
class TransformerEncoderLayer(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, d_model, num_heads, d_ff, dropout_rate=0.1):
        super(TransformerEncoderLayer, self).__init__()
        self.attention = MultiHeadAttention(d_model, num_heads)
        self.ffn = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Linear(d_model, d_ff),
            nn.ReLU(),
            nn.Linear(d_ff, d_model)
        )
        self.layernorm1 = nn.LayerNorm(d_model)
        self.layernorm2 = nn.LayerNorm(d_model)
        self.dropout1 = nn.Dropout(dropout_rate)
        self.dropout2 = nn.Dropout(dropout_rate)
    def forward(self, x, mask):
        attn_output = self.attention(x, x, x, mask)
        out1 = self.layernorm1(x + self.dropout1(attn_output))  # Residual connection
        ffn_output = self.ffn(out1)
        return self.layernorm2(out1 + self.dropout2(ffn_output))  # Residual connection
```

### Encoder-Decoder Structure

While encoder is the major part of the transformer for most applications requiring representation learning, decoder can be also necessary based on applications especially where generation is necessary. The full Transformer model consists of two parts:**The Encoder**: This processes the input sentence, performing several layers of multi-head attention and feed-forward networks to transform the input into a rich representation.**The Decoder**: The decoder generates the output sentence (such as a translation) by using both the previous words in the output sequence and the encoder’s representations of the input.Each decoder layer performs **self-attention** to focus on the output sequence generated so far and **encoder-decoder attention** to focus on the input sequence’s encoding. The decoder continues this process until the full output sequence is generated.Typically, the positional encoding is applied before the input is passed through the encoder layers. The encoder class can be written as below integrating positional encoding and transformer encoder layer:

```
class TransformerEncoder(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, d_model, num_heads, d_ff, num_layers, dropout_rate=0.1):
        super(TransformerEncoder, self).__init__()
        self.positional_encoding = PositionalEncoding(d_model, dropout_rate)
        self.enc_layers = nn.ModuleList([TransformerEncoderLayer(d_model, num_heads, d_ff, dropout_rate) for _ in range(num_layers)])
   
    def forward(self, x, mask):
        x = self.positional_encoding(x) 
        for layer in self.enc_layers:
            x = layer(x, mask)
        return x
```

Above snippets of code are provided only to help readers with how we can implement a transformer encoder if they are building the transformer form scratch using only Pytorch. We have provided all snippets for building the encoder, also added to the github repository, in the file in **chapter5** with name **transformer\_encoder.py**. The above snippets will help readers self-realize their understanding, below test snippet will help readers verify if the components are working correctly.

```
def test_transformer_components():
    pos_encoding = PositionalEncoding(d_model=64)
    sample_input = torch.randn(10, 50, 64)  # (batch_size, seq_len, d_model)
    pos_encoded_output = pos_encoding(sample_input)
    assert pos_encoded_output.shape == sample_input.shape, "Positional encoding test failed!"
    self_attn = MultiHeadAttention(d_model=64, num_heads=8)
    attn_output, _ = self_attn(sample_input, sample_input, sample_input)
    assert attn_output.shape == sample_input.shape, "Multi-head attention test failed!"
    enc_layer = TransformerEncoderLayer(d_model=64, num_heads=8, d_ff=256)
    encoder_output = enc_layer(sample_input, None)
    assert encoder_output.shape == sample_input.shape, "Encoder layer test failed!"
    print("All tests passed!")
test_transformer_components()
```

For tasks like text generation, the decoder layer is responsible for generating tokens sequentially. It uses masked self-attention to ensure that future tokens are not visible to the model, preserving autoregressive behavior.The decoder is similar to the encoder in structure but with key differences:

*   **Masked Multi-Head Attention**: This ensures that when predicting the next token, the decoder only attends to the tokens generated so far, not future tokens. This masking is crucial for auto-regressive tasks, where you want to generate sequences step by step.
*   **Cross-Attention Layer**: In addition to the self-attention (like in the encoder), the decoder has an extra attention layer that attends to the output from the encoder. This allows the decoder to focus on relevant parts of the input sequence while generating the output.

In practice, transformer implementations use distributed training frameworks to improve training efficiency, and can have very complex implementations. There are, however, several libraries with transformer implementations e.g. pytorch, huggingface and tensorflow have transformer implementations. In this chapter we have shown encoder and representation learning snippets above. The decoder aspect is necessary for sequence generation, and we will continue henceforth using transformer library to simplify implementation, and focus more on fine-tuning aspects with pretrained models rather than training language models from scratch. A good reference for training a transformer and generative pretrained transformer (GPT) models from scratch can be read here: https://mng.bz/M96o .While we discussed how transformers are built and the architectural components of transformers in this section, we will now focus on using them as fine-tuning language models with a hands-on example, in the following section.

## Fine-Tuning Language Models

In this section we will first understand how pretrained language models can be loaded, and then step through the process of fine-tuning. Pretrained language models are models with transformer architecture that have been already trained on a body of data, in this case text data. This significantly saves time when we are trying to solve similar problems where previously learned skills or representations can be utilized. Fine-tuning on pretrained models also helps with reducing carbon footprint, as training of large models requires lot of energy.We will now explore the HuggingFace transformer library, which provides a variety of pretrained models and tools for various natural language processing (NLP) tasks like text classification, translation, and summarization It is a popular library for working with state-of-the-art transformer models. Moreover, it also enables performing tasks across multiple modalities such as text, vision and audio. In addition to transformer library, we will also use a the datasets library from Hugging Face that provides easy access to a wide range of datasets and utilities for data processing. Using the datasets library simplifies tasks like loading, transforming, and preprocessing datasets, common to workflows of training machine learning models. This simplification will allow us to focus on using these datasets to infer from pretrained models, and later fine-tune them with specific data.Unlike traditional machine learning workflows, fine-tuning model workflow involves model selection, fine-tuning and evaluation phase, instead of training and evaluation phase. There exist some similarities with ML workflow, as far as data preparation phase is concerned. In Figure 4, we can see that the workflow can be divided into three phases: 1) Data preparation that includes collection, exploratory analysis, and preprocessing, 2) model selection, tuning the model and evaluating, 3) Deployment, monitoring and maintenance. The first and third phases are similar to traditional machine learning workloads, so we will mainly rely of datasets libraries for the first phase, which has a variety of datasets.

![Figure 5. 4 Fine-tuning workflow showing various phases.](media/file31.png)

Figure 5. 4 Fine-tuning workflow showing various phases.

### Set Up

Transformer models with reasonable size, requires hardware with graphical processing units (GPU), in order to complete training within reasonable time. While fine-tuning reduces training time, it also requires GPUs to train faster. For the exercises in this chapter, we will use them to reduce training time overhead. There are several options to setup training workflow using GPUs:

*   Get a local machine (a desktop or a laptop) with GPU hardware and enable GPU access. To check NVIDIA GPU details, you can run this command in your command line interface, depending on whether you have a windows or a mac operating system: \`nvidia-smi\`.
*   Buy a cloud resource such as a VM or container providing GPUs, some of the major ones are: Azure, AWS and Google Cloud.
*   Services such as google colab Pro, lambda labs, Azure ML service, and Amazon Sagemaker are an option.

Readers who are seriously investing into developing a workflow, can try any of the above options and go through corresponding documentation. For learning and educating yourself, using free tier of any of the cloud resources and/or services is a good starting point. For the purposes of this chapter, we will adhere to an option that will be most inclusive, that means fine-tuning lighter language models using free tier or limited cost options which students and readers just trying out and self-educating can afford. One such option is using the free google colab notebooks, or if needed monthly subscription.We will also use some huggingface datasets library and pretrained models from transformers library. Note that google colab will have some of the essential machine learning libraries already installed, but some libraries such as datasets needs to be installed using command such as `!pip install datasets`. In the notebook, we will first install and/or import all required libraries, as shown below.

```
# Import libraries
from transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModelForSequenceClassification, TrainingArguments, Trainer
from datasets import load_dataset
import torch
import numpy as np
from sklearn.metrics import accuracy_score, f1_score
```

### Datasets

Let’s go through the steps of using the datasets library for data preparation. Using the datasets library we can easily load the data, simplifying the data collection step. We will load only a subset of the dataset, in order to demonstrate how to fine-tune and how it improves inference, in this chapter. We will now load a subset of the “imdb” dataset, about 1000 samples, which is part of the datasets library from HuggingFace, from popular internet movie database (IMDB) source. Then, the data is split into 80% training data and 20 percent test data using the train\_test\_split method.

```
# Load dataset
dataset = load_dataset("imdb", split="train[:1000]")  # Sample a subset to keep it lightweight
dataset = dataset.train_test_split(test_size=0.2)  # Split into train and test
train_dataset, test_dataset = dataset['train'], dataset['test']
```

The datasets library from Hugging Face loads data primarily as Dataset objects. When you load datasets, it organizes multiple splits (like 'train', 'validation', 'test') into a dictionary-like structure where each key corresponds to a specific split, and the values are Dataset objects. DatasetDict is a specialized dictionary containing splits of the dataset. Each entry in the DatasetDict is a Dataset object that provides efficient access to the data, with functionalities for mapping, filtering, and processing. We use the train split and 1000 samples from within it, to keep the training light. Next, we will check some samples and check size of the training and test dataset.

```
# EDA - Check some samples
print("Sample Data: ", train_dataset[0])
print("Training Data Size:", len(train_dataset))
print("Testing Data Size:", len(test_dataset))
# Distribution of labels
print("Label Distribution: ", train_dataset.features['label'].names)
```

We can see the sample text of a movie review, data sizes, sentiment based label distribution:`Sample Data: {'text': "Holy freaking God all-freaking-mighty. This movie was so bad, I thought I was on drugs. In a bad way... The character acting is the poorest thing I've seen in quite some time. This movie was more akin to Lord of the G-Strings, IMHO(it's a real movie). Most of the movie appeared to be done on a horrible green screen. My favorite part was when they are in the carriage, and you can tell there's no horse. They're fleeing from alien monsters, and going about the same speed as a swift jog. Then it switches to a far-shot with a ridiculous CG horse. And the CG in general seems to be sub-par to 1992's Beyond the Mind's Eye. I mean, Come on, really. It felt like a horrible episode of Hercules, only without Kevin Sorbo there to save the day. Worst. Movie. Ever.", 'label': 0}`

```
Training Data Size: 800
Testing Data Size: 200
Label Distribution:  ['neg', 'pos']
```

If you load the dataset in individual cell and without splitting, you will see the data loading progress, and files stored in .parquet format. This choice is for their efficient columnar storage, which speeds up data loading and reduces file size through compression. They also support schema evolution and integrate well with big data tools, enhancing overall performance and flexibility.

### Tokenization

The tokenization process involves using specific tokenizer classes (like BertTokenizer or GPT2Tokenizer) that implement strategies such as subword tokenization (e.g., WordPiece or Byte Pair Encoding) to handle unknown words and reduce vocabulary size. Tokenizers also insert special tokens (like \[CLS\] for classification) and convert text into numerical identifiers corresponding to tokens in the model's vocabulary. They generate attention masks to indicate which tokens should be processed and segment identifiers to differentiate between input segments. Additionally, tokenizers can pad or truncate sequences to maintain consistent input lengths, making the transition from raw text to model-ready input efficient and effective for various NLP tasks. Here, we will use a particular tokenization class from the transformers library—the `AutoTokenizer` class, which allows you to load a tokenizer for a specified model. For this specific case, we specify to load the tokenizer for the **DistilBERT** model, a smaller and faster version of **BERT** that is uncased (i.e., it does not differentiate between uppercase and lowercase letters). The snippet below shows how to load the tokenization model and apply it to tokenize the data.

```
# Load tokenizer
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("distilbert-base-uncased")
# Tokenize the datasets
def tokenize_data(example):
    return tokenizer(example['text'], padding="max_length", truncation=True)
train_dataset = train_dataset.map(tokenize_data, batched=True)
test_dataset = test_dataset.map(tokenize_data, batched=True)
# Set format for PyTorch
train_dataset = train_dataset.rename_column("label", "labels").with_format("torch")
test_dataset = test_dataset.rename_column("label", "labels").with_format("torch")
```

The tokenize function uses the loaded tokenizer, finds the text values in the `text` field. The parameters padding="max_length" and truncation=True ensure that all tokenized outputs are of consistent length, padding shorter sequences to the maximum length and truncating longer ones. The `dataset.map` method applies the `tokenize_function` to the entire dataset in a batched manner, meaning it processes multiple examples at once. This is efficient and speeds up the tokenization process.

### Model Inference

Upon preprocessing the data into tokenized dataset, the next step involves Model Selection and using it to inference and test. We will use the `AutoModelForSequenceClassification` class from transformers library. This class allows you to automatically load a model suitable for sequence classification based on the model name you provide (e.g., "distilbert-base-uncased"). This provides flexibility to easily switch between different models without worrying about the underlying architecture. It also automatically fetches the configuration associated with that model with details like the number of output classes, and works seamlessly with the corresponding tokenizer. The output of the model includes logits, which can be converted to probabilities using a softmax function. This is useful for determining the predicted class probabilities for the given input. The model loading is mostly specifying the model and using the automodel method.

```
# Load the pretrained model
model = AutoModelForSequenceClassification.from_pretrained("distilbert-base-uncased", num_labels=2)
```

To evaluate the models, we write a function to compute some metrics. Here we will use the accuracy score that checks predicted output with labels on sentiment score of the movie reviews, then we also use weighted average f1 score comparing labels and predictions:

```
# Evaluation function
def compute_metrics(pred):
    logits, labels = pred
    predictions = np.argmax(logits, axis=-1)
    accuracy = accuracy_score(labels, predictions)
    f1 = f1_score(labels, predictions, average="weighted")
    return {"accuracy": accuracy, "f1": f1}
```

In the snippets below, we are running inference for the 20% test split data, test\_dataset, we loaded. Since model is pretrained, we are just going to inference using the base model, and consider the evaluation as baseline evaluation to compare with fine-tuning later. The training arguments and trainer methods imported from transformers library are used for specifying the prediction parameters. Note that we disable logging to Weights &Biases, an experimentation framework. But, readers can remove the line `report_to = ‘none’` to enable default Weights and Biases experimentation framework. It will require subscription key to use experimentation framework. There are also other opensource versions such as Mlflow, which readers can explore later. Usually this is helpful in real projects, where you want to track experimentation progress, as it involves hyperparameter tuning based on weights, batch size, tokenization, and prompts in GPT based experiments. For now, we do not require it.

```
# Trainer for evaluation
training_args = TrainingArguments(
    output_dir="./results",
    per_device_eval_batch_size=8,
    report_to='none')                # Disable logging to W&B)
trainer = Trainer(model=model, args=training_args, eval_dataset=test_dataset, compute_metrics=compute_metrics)
# Baseline evaluation
baseline_results = trainer.evaluate()
print("Baseline Results:", baseline_results)
```

Upon running the inference, the output might have a message indicating that some weights were not initialized from model checkpoint. It is okay to disregard it for now. This is mainly for models resuming training from exact weights, but that is not our goal here. This is how the output will appearSome weights of DistilBertForSequenceClassification were not initialized from the model checkpoint at distilbert-base-uncased and are newly initialized: \['classifier.bias', 'classifier.weight', 'pre\_classifier.bias', 'pre\_classifier.weight'\]You should probably TRAIN this model on a down-stream task to be able to use it for predictions and inference.The output of the inference, baseline results will appears something like this:

```
Baseline Results: {'eval_loss': 0.6827018857002258, 'eval_model_preparation_time': 0.0025, 'eval_accuracy': 0.765, 'eval_f1': 0.8668555240793201, 'eval_runtime': 3.0755, 'eval_samples_per_second': 65.03, 'eval_steps_per_second': 8.129}
```

We will use these later for comparison after fine-tuning the model using the training data.

### Fine-tuning

Now, we train the pre-trained transformer model (DistilBERT in this case as discussed in previous sections) on our specific dataset to improve its performance on a targeted task, such as sentiment classification for the movies dataset from IMDB. We start by defining the **training arguments**, specifying key hyperparameters such as the learning rate (how quickly the model updates), batch size (number of samples processed in each training step), number of epochs (full passes through the dataset), and weight decay (to prevent overfitting). Next, we initialize a Trainer object, which automates the fine-tuning process by combining the model, training arguments, dataset, and a function to calculate evaluation metrics. When we call trainer.train(), it iterates through the training data in batches, using the loss function to adjust model parameters with each batch, gradually adapting the model weights to better predict labels for the task. During this fine-tuning, the model learns patterns specific to the dataset, which enhances its ability to classify new examples in the same category, and this adapted model is expected to perform better than the baseline pre-trained version.

```
# Fine-tuning arguments
training_args = TrainingArguments(
    output_dir="./results",
    evaluation_strategy="epoch",
    learning_rate=2e-5,
    per_device_train_batch_size=8,
    per_device_eval_batch_size=8,
    num_train_epochs=3,
    weight_decay=0.01,
    report_to='none'
)
# Trainer for fine-tuning
trainer = Trainer(
    model=model,
    args=training_args,
    train_dataset=train_dataset,
    eval_dataset=test_dataset,
    compute_metrics=compute_metrics,
)
# Fine-tuning
trainer.train()
```

The training output from this cell will appear like this:

| Epoch || Training Loss || Validation Loss || Accuracy || F1 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 || No log || 0.001113 || 1.000000 || 1.000000 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2 || No log || 0.000520 || 1.000000 || 1.000000 |
| 3 || No log || 0.000415 || 1.000000 || 1.000000 |

```
TrainOutput(global_step=300, training_loss=0.01779682795206706, metrics={'train_runtime': 134.3684, 'train_samples_per_second': 17.861, 'train_steps_per_second': 2.233, 'total_flos': 317921756774400.0, 'train_loss': 0.01779682795206706, 'epoch': 3.0})
```

### Evaluation

Evaluation of trained models is a necessary step in machine learning. Same is true for fine-tuned models. The process is similar, but later we will discuss some challenges specific to large language model tuning. For evaluation of above fine-tuned model, we use the evaluate method of the trainer class we just used for fine-tuning and compare with baseline.

```
# Evaluation after fine-tuning
fine_tuned_results = trainer.evaluate()
print("Results after Fine-Tuning:", fine_tuned_results)
# Compare baseline and fine-tuned results
print("Improvement in Accuracy:", fine_tuned_results['eval_accuracy'] - baseline_results['eval_accuracy'])
print("Improvement in F1 Score:", fine_tuned_results['eval_f1'] - baseline_results['eval_f1'])
```

The fine-tuning results output and difference in accuracy and F1 score between fine-tuned and baseline is output as shown below:

```
Results after Fine-Tuning: {'eval_loss': 0.00041467114351689816, 'eval_accuracy': 1.0, 'eval_f1': 1.0, 'eval_runtime': 3.3424, 'eval_samples_per_second': 59.837, 'eval_steps_per_second': 7.48, 'epoch': 3.0}
Improvement in Accuracy: 0.235
Improvement in F1 Score: 0.1331444759206799
```

Note that just looking at positive improvement, shows that fine-tuning did help, but we will look at percentage improvement to understand relative impact of fine-tuning.

```
# Calculate percentage improvement for accuracy and F1 score
accuracy_improvement = ((fine_tuned_results['eval_accuracy'] - baseline_results['eval_accuracy']) / baseline_results['eval_accuracy']) * 100
f1_improvement = ((fine_tuned_results['eval_f1'] - baseline_results['eval_f1']) / baseline_results['eval_f1']) * 100
print(f"Percentage Improvement in Accuracy: {accuracy_improvement:.2f}%")
print(f"Percentage Improvement in F1 Score: {f1_improvement:.2f}%")
```

The fine-tuned model improved accuracy by % and F1 score by %. Note that these numbers will be slightly different for randomly picked subset of data, and readers can do experiments with training parameters to improve or see further change in results.

```
Percentage Improvement in Accuracy: 30.72%
Percentage Improvement in F1 Score: 15.36%
```

An important point to remember here is that the **Accuracy** and **F1 Score** are good metrics to improve and optimize training, or understand whether the training has something obviously wrong e.g. evaluation dataset is not correct or data is unbalanced, or fine-tuning training data isn’t sufficient. Some other metrics used for NLP tasks traditionally, such as **BLEU (Bilingual Evaluation Understudy)** for measuring precision of ngrams in model output, and **ROUGE (Recall-Oriented Understudy for Gisting Evaluation)** for comparing overlaps in n-grams, word sequences and pairs, traditionally used for machine translation and other tasks. These metrics can mislead when we have to consider semantics related to positioning of words, and other methods where the actual text outputs can be evaluated by subject matter experts, or using human inspection, or by more sophisticated AI model judges can be more effective. This is subject of current research as well as emerging practice.The fine-tuning demonstration notebook is available in the GitHub repository under chapter5 as well. Having realised how fine-tuning can help, we will now discuss various ways in which RLHF could be integrated with fine-tuning.

## RLHF in Fine-Tuning

Integrating **RLHF** (**Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback**) into the fine-tuning process can improve the alignment of fine-tuned models with human expectations of how the output should be. For the above process of fine-tuning using transformers library, this would involve adapting the fine-tuning process to incorporate human feedback as additional guidance for optimization of model outputs. While the Trainer class in Hugging Face abstracts out much of the fine-tuning, RLHF would typically require more customization, especially to manage the feedback loop and apply reinforcement learning techniques instead of (or in addition to) the standard supervised fine-tuning approach we tried in the last section using a particular dataset.Here’s how RLHF could be introduced conceptually:

1.  **Feedback Collection**: First, gather human feedback on model outputs, such as ratings on a scale or binary preferences between model outputs, based on their relevance or quality. For example, you could show the model’s responses to human reviewers, asking them to rate each response's correctness or quality.
2.  **Reward Modeling**: Use this feedback to train a *reward model*, which will act as a function estimating the quality of a given output. This reward model becomes an additional tool to "evaluate" how desirable each output is, based on the learned human preferences.
3.  **Policy Optimization**: Integrate reinforcement learning techniques, like Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), to optimize the model’s policy (its behavior in generating outputs) using the reward model’s scores. During training, rather than simply minimizing cross-entropy loss, the model’s weights are adjusted to maximize the expected reward from the reward model. This could be done by creating a loop where the model generates outputs, receives feedback through the reward model, and then updates weights to favor outputs with higher predicted rewards.
4.  **Training with Trainer or Custom Loop**: Hugging Face’s Trainer class would likely need to be customized or replaced with a more flexible reinforcement learning loop. Using a customized version of Trainer or an RL-specific library like CleanRL or Stable Baselines, the training loop would involve alternating between generating outputs, evaluating them with the reward model, and backpropagating based on the received reward signal, reinforcing behavior that aligns more closely with human preferences.

Integrating RLHF with Hugging Face’s Trainer class requires significant customization, as the Trainer is primarily designed for supervised learning. However, there are dedicated frameworks and libraries that make RLHF implementations more manageable by providing tailored reinforcement learning components, including support for reward modeling and policy optimization. Here’s an overview of options, including customizations to Trainer and alternative libraries:

### Using TRLX from Hugging Face’s TRL Library

**TRL (Transformer Reinforcement Learning)** is a library developed by Hugging Face, specifically designed to facilitate RLHF for transformers. The trlX library extends Hugging Face models and integrates RL algorithms like **Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)**, discussed significantly in the last chapter, and which is commonly used in RLHF setups.

*   **Advantages**: TRLX is built specifically to handle RLHF with transformers, making it simpler to implement reward-based optimization and policy training directly on top of Hugging Face models.
*   **Features**: Includes optimized reward-based training loops, reward models, and support for popular RL algorithms like PPO. It can leverage the same Hugging Face transformers models, maintaining compatibility with pre-trained checkpoints and allowing the integration of Hugging Face datasets.

TRLX is a strong choice because it is designed for language models and other transformer-based architectures, reducing the need for custom code and enabling efficient RL training on the Hugging Face model ecosystem.

### Customizing Trainer with Reward-Driven Loss

If you want to extend Trainer for RLHF, customization is necessary to implement a feedback loop involving reward modeling and reinforcement-based optimization:**Reward Modeling**: Use human feedback data to train a separate reward model that scores outputs based on human preference alignment.

*   **Loss Adjustment**: Modify the loss function to incorporate both the supervised loss and a reward-driven component. During training, rather than simply minimizing cross-entropy loss or another supervised loss, the model’s weights are adjusted to maximize the expected reward from the reward model. This could be done by creating a loop where the model generates outputs, receives feedback through the reward model, and then updates weights to favor outputs with higher predicted rewards.

This approach is flexible but can be complex and computationally expensive. You may also need to build in additional customizations to handle trajectory generation and sampling, especially if you need stable training with PPO or another RL algorithm.

### Using Reinforcement Learning Libraries

Libraries like **CleanRL** and **Stable Baselines3** offer reinforcement learning algorithms and training utilities, which can be helpful if you need more advanced RLHF setups or want to go beyond text-based models:

*   **Integration Approach**: Combine these libraries with a Hugging Face transformer model, where the model generates output actions, and the feedback is translated into rewards processed through CleanRL’s or Stable Baselines3’s PPO implementations.
*   **Advantages**: These libraries are highly optimized for RL tasks and support stable training with algorithms like PPO, A2C, and DDPG. They also provide additional tools for monitoring and debugging RL training.
*   **Limitations**: The integration is more involved than with TRLX, as these libraries are not specifically designed for transformers or language models, so some manual configuration and API integration are needed.

### Using OpenAI’s spinningup or Custom PPO Implementation

Custom PPO implementation or using some library such as OpenAI’s spinningup requiring minimal implrmentation of PPO or other RL algorithms can provide complete control over every aspect of the RL training process, enabling thorough customization to suit RLHF needs. This requires more coding and debugging, as every step—from model rollout to reward processing to gradient updates—needs to be implemented manually.In previous chapter, we covered how modularizing and adapting CleanRL’s PPO implementation, can allow more flexibility of integration and customization, as well as provide visibility into RL components. This was good for deeper understanding, however, we were using a relatively manageable policy architecture, and incorporating a transformer architecture will add further complexity to the code base. Readers planning to develop, maintain, and evolve their own code-base can adapt and opt for this option. However, since we are relying on transformers library here,we will be using a library such as trl and trlX to help expedite learning and experimenting, and at the same time, reduce code management overhead. This will also allow keeping focus to improving the effectiveness, streamlining and optimization of the RLHF process.While we dicussed various ways of integrating RLHF with fine-tuning, in this section, we will discuss RLHF implementation more in chapter 8. In the next section we will continue discussing evaluation in fine-tuning and challenges involved in fine-tuning that necessitate implementing RLHF.

## Evaluation and Challenges in Fine-Tuning

In the previous section on fine-tuning we used accuracy score and f1 score as evaluation metrics for evaluating fine-tuning. By comparing base-line model inference results with fine-tuned model inference, we are able to compare progress gained by fine-tuned model. We will discuss in more detail evaluation metrics and methods in Chapter 11. In this section, we will discuss common challenges in evaluation of fine-tuned models, how RLHF helps address some of these challenges, and challenges that still remain to be addressed by RLHF.

*   **Metric Selection:** Choosing the right evaluation metrics is complex. Traditional metrics like accuracy and F1 score may not adequately capture the nuances of language generation, such as coherence, relevance, or creativity. For example, the Accuracy and F1 score used for sentiment scoring in previous section, can provide some insights, but they may not be sufficient for a comprehensive evaluation in complex text generation and understanding scenarios. RLHF can enhance metric selection by incorporating human feedback directly into the training process, allowing models to optimize for qualities that humans find valuable. This can lead to more nuanced metrics that better reflect real-world performance with human users.
*   **Trade-offs Between Metrics:** Sometimes optimizing one aspect of a model’s performance (e.g., fluency) can negatively impact another (e.g., factual accuracy), making it difficult to balance competing objectives. This is where RLHF can assist in finding a more favorable balance by allowing human evaluators to prioritize certain traits based on the intended use case, leading to more tailored performance adjustments.
*   **Interpretability:** Understanding why a model performs well or poorly can be challenging, complicating both the evaluation process and efforts to improve the model. RLHF can enhance interpretability by making the decision-making process more transparent, as models learn from explicit human feedback about what is considered good or bad performance.
*   **Contextual Sensitivity:** The effectiveness of a model's outputs can vary greatly depending on context, making isolated evaluations less informative. RLHF can help models become more context-aware by providing feedback that emphasizes situational relevance and appropriateness, if this type of feedback is collected.

In addition to these challenges, fine-tuned models often exhibit different performance levels across various domains (e.g., medical vs. casual conversation), complicating the evaluation of generalization. This is termed as domain-variability. RLHF can help tailor models to specific domains by utilizing human feedback relevant to those contexts, potentially improving performance and generalization within targeted areas. However, it may not fully address performance variability across diverse, unforeseen domains, as the model's training data and feedback are inherently limited to known contexts.

This chapter introduced you to language models, starting with historical evolution of language models from early models to neural network based models and then emergence of transformer based models. Then, an in-depth discussion on transformer architecture focusing on encoder architecture to help readers gain understanding of the inner workings of transformers, with some hands-on use of transformers library. This is followed by a discussion on fine-tuning language models. A discussion of fine-tuning workflow, followed by a walkthrough of an example use case using the transformers library, the datasets library, tokenization, model inferencing and fine-tuning on for reviews from a movie database, and its human provided sentiment scores for evaluation help users gain hands-on experience and realization of how fine-tuning can improve model predictions. How RLHF can be incorporated for fine-tuning is then discussed and possible viable options of using RLHF. There are some caveats and challenges to using fine-tuning evaluation metrics and where RLHF can compensate with those shortcomings also discussed here. In the next chapter we will discuss specific techniques of fine tuning related to Parameter Efficient Fine-Tuning.
