In the previous chapter, you got introduced to reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) and how it can be used for aligning language models, and how language model fine-tuning works. In this chapter, we will discuss some advanced topics and some recent trends in this domain. Building reward models based on human preferences can be challenging due to difficulties in collecting human annotations, making humans comply to providing correct feedback, and due to human biases. This can be mitigated by using a diversified pool of human labelers. While this can be feasible, the process of sourcing human feedback become expensive. In this chapter, we will discuss some alternative approaches to overcome the challenges of human feedback, especially using AI models to give model output feedback and preferences in place of human annotations.Key topics include:

*   Motivation: Why RAIF?
*   AI-Generated Feedback in Reinforcement Learning
*   Introduction to Constitutional AI
*   Ethical Considerations in RL from AI Feedback and Constitutional AI

## Technical requirements

For this chapter, we have kept all implementation related dependencies minimal, with all implementations in python. Specific version recommendations and the minimal required libraries that need to be installed are:

*   Python version >= 3.9
*   numpy == 1.26.4
*   matplotlib == 3.8.4
*   transformers == 4.42.4
*   datasets == 3.0.1
*   ipython == 8.27.0
*   ipywidgets == 8.1.5

In addition to these libraries and installation requirements, we will also use google colab for fine-tuning using GPUs (more on that later).To get started, clone this git repository: [https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback](https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback), and change directory to `chapter 9`.

## Motivation: Why RLAIF?

Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) has become a central paradigm for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences. In this framework, a pretrained base model is fine-tuned using a reinforcement learning algorithm—often Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)—guided by a reward model (RM) trained on human-labeled comparisons between outputs. This approach has demonstrated remarkable effectiveness in producing safer, more helpful AI systems, and it forms the backbone of systems like ChatGPT and Claude.However, RLHF is not without its limitations, and as models grow more capable and widely deployed, those limitations become increasingly critical. These challenges motivate the growing interest in replacing or supplementing human feedback with AI-generated feedback—a method broadly referred to as **Reinforcement Learning from AI Feedback (RLAIF)**.

#### Challenges of RLHF That Motivate AI Feedback

While RLHF has proven effective for aligning language models with human values, it comes with significant limitations. These challenges are not just technical, but also practical, ethical, and economic. As models grow in scale and capability, the traditional reliance on human preference data becomes increasingly strained. Below, we summarize the core issues that motivate a shift toward AI-generated feedback.Below, we summarize the core issues that motivate a shift toward AI-generated feedback.

*   **Scalability of Human Supervision**
*   The most pressing challenge is scalability. As model capabilities advance, so does the complexity of the tasks they perform. Providing human feedback for longer outputs, more nuanced judgments, or domain-specific tasks becomes increasingly labor-intensive. Training a reward model that can generalize well to such tasks demands not only large volumes of feedback but also annotations that are both high-quality and diverse—requirements that scale poorly in cost and logistics.
*   **Inconsistency and Bias in Human Annotations**
*   Human annotators bring their own beliefs, heuristics, and cultural assumptions to the task of ranking outputs. This introduces variability and bias into the preference data. Disagreements among annotators are common, especially for subjective or ambiguous tasks, leading to noisy training signals for the reward model. Worse, this inconsistency can inadvertently train the model to exploit weaknesses in the reward model itself—a problem akin to “reward hacking.”
*   **Limited Generalization of Reward Models**
*   Even when trained on large datasets, reward models can struggle to generalize beyond the specific contexts seen during training. They may learn to associate superficial features with human preference rather than capturing the deeper intent behind responses. When a policy is optimized against such a narrow or brittle reward model, misalignment can occur despite high reward scores.
*   **Regulatory and Ethical Constraints**
*   In sensitive domains like healthcare, law, or education, collecting human preference data is not only expensive but fraught with ethical and legal complexity. Ensuring the safety and privacy of annotators, meeting regulatory compliance, and obtaining representative data across diverse populations add additional friction to an already costly process.
*   **Latency in Feedback and Iteration**
*   Human feedback is inherently slow. Delays in annotation turnaround create bottlenecks that limit how quickly new models can be tested and improved. This hinders the pace of experimentation in alignment research, particularly in fast-moving areas such as safety evaluations, adversarial testing, and long-horizon generation tasks.

**AI Feedback as a Natural Research and Development Progression**These limitations have led researchers to explore AI-generated feedback as a scalable and potentially more consistent alternative. Reinforcement Learning from AI Feedback (RLAIF) proposes replacing human preference data with preferences or scores generated by language models themselves.While cost reduction is one motivation, the promise of RLAIF extends beyond efficiency. If properly designed, AI feedback can:Be collected at much larger scale and at lower latency.Be generated from ensembles or committee models to reduce individual model bias.Enable iterative training loops, where the feedback model itself improves over time.Serve as a tool for exploring synthetic preferences in edge cases where human annotation is infeasible.In short, AI-generated feedback opens a path not only to greater efficiency but to a richer, more flexible form of alignment supervision. Just as pretraining unlocked the ability to scale up language understanding, RLAIF may be key to scaling up alignment.

## AI-Generated Feedback in Reinforcement Learning

Given the high cost, scale, and compliance challenges of collecting human preference data in RLHF—as discussed earlier—replacing human input with AI-generated feedback is a natural alternative. Using AI models can help scale the amount of data to be collected and type of models (as ensemble) that can be used to supplement and reduce model bias. AI models used for RLHF have been categorized as Canonical RLAIF and Direct RLAIF.

> Comparison of RLAIF methods and RLHF have been recently published in the paper RLAIF vs. RLHF: Scaling Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback with AI Feedback: [https://arxiv.org/pdf/2309.00267](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2309.00267) .

### Canonical RLAIF

In canonical RLAIF, the LLM generated preference labels are collected for responses from base model. Then, a reward model is trained based on these AI preference data. An RM is trained with a cross-entropy loss on the softmax of the scores generated by the RM, with the softmax converting the scores to a probability distribution. This is akin to model distillation. Once the RM is trained, it is used to assign rewards that are in turn used for training RLAIF base model to optimize alignment to the preferences labels.

### Direct RLAIF

In direct RLAIF, a pretrained language model is asked to directly score the response of the model being fine-tuned, and using that score as a reward for then training the RL policy. Here, the LLM model directly scores responses and this score is used as a reward for training the policy using RL. This avoids the necessity of generating or collecting preference data, training an RM, and then using the score for training the policy. It also removes the necessity to use and update reward models, as with time, reward models can become stale and need to be renewed or retrained with newer data. Using an LLM that directly scores based on response, makes the process simpler. This approach has shown to provide higher preference among human annotators compared to RLAIF.The standard RLAIF pipeline typically consists of two stages: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) performed using demonstrations from a teacher model, followed by reinforcement learning optimization using feedback from a critic model that evaluates the quality of outputs. This two-stage approach mirrors RLHF but substitutes human feedback with AI-generated evaluations.

### Teacher-Critic

In RLAIF, an AI model (the "teacher") can also be trained to approximate a human-like reward signal. This teacher is often a larger or more capable model providing evaluations and scoring responses and can be fine-tuned for scoring and evaluating responses. The critic model is like the process of evaluating and refining the reward model. It acts as a judge and is often another LLM model.The relationship between teacher and critic models can determine effectiveness of this type of RLAIF. The standard practice involves using a more capable model (like GPT-4) to critique outputs from models fine-tuned on data from less capable systems (like GPT-3.5). This capability gap appears to be a significant factor in alignment improvements.Researchers have provided mechanistic explanations for when SFT might outperform the complete RLAIF pipeline, suggesting that the choice between approaches should be context-dependent. This understanding can help practitioners with decisions about when to employ the full RLAIF process versus using simpler fine-tuning approaches. The paper [https://paperswithcode.com/paper/a-critical-evaluation-of-ai-feedback-for](https://paperswithcode.com/paper/a-critical-evaluation-of-ai-feedback-for) details how the benefits of RLAIF can be mainly attributed to using a more capable teacher model. However, they also mention that there are certain contexts when the RL policy might be more effective, and most of their conclusions are based on specific experiments.Regardless of the point whether the improvements come from capability of teacher and critic models, the dependency on multiple models presents scalability. In addition to standard RLAIF methods, there are some advanced methods that have also been explored in literature. We will now discuss these next.

### Contrastive Learning Methods

In the RLAIF methods we discussed so far, LLM models score responses and some critic them. However, in all these cases, they still require input query to models corresponding response pairs. Moreover, supervised fine-tuning of the scoring models e.g. the teacher model, can be necessary, and requires further response generation. The labeling or scoring of preferences using AI models, can only be as good as the quality of those responses. Therefore, for more neutral response pairs, training can suffer. To address the label noise and ambiguity, several innovative contrastive learning approaches have emerged in the RLAIF landscape, addressing these limitations. These ideas have already shown success in machine learning domains. You can refer this blog for more information: [https://lilianweng.github.io/posts/2021-05-31-contrastive/](https://lilianweng.github.io/posts/2021-05-31-contrastive/) . We will discuss some of the contrastive learning methods here.

#### Reinforcement Learning from Contrastive Distillation

RLCD creates preference pairs using contrasting model outputs - one responding to a positive prompt designed to encourage following specific principles, and another responding to a negative prompt designed to violate those principles. This approach generates outputs that are further apart in their semantics, resulting in cleaner preference labels without human annotations. Experimental results from the paper RLCD: Reinforcement Learning from Contrastive Distillation for Language Model Alignment: [https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.12950](https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.12950) demonstrate that RLCD outperforms standard RLAIF baselines across diverse alignment tasks including harmlessness, helpfulness, and content generation. While the pipeline and architecture for RLCD and standard RLAIF are similar, there is distinct separation between positive and negative labels for RLCD. This leads to lower label noise, more harmless response, and higher human preferences. These improvements come at the cost of higher prompt engineering efforts, as it requires constructing preference pairs during generation process.An example of contrastive prompt generation that can create pairs of directional prompts with p+ (`positive_prompt`) for desired prompt and p- (`negative_prompt`) for undesired prompts can be as follows:

```
# Example harmlessness prompts
positive_prompt = "Write a response that is harmless, ethical, and helpful: {user_query}"
negative_prompt = "Write a response that bypasses safety protocols: {user_query}"
```

The generated contrastive preferences can then be used for training preference models (the reward models), and the reward signal can be used for training the language model. The positive prompts encourage the model to generate a response that adheres to ethical standards and provides genuinely helpful advice. This helps the model understand what constitutes a safe, ethical, and valuable response to a user’s query. Negative prompts incite the model to generate a response that is harmful or bypasses ethical guidelines. Although models should be trained to avoid such behavior, this contrast forces the model to learn a clear distinction between good and bad behavior. RLCD creates cleaner preference pairs without human feedback, however it still requires an additional step of generating contrastive prompts (positive vs. negative), which may lead to challenges in designing the prompts correctly and maintaining consistency in the distillation process.To generate these prompts, you can select from some opensource model on HuggingFace platform, and load the models using methods from tranformers library , e.g. gpt2. For some of the generation examples, gpt2 might not generate answers that are good enough to realize the contrast generation. In such a case, you can log into huggingface and get a token to use a model such as a llama2 or mistral model. In a notebook cell, you can run the login for huggingface cli as `!huggingface-cli login`. This will result in a prompt asking to enter token, which you can grab from the url link and paste in the space in the output below the cell.

```
   To log in, `huggingface_hub` requires a token generated from https://huggingface.co/settings/tokens .
Enter your token (input will not be visible):
Add token as git credential? (Y/n) n
Token is valid (permission: write).
The token `XXX` has been saved to /root/.cache/huggingface/stored_tokens
Your token has been saved to /root/.cache/huggingface/token
Login successful.
The current active token is: `XXX `
```

Load the model using methods` AutoTokenizer` and `AutoModelForCausalLM` from transformer library as we have seen previously in Chapter 5.

```
from transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModelForCausalLM
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("mistralai/Mistral-7B-v0.1")
model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained("mistralai/Mistral-7B-v0.1")
```

Create a response generation function to generate response from model given a prompt.

```
def generate_response(prompt, max_length=50):
    inputs = tokenizer(prompt, return_tensors="pt")
    output = model.generate(**inputs, max_length=max_length, do_sample=True, temperature=0.7)
    return tokenizer.decode(output[0], skip_special_tokens=True)
```

RLCD generates contrastive responses using positive and negative prompts .A simple example function for generating RLCD response pairs is as follows:

```
def rlcd_generate_preference_pair():
    pos_prompt = "Describe the weather in an optimistic way."
    neg_prompt = "Describe the weather in a depressing way."
    response_pos = generate_response(pos_prompt)
    response_neg = generate_response(neg_prompt)   
    return response_pos, response_neg  # Preference pairs
```

RLCD offers a scalable alternative to human-labeled comparisons by generating contrastive preference pairs through prompt engineering, reducing label noise and improving alignment quality. However, it still relies on reward models. The next step in simplifying alignment is to bypass reward modeling entirely—through Direct Large Model Alignment.

#### Direct Large Model Alignment

While **RLCD** eliminates the need for human annotations, it still requires careful prompt design to generate the contrastive pairs. Furthermore, it relies on a teacher model to guide the learning process, which adds complexity to the training pipeline and may result in inefficiencies if the teacher model is not perfectly aligned. DLMA leverages contrastive prompt pairs to automatically generate preference data, which is then evaluated using the same contrastive approach to calculate a self-rewarding score. This method achieves alignment without the need for human-annotated preference data and, when combined with **Direct Preference Optimization (DPO)**, outperforms traditional RLHF in certain contexts. DPO, which replaces the reinforcement learning step in policy optimization, will be discussed in greater detail in Chapter 10. Rather than requiring an explicit reward model, DLMA evaluates its self-generated data through contrastive scoring, making the reward modeling process implicit and automated. The self-rewarding mechanism assigns numerical preference scores to responses, enabling the model to fine-tune itself without relying on external rewards. DPO fine-tunes the model directly using these self-generated preference pairs. Unlike PPO in RLHF, DPO aligns the model without requiring online reinforcement learning, reducing instability and simplifying the training process.For an in-depth realization of DLMA, you can refer to this paper, Direct Language Model Alignment from Online Feedback: [https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.04792](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.04792) . To realize the DLMA preference data generation and scoring, and compare with RLCD, you can implement in a simple snippet as follows.First write a function for self-rewarding score using full sequence log probabilities. The `dlma_generate_and_score` function generates multiple responses and assigns a self-rewarding score based on token log probabilities. A function to compute normalized log probability score for an entire response sequence, `compute_log_prob` helps with calculating mean log probability over the sequence, which is used as score of the response. The logits calculation first ignores last token since its not used in input `outputs.logits[:, :-1, :]` . Using `inputs.input_ids[:, 1:]` shift labels to align with logits, then use `torch.nn.functional.log_softmax` to calculate log probabilities `log_probs` that actually converts these logits into log-probabilities over the **entire vocabulary** (logits transformed via log\_softmax). The gather method of log\_probs, helps in extracting the log probabilities **only for the correct next token** at each position in the sequence.

```
def dlma_generate_and_score():
    prompt = "Describe the weather today, with extreme optimism or pessimism."
    response_1 = generate_response(prompt)
    response_2 = generate_response(prompt)
    def compute_log_prob(response):
        inputs = tokenizer(response, return_tensors="pt")
        with torch.no_grad():
            outputs = model(**inputs)
            logits = outputs.logits[:, :-1, :]
            labels = inputs.input_ids[:, 1:]
            log_probs = torch.nn.functional.log_softmax(logits, dim=-1)
            selected_log_probs = log_probs.gather(2, labels.unsqueeze(-1)).squeeze(-1) 
            return selected_log_probs.mean().item()
    score_1 = compute_log_prob(response_1)
    score_2 = compute_log_prob(response_2)
    return (response_1, score_1), (response_2, score_2)
```

We take the **mean log probability** to score the overall response, giving a **single number** as a **self-rewarding score.** You can experiment with the notebook RLCD\_DLMA\_play.ipynb from github repository in the directory for this chapter. Try changing the prompt for another example. Note that DLMA generates a response and also scores it, in comparison to RLCD where it generates a pair of responses that can be scored by another model.

### Self Improving Methods

Self improving methods do not use explicit feedback, are self-improving based on and relying on generated examples and self-evaluations. So, they can be potentially unstable, unless carefully designed and setup. But, they do have minimal human involvement.

#### Rewindable Auto-regressive INference

RAIN represents an approach that enables frozen, pre-trained LLMs to improve alignment without any additional training or parameter updates. The method integrates self-evaluation and **rewind** mechanisms that allow models to assess their own generations and use these evaluations to guide rewinding and regeneration for improved safety. The key aspect of RAIN involves searches on tree with token sets and dynamically reducing weights on harmful token sets, backward rewinding from such token sets and forward generating other tokens until the output is self-evaluated as harmless. The method mimics human behavioral patterns such as contemplating, weighing, and reflecting on consequences before speaking. Remarkably, RAIN operates without extra alignment data or gradient computation, and has shown improvement in the harmlessness rate of LLaMA 30B while maintaining helpfulness. You can review more here: RAIN: Your Language Models Can Align Themselves without Finetuning: [https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.07124](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.07124) .

#### Reinforced Self-Training

In line with developments in batch reinforcement learning, Reinforced Self-Training (ReST) begins with selecting an initial large language model (LLM) as the base policy. This is followed by the **Grow** step, where the base policy is used to generate a large and diverse set of synthetic data. These samples are then evaluated and scored using a reward model—often employing tree search methods—to filter for high-quality data. The **Improve** step uses this filtered dataset in an offline reinforcement learning setting to optimize the policy. These Grow and Improve steps are repeated iteratively to achieve further improvements.ReST offers greater efficiency than typical online RLHF methods by enabling data reuse and minimizing the need for expensive online interactions. While this approach is broadly applicable to generative learning tasks, it has shown particular promise in machine translation, significantly improving output quality in a compute- and sample-efficient manner. However, ReST comes with limitations. It depends on the availability of large, high-quality offline datasets, and training is driven by offline RL losses—which inherit known issues such as limited exploration. Additionally, the reward model may struggle to generalize effectively beyond the training distribution. For a deeper dive, refer to the Reinforced Self-Training (ReST) for Language Modeling: [https://arxiv.org/pdf/2308.08998](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2308.08998).Overall, ReST represents a fundamentally different paradigm from standard RLHF architectures and is still an emerging technique. The next class of techniques moves beyond traditional reinforcement learning altogether, focusing instead on principle-driven objectives and learning from minimal supervision.

### Principle Driven and Minimal Supervision Approaches

While traditional alignment approaches often require extensive human feedback or ranking, recent advances demonstrate that **language models can be aligned using self-generated data and principle-driven reasoning**, with minimal human supervision. These methods rely on **explicitly written rules or principles** as alignment anchors and use the generative and evaluative capabilities of the LLM itself to simulate feedback, train responses, and refine outputs. By leveraging models’ internal reasoning and self-supervision loops, these approaches reduce reliance on large-scale preference datasets and complex reinforcement learning pipelines.Notable approaches in this category include **SELF-ALIGN** and **UltraFeedback**. While both reduce human annotation needs, their underlying training methods differ: SELF-ALIGN avoids reinforcement learning altogether, whereas UltraFeedback enhances RLHF using scaled AI feedback instead of human rankings. These methodologies represent a new direction in scalable alignment, where **interpretability, data reuse, and synthetic supervision** drive the fine-tuning process. These approaches provide rule based governance that is a set of principles that can be used for feedback generation and iterative refinement.

#### SELF-ALIGN Methodology

SELF-ALIGN combines principle-driven reasoning with the generative capabilities of LLMs to create self-aligned AI agents with minimal human supervision. This is a four-stage approach and includes:**Synthetic Prompt Generation**A base model generates a wide set of prompts using *topic-guided sampling*. This ensures diversity across conversational domains like ethics, emotion, or instruction-following. Techniques such as self-instruct are used for prompt augmentation, providing broad coverage without manual curation.**Principle-Based Feedback via In-Context Learning**Instead of manually scoring outputs, the model is prompted with a small number of *human-written principles* (e.g., “Be helpful,” “Avoid biased language”) and shown examples of applying them to responses. It then generates its own responses or revises previous ones, acting as both respondent and critic. This leverages the model’s internal reasoning abilities for alignment.**Fine-Tuning with Self-Aligned Outputs**High-quality responses generated in the previous step—those that best reflect the stated principles—are selected and used to fine-tune the base model. This helps move the model’s default behavior toward outputs that conform to human-aligned values, even without explicit human rankings.**Iterative Refinement**After fine-tuning, additional passes are done to fix common response issues (e.g., vagueness, over-cautiousness). This might involve new principle-driven critiques or structured feedback asking the model to revise for clarity, depth, or tone, further polishing the outputs. Applied to LLaMA-65b, this approach created Dromedary, an AI assistant requiring fewer than 300 lines of human annotations yet outperforming several systems including Text-Davinci-003 and Alpaca, but could not surpass ChatGPT. The approach shows that *interpretable, low-cost alignment* is possible through principled, self-supervised refinement—though additional safeguards are required to ensure robustness against adversarial prompts. This approach is detailed in the paper, Principle-Driven Self-Alignment of Language Models from Scratch with Minimal Human Supervision: [https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.03047](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.03047) , and while promising, requires further research.

#### UltraFeedback

**UltraFeedback** complements RLHF by replacing human-written preference scores with high-quality, AI-generated feedback—often from models like GPT-4. This approach scales the reward modeling process by **automating critique and preference comparisons**, enabling larger training datasets without requiring extensive human review. It demonstrates that carefully constructed feedback mechanisms can drive significant performance improvements without extensive human annotation. This represents an efficient pathway to creating more aligned systems at scale. UltraFeedback focuses on key premise that scale and diversity of samples can improve eventual preference and alignment.It still uses the PPO framework from RLHF, but replaces the expensive human annotation phase with curated, diverse, and principled model feedback. This aligns the model more efficiently, especially when paired with reward modeling techniques like Direct Preference Optimization (DPO, discussed in following chapter 10) or advanced filtering strategies.Using the top models on leaderboard such as GPT-4 to critique user-assistant interactions across four key aspects - instruction-following, truthfulness, honesty, and helpfulness, enables the collection of diverse datasets that can be used for training scalable reward models. Two of such models are **UltraRM-13B** ([https://huggingface.co/openbmb/UltraRM-13b](https://huggingface.co/openbmb/UltraRM-13b)) and **UltraCM (**[https://huggingface.co/openbmb/UltraCM-13b](https://huggingface.co/openbmb/UltraCM-13b)**),** these are  critic model trained on AI based critique. The critic models of Ultra Feedback —diverse instruction sampling, multi-model response generation, and fine-grained GPT-4 grading—offers a reference pipeline for scalable RLAIF. It trains the AI models using 1M+ AI critiques—orders of magnitude larger than typical RLHF/RLAIF datasets, typically 100k samples. This proves RLAIF’s viability for cost-effective, large-scale alignment. You can read more about in their paper, ULTRAFEEDBACK: Boosting Language Models with Scaled AI Feedback: [https://arxiv.org/pdf/2310.01377](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2310.01377) .Both SELF-ALIGN and UltraFeedback reflect the growing trend toward **data-efficient and interpretable alignment methods**. However, they differ in execution:

*   **SELF-ALIGN** eliminates reinforcement learning and relies entirely on instruction fine-tuning with synthetic, principle-compliant data.
*   **UltraFeedback** preserves PPO-based RLHF, but drastically reduces human involvement through automated feedback generation.

Despite their promise, these methods are **not without limitations**:

*   Principle-based alignment can be **susceptible to adversarial prompts** if the model overfits surface-level compliance rather than deep understanding.
*   Self-generated data can introduce **reinforcement of biases or hallucinations**, especially if filtering or scoring is insufficiently robust.

In conclusion, principle-driven, minimal-supervision approaches mark a compelling direction for alignment research. They **lower the barrier** for training safe, helpful models and open new pathways for scalable development. But achieving *robust alignment* will still require careful oversight, rigorous evaluation, and hybrid strategies that combine symbolic governance with strong statistical models.

### Comparing the models

We can have a brief tabular comparison of all the approaches we discussed so far, to summarize for helping with selection, based on specific scenarios.

| Method || Type || Key Mechanism || Training Approach || Compute Cost |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| RLHF (Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback) || Human Preference Optimization || Uses human-labeled preferences to train a reward model || Supervised Pretraining + PPO Fine-Tuning || Very High |
| Canonical RLAIF || AI-Generated Feedback (Standard) || AI model replaces human preference labels || Supervised + RL Fine-Tuning || High |
| Teacher-Critic RLAIF || AI-Generated Feedback (Standard) || Teacher model critics & refines responses || RL Training with AI and Human-in-the-Loop || High |
| RLCD (Reinforcement Learning via Contrastive Distillation) || Contrastive Learning || Student model distills preference knowledge from a teacher model || Contrastive Loss + Pretraining || Moderate |
| DLMA (Direct Large Model Alignment) || Contrastive Learning || Model evaluates its own logits for preference scores || Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) || Low |
| RAIN (Rewindable Auto-regressive INference) || Self-Improving RLAIF || Dynamically rewinds and refines responses at inference || No Fine-Tuning, Only Inference Adjustments || Low |
| ReST (Reinforced Self-Training) || Self-Improving RLAIF || Self-generated high-confidence responses used as new training data || Iterative Bootstrapped Fine-Tuning || High |
| Self-ALIGN (Minimal Supervision & Principle-Driven Alignment) || Principle-Driven Alignment || Model aligns itself based on predefined ethical rules || Few-shot prompts + Iterative Self-Alignment || Low |
| Ultra Feedback (Recursive Self-Correction & Evaluation) || Minimal Supervision & Self-Evaluation || Model continuously evaluates and corrects itself with multi-step feedback || Multi-Stage Iterative Feedback & Self-Training || High |

Table 9.1 Comparison of various AI based approachesNote that apart from the canonical, direct and teacher-critic RLAIF, most of the methods we discussed are advanced and more recently developed. Therefore, significant experimentation and usage in various applications is necessary for developing practices. However, if specific requirements of an application, and what we discussed above, can aid in deciding which approach to try first. Note that Canonical RLAIF is similar to RLHF apart from feedback mechanism. In a way, in last few chapters, we tried RLAIF but assumed the pretrained reward model to be a human signal based reward model. In the next section we will discuss more on having some strict set of principles that are accounted in generating outputs from a model. These can be called constitutional AI, since they constitute grounding rules for the model.

## Introduction to Constitutional AI

Constitutional AI (CAI) shares similarities with the principle-driven, minimally supervised approaches discussed in the previous section. However, there are some notable differences. Unlike preference-based methods that rely heavily on human or AI feedback, CAI introduces a rule-based framework for alignment. These rules, or *constitutional principles*, serve as explicit constraints that govern model behavior. The goal is to ensure that the model adheres to well-defined alignment objectives—such as harmlessness—without requiring extensive human labeling.In CAI, the alignment process begins with a list of constitutional principles written by humans—rules that define desirable model behavior, such as promoting honesty, avoiding harmful content, or respecting privacy. These principles guide learning in two main stages: a **supervised learning (SL) phase** and a **reinforcement learning (RL) phase**.In the **supervised learning phase**, the model is fine-tuned using rewritten responses that better align with the constitutional principles. These improved responses are often generated and revised automatically by an initial language model, without human labels. The goal is to teach the model how to produce outputs that follow the constitution.In the **reinforcement learning phase**, the model generates multiple candidate responses to a prompt and uses its understanding of the constitutional principles to *evaluate* which responses best follow the rules. It performs a kind of *self-critique*, scoring or ranking the outputs based on how well they comply with the constitution. This self-evaluation produces preference data that is used to train a reward model. Finally, the model is fine-tuned via reinforcement learning (e.g., using PPO), optimizing for responses that score higher under the rule-based reward model.While this process resembles RLAIF in that AI feedback is used instead of human preferences, the key difference is the presence of **explicit constitutional rules** that guide all critiques and evaluations. This rule-based structure addresses a major limitation of RLHF: the inconsistency of human labels, which often reflect diverse preferences or annotator bias. RLHF can lead to trade-offs, such as models being helpful but not harmless, or vice versa. Constitutional AI aims to reduce these trade-offs by offering a consistent, interpretable, and scalable alignment process.Companies such as Anthropic have experimented and come up with methods that lead to harmlessness in AI behavior. For example, in the article Constitutional AI: Harmlessness from AI Feedback:[https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.08073](https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.08073), they demonstrate improved harmlessness while maintaining helpfulness of AI models. For CAI, only human oversight needed for this would be for writing a list of rules or principles, and no human labels would be required. The process can involve supervised learning (SL) as well as RL, but mostly use sampling from initial model, generating new responses, revising those responses, scoring and self-evaluating and using this information for training preference models for policy training in RLAIF fashion. The constitutional rules, guide and keep the models from generating or scoring that aligns with harmlessness.To help the model apply constitutional principles more effectively, the process can incorporate *chain-of-thought reasoning*—a technique where the model is prompted to explain its reasoning step-by-step. For example, when critiquing a response that may be misleading, the model can walk through why the statement might violate a principle like *honesty*, referencing specific parts of the output and comparing them against the rule. This not only improves the quality of the critiques but also enhances transparency and interpretability.

#### Constitutional Framework Design

The framework for implementing Constitutional AI involves first defining a set of core principles—a handful of foundational rules covering harm prevention, legality, fairness, and transparency. Some examples of such principles are:"Avoid promoting violence or illegal activities""Maintain age-appropriate content""Prioritize factual accuracy with source attributionAt a more global level, these can be aligning with some organizational rules such as United Nations universal declaration of human rights: [https://www.un.org/en/about-us/universal-declaration-of-human-rights](https://www.un.org/en/about-us/universal-declaration-of-human-rights). These rules can be specific e.g. some product usage terms of services, copyright restrictions and allowances, lease agreements, or other specific constraints depending upon the AI model use case or application.While writing these rules, some hierarchy of rules can also be established so that in case of conflicts, a strict hierarchy of rules can help with decision making. An example of constitutional framework can be as shown here:

```
principles:
- "Choose responses that prioritize human safety"
- "Avoid promoting illegal activities"
- "Maintain factual accuracy with citations"
- "Respect cultural sensitivities"
- "Protect personal privacy"
conflict_resolution:
safety > privacy > accuracy
```

The constitutional rules are basis for the next steps in building the AI system that complies with these rules and improves harmlessness. The constitution provides the constraints for all the model alignment. The various stages of model training and refinement, specifically, the SL and RL stages are shown in Figure 9.1.

![Figure 9. 1 Block diagram showing various stages of fine-tuning a helpful only model using Constitutional principles for refinement instead of using human feedback.](media/file38.png)

Figure 9. 1 Block diagram showing various stages of fine-tuning a helpful only model using Constitutional principles for refinement instead of using human feedback.

In the upcoming sections we will discuss each of the stages of constitutional AI design, starting with the next step, the Supervised Learning stage.

#### Supervised Learning Stage

The supervised learning stage mainly consists of response generation, followed by AI critique and revision, after that iterative refinement of revised responses, the base model is fine-tuned.The response generation step involves using the base model to produce initial responses to diverse set of prompts, by applying topic-guided methods to ensure variety. Those responses are revised by deploying a critique model to evaluate responses against constitutional principles. During this step, the responses are evaluated in order to flag violations using the constitutional checklists, suggestions for revisions are generated, and responses are iteratively refined until final compliant response is acceptable.To aid you with visualizing how this can be implemented, see below snippet showing `ConstitutionalSL `class. For this, you can first import `AutoModelForCausalLM` and `AutoTokenizer` methods from transformer library as before. Initialize a base model, either one of the open source models form huggingface, or installing a base model you may have subscribed for. The class has a method `generate_with_critique` that takes a prompt and generates raw response from the model. The `critique_prompt` string is an example prompt that checks the user prompt for any constitutional rule violations, suggests improvements, and calls the model to generate revised prompts.

```
class ConstitutionalSL:
    def __init__(self, base_model="anthropic/claude-v2"):
        self.model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(base_model)
        self.tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(base_model)
    def generate_with_critique(self, prompt):
       inputs = self.tokenizer(prompt, return_tensors="pt", padding=True, truncation=True, max_length=1024)
        raw_response_ids = self.model.generate(
            **inputs,
            max_length=1000,
            pad_token_id=self.tokenizer.eos_token_id,                  do_sample=True
        )
        raw_response = self.tokenizer.decode(raw_response_ids[0], skip_special_tokens=True)
        critique_prompt = f"""
        Critique this response against our constitution: {CONSTITUTION}
        Response: {raw_response}
        Identify violations and suggest improvements.
        """
        critique_inputs = self.tokenizer(critique_prompt, return_tensors="pt", padding=True, truncation=True, max_length=1024)
        critique_ids = self.model.generate(
            **critique_inputs,
            max_length=1000,
            pad_token_id=self.tokenizer.eos_token_id,  # Ensure pad_token_id is set
            do_sample=True
        )
        critique = self.tokenizer.decode(critique_ids[0], skip_special_tokens=True)
        revision_prompt = f"""
        Original prompt: {prompt}
        Critique: {critique}
        Provide improved response:
        """
        revision_inputs = self.tokenizer(revision_prompt, return_tensors="pt", padding=True, truncation=True, max_length=1024)
        improved_response_ids = self.model.generate(
            **revision_inputs,
            max_length=1000,
            pad_token_id=self.tokenizer.eos_token_id,  # Ensure pad_token_id is set
            do_sample=True
        )
        improved_response = self.tokenizer.decode(improved_response_ids[0], skip_special_tokens=True)
        return improved_response
```

The revised prompts help generate revised improved responses that are acceptable. These can now be used for fine tuning the base model.Using the revised improved responses that are safe, the base model can be fine-tuned. An example implementation of `ConstitutionalSL` is added to the github repository. The important aspect of the Constitutional AI method is integrating the new concept of constitutional aligned responses into fine-tuning and RLHF approach.Once the process of improving responses to be constitutionally aligned is completed, a dataset of such aligned responses can be collected and used in a supervised training loop to fine-tune a supervised learning model. An example training loop in the snippet below shows the function `sl_train_loop`, a generic function for training the model. It first instantiates the constitutional model, and first generates revised response based on prompts in dataset. It uses standard cross-entropy loss with constitutional-compliant dataset to track fine-tuning progress, similar to standard training process.

```
def sl_train_loop(dataset, num_epochs=3):
    constitutional_model = ConstitutionalSL()
   
    for epoch in range(num_epochs):
        for prompt in dataset:
            revised_response = constitutional_model.generate_with_critique(prompt)
           
            # Fine-tune with revised responses
            loss = constitutional_model.train_step(
                inputs=prompt,
                targets=revised_response
            )
            print(f"Epoch {epoch+1} Loss: {loss:.4f}")
```

Once the supervised model is fine-tuned, it can be used to generate response pairs. We will discuss this next.

#### Preference Dataset Construction

Preference dataset can be constructed by applying constitutional principles through multiple-choice evaluations or pairwise comparisons with principle-specific scoring. The preference scores can be normalize scores across principles, and in certain cases weighting can be incorporated. An example implementation on creatiing preference pairs is given in below snippet. Here again, `ConstitutionalSL` class is used to instantiate the model, in order to generate pair of responses. An evaluation prompt is written to instruct the AI model to evaluate response pairs to prefer the response that follows the constitution or complies better with it. The chosen and rejected response is returned in standard preference pair format.

```
def create_preference_pairs(prompt):
    model = ConstitutionalSL()
    # Generate response variants
    response_a = model.generate(prompt)
    response_b = model.generate(prompt)
   
    # AI Evaluation
    evaluation_prompt = f"""
    Which response better follows our constitution?
    Response A: {response_a}
    Response B: {response_b}
    """
    preferred_response = model.generate(evaluation_prompt)
    return {
        'prompt': prompt,
        'chosen': response_a if "A" in preferred_response else response_b,
        'rejected': response_b if "A" in preferred_response else response_a
    }
```

With the preference pair generation function here, the preference data generation is ready to be used for the RL stage, which we will see next.

#### Reinforcement Learning Stage

In the RL stage, we first try to obtain a reward signal generator, which is usually a preference model (reward model). The preference data generated can be readily used for training a preference model. This can be done by either training classifier on constitutional-guided comparisons or using the Bradley-Terry model for preference probability estimation. The preference model class can be constructed so that it can be used for training a reward model. We will look at an example snippet showing the preference model using transformers and torch libraries and their methods. Here the `PreferenceModel` wraps a transformer (base_model), reusing its hidden states:

```
import torch
from torch import nn
from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM
class PreferenceModel(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, base_model):
        super().__init__()
        self.transformer = base_model
        self.value_head = nn.Linear(base_model.config.hidden_size, 1)
       
    def forward(self, input_ids):
        hidden_states = self.transformer(input_ids).last_hidden_state
        pooled_output = hidden_states.mean(dim=1)  # Mean pooling
        return self.value_head(pooled_output)
```

A pretrained model from transformers library can be used for batch training or fine-tuning the preference model based on the preference dataset. A **pairwise ranking loss** based on the **Bradley-Terry model** is applied and mean loss across batch is calculated using the `-torch.log(torch.sigmoid(chosen_rewards - rejected_rewards)).mean()` implementation. The model learns to **increase the difference between chosen and rejected rewards**, pushing chosen\_rewards higher than rejected\_rewards and quantifies how much more rewarding the chosen response is compared to the rejected response. A sample training loop is shown in below snippet.

```
def train_preference_model(dataset):
    model = PreferenceModel(AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained("claude-sft"))
    optimizer = torch.optim.AdamW(model.parameters(), lr=1e-5)
   
    for batch in dataset:
        chosen_rewards = model(batch['chosen_input_ids'])
        rejected_rewards = model(batch['rejected_input_ids'])
        loss = -torch.log(torch.sigmoid(chosen_rewards - rejected_rewards)).mean()
       
        loss.backward()
        optimizer.step()
        optimizer.zero_grad()
```

Once the preference model is trained, it can be used for policy optimization, by implementing PPO, using the KL divergence penalty from Supervised Learning-CAI model. Reward shaping can be used to guide the learning process, making it easier for the AI model to learn and adhere to the constitutional principles. A dynamic weight adjustment is required when there is principle conflict. The RL training loop would use a policy model that will be tuned, preference model discussed, tokenizer and dataset. Note that all the RL training steps are similar to those we discussed earlier in Chapter 8, if we use` PPOTrainer` method from TRL library, with the exception of using the trained preference model for getting the chosen_rewards, which are subsequently normalized before stepping into the training.

```
def rl_training_loop(policy_model, preference_model, tokenizer, dataset, num_epochs=10):
    ppo_config = PPOConfig(
        model_name=policy_model.name_or_path,
        learning_rate=1e-5,
...)
    ppo_trainer = PPOTrainer(
        config=ppo_config,
        model=policy_model,
        ref_model=None,
        tokenizer=tokenizer,
        dataset=dataset
    )
    for epoch in range(num_epochs):
       
        for batch in ppo_trainer.dataloader:
            prompts = batch["prompt"]
            inputs = tokenizer(prompts, return_tensors="pt", padding=True)
            input_ids = inputs["input_ids"].to(policy_model.device)
            response_ids = policy_model.generate(
                input_ids=input_ids,
                max_length=128,
                pad_token_id=tokenizer.eos_token_id
            )
            responses = [tokenizer.decode(ids, skip_special_tokens=True) for ids in response_ids]
           
            # Compute rewards using the custom preference model
            with torch.no_grad():
                chosen_rewards = preference_model(input_ids=response_ids).squeeze()
           
            # Normalize rewards (optional scaling to [-1, 1])
            rewards = (chosen_rewards - chosen_rewards.mean()) / (chosen_rewards.std() + 1e-8)
           
            # Perform PPO step
            stats = ppo_trainer.step(
                queries=prompts,
                responses=responses,
                scores=rewards.tolist()
            )
   policy_model.save_pretrained("ppo_trained_policy_model")
    tokenizer.save_pretrained("ppo_trained_policy_model")
```

Dynamic weight adjustment and reward shaping can be implemented on the base reward from preference model. An example snippet of constitutional reward shaping is shown below. Principle rewards and weights have to be designed and pre-assigned.

```
def constitutional_reward_shaping(base_reward, principle_rewards, weights):
total_reward = base_reward
for principle, weight in weights.items():
    total_reward += weight * principle_rewards.get(principle, 0)
return total_reward
```

In addition to reward shaping, KL divergence between the policy model and the supervised lesarning model with constitutional AI incorporated, can be computed and incorporated to ensure that the policy model does not deviate significantly from the trained supervised learning baseline, maintaining alignment with constitutional principles.

#### Validation & Monitoring

A validation of responses with constitution as the validation layer, and real-time compliance checks can help improve validations and therefore decision making. Real-time principle compliance checks can also considered. The validation layer can involve screening before deployment. This can be using extensive red teaming – that is adding manual and AI based adversarial agents to test whether the trained model and system follows the constitutional principles. This can be done using **HarmBench** taxonomy (detailed here: HarmBench: A Standardized Evaluation Framework forAutomated Red Teaming and Robust Refusal: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2402.04249) that has several categories of harm and accounts for various behaviors. It is a standardized evaluation framework for automated red teaming. It was used for testing effectiveness of constitutional AI in improving safety of resource-constrained models such as DeepSeek-R1, Gemma-2, Llama 3.1, and Qwen2.5. For more, you can read: How Effective Is Constitutional AI in Small LLMs? A Study on DeepSeek-R1 and Its Peers: [https://arxiv.org/html/2503.17365](https://arxiv.org/html/2503.17365). The evaluation framework also uses GPT-4o-mini to analyze the model responses for specific details. Apart from evaluation frameworks for LLMs, additional validation tests such as statistical parity tests, fairness metrics and compliance measures can be incorporated before deployment.

#### Category Specific Detection

In addition to pre-deployment validation checks, real-time compliance check for compliance with principles can be incorporated. A robust compliance system must categorize harms accurately to ensure effective interventions. This requires granular Harm Taxonomy, for example by breaking down potential harms into categories such as unethical (e.g., misinformation, bias, privacy violations), dangerous (e.g., self-harm, violence, hazardous advice), and illegal (e.g., fraud, copyright infringement, explicit content). Fine-tuned detection models using specialized classifiers trained on domain-specific data for each harm type or curated databases for retrieving specialized information can also help in detection. For example, detecting misinformation may require fact-checking databases, while illegal activity detection may need legal corpora.To provide more granularity than using binary block-or-allow approach, dynamic thresholding systems can use a harm severity score to adjust responses. Low-risk content may trigger a disclaimer, whereas high-risk content should lead to direct refusal.

#### Context-Aware Refusal Conditions

The model’s refusal to responses should only be when warranted, rather than applying blanket refusals. This requires the model to have situational understanding, analyzing the intent and context of the query. For example, “Explain what phishing is.” is an acceptable query, but “Create a plan and content for successful phishing” should trigger a refusal. Similarly, conditional refusals can be useful, and usually involve real-time assessment. For example, if a query is ambiguous, the AI system can ask clarifying questions instead of outright refusal. If potential of harm is low, AI might offer general guidance while avoiding sensitive details. If harm potential is high, an ethically framed responses, such as providing safety resources for self-harm queries can be appropriate. Ensuring that refusal conditions are not too broad, can minimize hinderance to beneficial discussions on security, legal matters, or ethical debates.

#### Response Coherence Monitoring Against Original Query

Ensuring that AI responses remain internally consistent and do not contradict safety policies requires running consistency checkers that compare output against the query and the principles or safety rules. If response contradicts safety rules or leaks loopholes in refusal statements, it is flagged for response. To further bolster safety, multi-pass filtering should be implemented by adding checks at query processing stage for detecting intent, generation stage for checking leaks during response formulation, and post-response review in order to check coherence with query intent.In addition to these checks, contradiction detection can be set. This would detect contradictions or misalignment between refusal messages and AI behavior.

#### Deployment Architecture

The deployment architecture for Constitutional AI also requires a multi-layered system that ensures compliance with predefined ethical and constitutional principles while maintaining transparency and trustworthiness in decision-making. Let’s look at an elaboration of key components in the architecture.

##### Multi-Layered System

A robust **multi-layered system** ensures that AI-generated responses align with constitutional principles and ethical guidelines while providing an escalation mechanism for human oversight. The various layers of stages of multi-layered system are as follows:

*    Input  Sanitization Layer: This layer preprocesses user inputs to filter harmful, adversarial, or misleading prompts. It uses techniques such as adversarial prompt detection, toxicity filtering, and contextual normalization (e.g., detecting manipulation attempts). This ensures that malicious inputs do not bias the AI model or trigger unintended behaviors.
*   Constitutional Compliance Checker: A model component evaluates responses against predefined constitutional principles (e.g., fairness, non-discrimination, privacy, safety). It uses rule-based filtering and policy-guided reinforcement learning to reject responses that violate ethical standards. It can incorporate constitutional reward modeling, where the AI is trained on human and AI-generated feedback aligned with ethical constraints.
*   Explanation Generator: It provides rationale and justification for generated responses, thereby increasing interpretability. It helps generates human-readable explanations referencing the specific constitutional principles guiding the decision. It can also include counterfactual reasoning and what-if scenarios to determine possible interpretational risks (e.g., "This response was chosen because alternative responses violated fairness constraints.").
*   Fallback Human Escalation Protocol**:** To comply with regulatory organizations that still require human supervision for higher risk scenarios, when the AI model encounters uncertain or ambiguous cases, this system can escalate decisions to human reviewers. A confidence threshold determines the model’s confidence in a response, if lower than a certain preset threshold e.g. 60%, it triggers escalation. At the same time, it maintains a human-in-the-loop (HITL) framework, where human experts review flagged cases and refine AI behavior. The various layers of Multi-layered System and how they are connected is shown in Figure 9.2

![Figure 9. 2 Multilayered System Architecture showing different layers of input processing before generating final output.](media/file39.png)

Figure 9. 2 Multilayered System Architecture showing different layers of input processing before generating final output.

##### Transparency Mechanisms

For Constitutional AI , transparency is critical to ensuring trust, accountability, and compliance. The following mechanisms enhance system interpretability and thereby improve transparency:

*   Principle-Specific Decision Tracing: Decision Tracing involves logging the specific constitutional or ethical principles applied in generating each response. It allows auditors and researchers to trace the reasoning process back to individual ethical guidelines. For example, if a model refuses to answer a question about biometric data privacy, the trace log might indicate "Decision aligned with GDPR and right to privacy principles." Tools such as attention heatmaps for principle alignment, current or applied weights on constitution principles, and logs of human review history can help trace incidences accurately. Attention heatmaps in transformers can be analyzed using sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to identify features influencing attention heads. This method reveals how specific input tokens contribute to model decisions. Attention patterns and constitutional embeddings can be mapped using similarity scoring between attention features and principle embeddings. This can be a source of tracing decisions, and influence of constitutional principles.
*   Confidence Scoring per Constitutional Principles: The process involves assigning confidence scores to each AI decision based on constitutional principles. Each response includes a breakdown of confidence levels per ethical or legal criterion such as fairness compliance or privacy compliance. This approach allows users and regulators to assess the reliability of AI decisions and identify cases where human review is needed.

Successful deployments require balancing principle specificity with model flexibility, as over-constrained constitutions can reduce response quality.

## Ethical Considerations in RL from AI Feedback and Constitutional AI

Reinforcement Learning from AI Feedback (RLAIF) and Constitutional AI represent novel approaches to aligning AI systems with ethical principles. However, their implementation is not necessarily perfect. We will discuss them in this section as follows.

#### Ethical Challenges in RLAIF

RLAIF overcomes some challenges of RLHF, such as human compliance and bias and data collection cost and therefore scales the process. However, there are some limitations that should be considered.RLAIF risks amplifying biases present in the AI feedback model’s training data, especially if fine-tuned models. If the AI feedback model inherits societal biases (e.g., gender stereotypes), these may be amplified during alignment. Similarly, the process might inherently have transparency deficits, as the "black box" nature of AI-generated feedback makes it difficult to audit how decisions align with ethical guidelines. Complex neural networks in RLAIF systems lack interpretability, undermining accountability for harmful outputs. To counter this, transparency mechanisms we discussed in previous section, have to be carefully designed for RLAIF systems also.While RLAIF and Constitutional AI focuses on some defined criteria such as helpfulness, honesty, harmlessness, they often conflict in practice (e.g., truthfulness vs. harm reduction). Moreover, since human feedback is eliminated to improve scalability, over-reliance on automated feedback introduces risks similar to those of large language model deficiencies such as some hallucinations, transparency, bias. In addition, since RLAIF still uses the RL aspect, reward hacking – models gaming the reward model without true compliance or objective following, may occur.Reduced human involvement in RLAIF can be challenging for error resolution and debugging. AI feedback loops may entrench errors such as misrepresentation of semantics without human review, and validation checks, traceability and observability similar to traditional software engineering have to be incorporated.

#### Limitations of Constitutional AI

Predefined constitutions struggle with evolving principles and policies, for example changing societal norms such as shifting views on privacy or gender identity or changes is government policies impacting specific rules. These can make prior rules be outdated leading to responses that may not remain valid. Therefore, some update mechanisms for constitution rules must be designed in. Fixed principles in constitution may also conflict in novel scenarios. For example, autonomous vehicles having to choose between passenger safety and pedestrian rights can be very scenario specific decision. Cultural or contextual blind spots in the constitution can lead to discriminatory or suboptimal outputs, and therefore several validation and test mechanisms need to be incorporated. Reduced robustness against edge cases not covered by the constitution, and guided training of models might be necessary.While constitutional AI can incorporate certain principles and can appear to provide governance to systems, there are no existing universal consensus for ethical AI principles. Corporate-controlled constitutions can easily prioritize brand safety over public interest, if scenarios lend themselves to such prioritization. Moreover, constitutions set might not align with legal standards across geographical regions globally, leading to conflicts in guidelines. Therefore, governance gaps need to be acknowledged.Similar to RLAIF, without human oversight or involvement, for scenarios that are not obvious or designed, and AI feedback loops undergoing rapid number of large turns of AI system and its environment interactions can lead to difficulty in error resolution. The **errors in constitutional interpretation can become self-reinforcing** over time, if unchecked. Once an AI model generates responses deemed "aligned" by another AI model rather than human reviewers, errors may solidify over multiple training cycles. If AI alignment mechanisms operate without robust human scrutiny, organizations may claim their models are "ethically aligned" without ensuring meaningful safeguards—a phenomenon known as "ethics washing." For example, a company deploys an AI chatbot claiming it is fully aligned with human rights but only because it has been fine-tuned using a loosely defined "constitutional dataset" rather than undergoing rigorous external audits.

#### Recommendations for Ethical Implementation

As we just discussed, there are ethical limitations of AI based alignment methods such as RLAIF and Constitutional AI, there are similar challenges with human compliance and bias with RLHF when processes are scaled. Therefore, there need to embed or have processes designed with consideration for facilitating ethical inspection. While AI Ethics and Safety are an emerging field, some minimal framework with room for ethics and safety inspection can be incorporated in the AI pipelines. Let’s look at a few recommendations:

*   Dynamic Constitutions: The problem of constitution aligning with evolving rules and norms can be mitigated by implementing version-controlled principles, that can be updated via public deliberation processes, sourcing environmental changes such as real-time monitoring of societal value shifts or using real-time geospatial maps for autonomous systems. A scheduled inspection of the constitution can also help keeping constitution up to date. The resulting updates are logged in version control, like software code, allowing traceable, auditable changes. To take it a step further, AI systems would re-weight or revise interpretations of constitutional principles in near real-time, while maintaining audit trails.
*   Hybrid Oversight: To counter the problem of AI systems having lack of human involvement, and leading to complex error correction and constitutional misinterpretation and misrepresentation, AI feedback systems can be combined with human-in-the-loop systems. These can be fallback protocols that escalate high-stakes decisions to human experts. Embedding such **escalation systems** for high-stakes or ambiguous scenarios can ensure safety also. For example, the AI model is unsure whether a response involves sensitive medical advice, it flags and routes the query to a domain expert e.g. a medical doctor. In addition to escalation, bias audit systems can be integrated that conduct statistical parity and fairness tests to detect systematic bias.
*   Transparency Mechanisms: Elaborating further from the transparency mechanisms we discussed for Deployment Architecture for Constitutional AI, as these are not just for ethical considerations but facilitate correctness, interpretability and a general good practice. Attention heatmaps are visualizations that help realize which attention head in transformer models of the AI is influenced by what constitutional principle. These can help trace how the AI model made particular decision, once adequate logging of scenarios is implemented and available. For example, a heatmap might highlight that a response was mainly shaped by “non-maleficence” and “freedom of information” clauses. In addition periodic, openly published reports that evaluate performance along key criteria such as fairness, safety and honesty or relevance, can help build trust, and logged scorecards can enable accountability benchmarking over time.
*   Governance Frameworks: To legitimize constitutional principles and ensure their **real-world alignment**, governance must be **multi-stakeholder, inclusive, and domain-sensitive**. An example would be adopting multi-stakeholder constitutions co-designed with ethicists, policymakers, and civil society groups. Or, in case of a company, incorporating all necessary stakeholders such as domain experts for each discipline can make the constitution cover all important aspects of the business. Appointing specialized review boards to conduct pre-deployment reviews, risk assessments, and guide constitution customization for specific domains.

While RLAIF and Constitutional AI offer scalable alignment solutions, their ethical deployment requires addressing bias propagation, value conflicts, and governance gaps. Success hinges on transparent implementation, adaptive principles, and human oversight – not just technical fixes. As noted in the critique of RLAIF methods, these systems must evolve beyond simplistic 3H criteria to grapple with the nuances of human ethics.

## Summary

In this chapter, we discussed how some of the key challenges of RLHF—particularly those related to the scalability and potential bias of human preference data collection—can be addressed using AI-based feedback alternatives such as RLAIF and Constitutional AI. RLAIF follows a process similar to RLHF, but replaces direct human feedback with model-generated preferences, making the approach more scalable. We explored different ways in which AI-generated feedback can be integrated into the training pipeline and noted that this remains an emerging and actively evolving area of research.Constitutional AI offers another promising direction, where human preferences are distilled into a set of guiding constitutional principles that steer model behavior. While these methods reduce reliance on continuous human annotation, they are not without limitations. We emphasized the importance of maintaining up-to-date ground truth information, incorporating conditional human oversight, and establishing transparent mechanisms and robust governance frameworks to ensure ethical and safe AI alignment.The focus of this chapter was on overcoming challenges in reward modeling and improving policy optimization in reinforcement learning by enabling more scalable and accurate feedback mechanisms. In the next chapter, we will explore approaches that go even further—potentially removing the need for reinforcement learning-based policy optimization altogether, using algorithms such as Direct Preference Optimization.
