## Model Steerability and the Limitations of Traditional RLHF

**Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback** (**RLHF**) pipelines typically combine **supervised fine-tuning** (**SFT**), reward modeling, and policy optimization—often using **Proximal Policy Optimization** (**PPO**). While this modular design allows for stage-wise evaluation and debugging, it also introduces significant complexity and fragility. SFT on human-labeled data improves a model’s responsiveness to specific instructions, but it can also induce overfitting, reduce generalization to novel prompts, and even degrade pre-trained capabilities—manifesting as factual errors, hallucinations, or regressions in safety metrics.To address these shortcomings, RLHF incorporates a reward model trained on human preference comparisons, enabling the system to distinguish between better and worse model responses. The policy is then optimized to generate outputs that align with higher-rated preferences. However, reward models themselves are susceptible to overfitting to narrow training distributions, misjudging semantically equivalent rephrasings, and incentivizing unintended behavior—commonly referred to as *reward hacking*. PPO adds further complexity due to its sensitivity to reward scaling, Kullback–Leibler (KL) penalties, hyperparameter tuning, and distributional shifts. As training data evolves, the cost of retraining grows, especially when frequent updates are needed to prevent misalignment and reward exploitation.Despite its limitations, RLHF remains a foundational technique for steering large language models toward desired behavior. By aligning model outputs with human preferences, RLHF enhances model steerability—the model’s ability to adapt its responses to specific user goals or instructions, measurable by how well it satisfies task-specific or user-defined criteria. The paper ​”\[2503.17482v1\] What's Producible May Not Be Reachable: Measuring the Steerability of Generative Models“ (https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.17482v1) studies various models to measure whether steerability is achievable. The paper finds that is it is much harder to steer a model to generate outputs desired responses without deliberately optimization. In contrast, making a model simply generate a response (producibility) is easier. While effective, RLHF based approaches introduce challenges such as reward misalignment, training instability, and resource intensiveness. These drawbacks have motivated interest in alternative alignment strategies that offer greater sample efficiency, robustness, and flexibility in adapting to evolving user needs.One such promising alternative is **Direct Preference Optimization** (**DPO**), which belongs to a broader class of methods known as **Direct Alignment from Preferences** (**DAP**). These approaches bypass the need for an explicit reward model and reinforcement learning by optimizing policies directly from pairwise human preference data. By simplifying the alignment pipeline, DPO addresses many of the fragilities inherent in RLHF—most notably reward model inaccuracies and the instability of policy optimization algorithms like PPO. In the next section, we explore the conceptual underpinnings of DPO and how it rethinks the path from preference data to steerable model behavior.

## Emergence of Direct Preference Optimization

The limitations of traditional RLHF particularly its reliance on brittle reward models and unstable reinforcement learning pipelines-created fertile ground for a paradigm shift. **Direct Preference Optimization** (**DPO**) emerged not merely as a technical tweak but as a conceptual breakthrough, reframing alignment as a direct policy optimization problem rather than an indirect reward-driven task.By 2023, mounting evidence presented pain points of RLHF. Reward models often failed to generalize beyond their training distribution as discussed in the paper *Secrets of RLHF in Large Language Models Part II: Reward Modeling*  ( https://arxiv.org/html/2401.06080v2). They introduced challenges such as reward hacking and computational bottlenecks, as detailed in *Direct Preference Optimization: Your Language Model is Secretly a Reward Model*: (https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.18290). Researchers started exploring the possible hypothesis that modeling human preferences via an explicit reward function might be unnecessary. The policy itself could internalize preferences directly through a clever reparameterization of the reward-policy relationship. This insight bypassed the need for separate reward modeling and RL fine-tuning, collapsing alignment into a single classification objective.This practical pain inspired a foundational question: is it even necessary to model preferences via an explicit reward function? Researchers hypothesized that the policy itself could internalize preferences directly through a clever reparameterization of the reward-policy relationship. This insight bypassed the need for a reward model and a separate RL fine-tuning phase—collapsing the two into a single classification-based objective.DPO offered a simple yet powerful idea: given a prompt and two responses (one preferred, one rejected), fine-tune the model to increase the likelihood of the preferred response and decrease the likelihood of the rejected one. Crucially, the optimization is regularized against a frozen reference model, anchoring the new policy and removing the need for carefully tuned KL divergence penalties that RLHF pipelines required to prevent reward overoptimization and model drift.Initial experiments with DPO demonstrated striking results: it matched or exceeded RLHF’s performance in sentiment control and summarization tasks while requiring 3x less compute. Crucially, DPO proved more robust to distribution shifts, as it avoided the compounding errors of imperfect reward models. These findings catalyzed rapid adoption, with frameworks like Hugging Face’s TRL integrating DPO within months of its proposal.DPO belongs to a growing class of *Direct Alignment from Preferences* (DAP) methods, such as methods discussed in *Direct Language Model Alignment from Online AI Feedback* (https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.04792) and *Direct Alignment with Heterogeneous Preferences* (https://arxiv.org/html/2502.16320v1), that prioritize end-to-end optimization. Where RLHF decomposes alignment into reward learning and policy optimization, DAP methods treat preference data as direct supervisory signals-a shift mirroring trends in self-supervised learning. This approach better accommodates the multifaceted nature of human values, sidestepping the lossy compression of preferences into scalar rewards.Just as self-supervised learning eliminated the need for labeled data in representation learning, DAP methods challenge the necessity of reward models in alignment. Instead of compressing rich, subjective preferences into scalar reward values, DPO allows models to directly model preference relations in a contrastive framework—preserving more of the structure and ambiguity of human feedback.The emergence of DPO marked a turning point, proving that RLHF’s complexity wasn’t inevitable. Its success validated a new design philosophy: alignment could be both simpler and more effective when grounded in the policy’s inherent capacity to model preferences. In the next section, we will elaborate on DPO pipeline and work through the process.

## DPO pipeline

**Direct Preference Optimization** (**DPO**) shifts the paradigm of preference-based alignment, by streamlining the reward modeling and policy optimization phases of traditional RLHF into a single, elegant objective rooted in preference comparisons. This section details how DPO works as a pipeline, how it uses human preferences directly to shape the policy, and how its loss function offers a principled probabilistic framework for preference learning.

### DPO Overview

**Direct Preference Optimization** (**DPO**) represents a significant advancement in language model alignment, offering a more elegant and efficient alternative to traditional **Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback** (**RLHF**). At its core, DPO learns directly from pairwise human preference data—bypassing the need for an explicit reward model or complex reinforcement learning machinery.The foundational insight behind DPO is intuitive: when presented with two responses to a prompt—one preferred by a human and one rejected—the model should be updated to increase the probability of generating the preferred response, while decreasing the likelihood of the rejected one. This is achieved through a contrastive learning framework that treats preference comparisons as a binary classification problem.The DPO training pipeline is streamlined and efficient:**Data Collection**: Human annotators provide feedback in the form of triplets: (prompt, preferred response, rejected response).**Model Initialization**: The policy model is initialized from a pre-trained checkpoint, typically one that has undergone **supervised fine-tuning** (**SFT**).**Reference Model Anchoring**: A frozen copy of the initial model is used as a reference to regularize updates, preventing the policy from diverging too far from its base behavior.**Contrastive Preference Optimization**: The model is fine-tuned using a specialized loss function that encourages it to assign higher log-likelihoods to preferred responses relative to rejected ones.One of DPO’s key innovations is that it reframes the reward maximization objective in RLHF as a more tractable binary classification task over human preferences. This reformulation leads to a training objective that is simpler to implement, more stable to optimize, and often more efficient in practice.

### How Human Preferences Are Used Directly to Shape the Policy

In traditional RLHF, human preferences are first converted into scalar reward values via a learned reward model. The policy is then trained to maximize expected returns using reinforcement learning. **Direct Preference Optimization (DPO)** removes this intermediate step entirely. Instead, it leverages preference data directly, treating pairwise comparisons as **contrastive supervision signals**.For each prompt , human annotators provide a preference between two candidate responses: the **preferred response** and the **rejected response** . The objective is to update the policy π so that it assigns higher likelihood to the preferred response:Rather than enforcing this inequality explicitly, DPO operationalizes it through a loss function derived from the log-likelihoods of both responses. A typical form uses the logistic (sigmoid) transformation to express the preference probability:Here, is a **frozen reference model** (often the supervised fine-tuned checkpoint), and β is a temperature parameter controlling the sharpness of the preference signal. The subtraction of the reference log-probabilities introduces a **KL divergence regularization** effect: it implicitly penalizes the new policy for deviating too far from the reference model.This regularization anchors the updates to known good behavior and stabilizes training. The policy is thus encouraged to improve on human preferences *only when the gain is significant* enough to justify divergence from the baseline—balancing preference alignment with output stability and diversity.

### Loss Function Intuition

The DPO loss function draws inspiration from the principle of maximum likelihood under a Boltzmann rational model of preferences. The underlying assumption is that human preferences can be modeled probabilistically: the likelihood of preferring one response over another is proportional to the exponentiated difference in their log-likelihood scores under the policy.Given a prompt and two responses the **preferred response** and the **rejected response** , the probability that the human prefers over , is modeled as:Here, is a temperature parameter that controls how sharply preferences are enforced. The loss is then defined as the negative log-likelihood of the observed human preference:where:  This subtraction of the reference model's log-probabilities introduces **implicit KL regularization**. The resulting objective ensures that the policy improves only where preferences demand it, maintaining conservatism relative to the reference. This approach is conceptually aligned with **conservative policy optimization**—a technique designed to constrain policy updates to remain within a trust region around a baseline.From a variational perspective, DPO can be interpreted as minimizing a **contrastive KL-divergence** between the policy and a target distribution shaped by preferences, while anchoring the updates using the reference model.

> Note:
> 
> > This loss is commonly implemented using a binary cross-entropy formulation with a sigmoid transformation, as in standard deep learning practice. The use of the sigmoid function provides numerical stability and naturally fits the probabilistic interpretation of preferences.

The DPO pipeline streamlines alignment by directly optimizing on human preferences, bypassing the need for a reward model. With the core concepts in place, we now shift to implementation—showing how to preprocess preference data and train a model using the DPO loss.

## Implementation of DPO

In this section, we walk through the practical steps to implement DPO. Consistent with the approaches in previous chapters, we use the Hugging Face trl library along with a Colab notebook setup for reproducibility and ease of experimentation. For GPU setup refer to *Chapter 8* as well, or keep it consistent as in previous chapters.You can install all the required packages either in your local environment (see the requirements listed at the start of this chapter) or directly within the notebook by running:

```
!pip install -U -q peft==0.15.2 transformers==4.51.3 accelerate==1.7.0 trl==0.17.0 bitsandbytes==0.45.5
```

In our example implementation, we use the Qwen2-0.5B-Instruct model as the base policy. This compact, instruction-tuned model offers a practical balance between computational efficiency and expressive capacity. Its moderate size (500 million parameters) makes it accessible for readers to experiment with on limited hardware, such as a single GPU or a Google Colab instance, while still allowing for meaningful preference-driven updates. Compared to alternatives like Mistral or LLaMA-2, Qwen2 provides strong multilingual capabilities and robust pre-training objectives in an open-weight format, making it an appealing choice for reproducible alignment experiments.Model and Quantization SetupFor our example, we use the Qwen2-0.5B-Instruct model, a compact instruction-tuned causal language model. To enable training and inference on limited hardware such as a single GPU or Google Colab, we load the model using 4-bit quantization provided by the bitsandbytes library. This approach greatly reduces memory footprint and speeds up model loading.While quantization does not alter the DPO algorithm itself, it reflects real-world hardware constraints encountered during human alignment training workflows.For full code details, please refer to the companion notebook in the GitHub repository:[https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback](https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback) (navigate to *Chapter 10*).

### Applying LoRA for Efficient Fine-Tuning

As covered in *Chapter 6*, **Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning** (**PEFT**) using LoRA adapters is crucial for adapting large models efficiently during DPO. The key step is specifying the correct internal layers of the model where LoRA should be applied.For the Qwen2 model, the target modules include:

```
target_modules = [
    "q_proj", "k_proj", "v_proj", "o_proj",
    "gate_proj", "up_proj", "down_proj"
]
```

The following snippet shows how to configure and apply LoRA to the quantized Qwen2 model:

```
from peft import LoraConfig, get_peft_model, TaskType
peft_config = LoraConfig(
    r=8,
    lora_alpha=16,
    target_modules=target_modules,
    lora_dropout=0.05,
    bias="none",
    task_type=TaskType.CAUSAL_LM
)
model = get_peft_model(model, peft_config)
```

> Note:
> 
> > Make sure the target modules align exactly with your base model’s internal layer names to avoid errors. For guidance on inspecting model layers, see the notebook in github repository.

### Loading and Preprocessing Preference Data

The DPO algorithm requires paired examples of human preferences—specifically, prompt-completion pairs where one response is preferred over another. We use a small subset of the UltraFeedback Binarized dataset for illustration. This dataset includes entries with both a preferred (chosen) and a dispreferred (rejected) response to the same prompt.We begin by loading the dataset and structuring it for later use:

```
from datasets import load_dataset
# Load a small sample for testing
dataset = load_dataset("trl-lib/ultrafeedback_binarized", split="train[:100]")
```

Next, we split the dataset into training and evaluation portions. This enables us to measure the generalization performance of the model during fine-tuning.

```
split_dataset = dataset.train_test_split(test_size=0.2)
train_dataset = split_dataset["train"]
eval_dataset = split_dataset["test"]
```

### Tokenizing the Preference Data

To train a transformer-based language model, we need to tokenize our input text. We use the tokenizer associated with the Qwen2-0.5B-Instruct model. For simplicity and diagnostic purposes, we concatenate the chosen and rejected responses in this step. This helps confirm that the tokenizer is behaving as expected. Later, the DPO trainer will process prompts and responses separately.

```
from transformers import AutoTokenizer
# Load the tokenizer
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("Qwen/Qwen2-0.5B-Instruct")
# Tokenization: concatenate chosen and rejected for a sanity check
def preprocess_function(examples):
    input_text = [f"Chosen: {ch} Rejected: {rej}" for ch, rej in zip(examples['chosen'], examples['rejected'])]
    return tokenizer(input_text, padding=True, truncation=True, max_length=512)
# Apply preprocessing
tokenized_train = train_dataset.map(preprocess_function, batched=True)
tokenized_eval = eval_dataset.map(preprocess_function, batched=True)
```

> Note:
> 
> > The concatenated format ("Chosen: ... Rejected: ...") is a placeholder for validation. During DPO training, the model receives separate inputs for the prompt, chosen response, and rejected response. This is handled automatically by the `DPOTrainer`.

### Configuring the DPO Training

The `TRL` library includes a high-level `DPOTrainer` class, which requires a `DPOConfig` object to specify training hyperparameters. Here, we define a minimal training configuration suitable for prototyping on small GPUs or cloud environments like Google Colab.

```
from trl import DPOConfig
dpo_config = DPOConfig(
    output_dir="qwen2-dpo-output",
    per_device_train_batch_size=4,
    gradient_accumulation_steps=4,
    num_train_epochs=3,
    learning_rate=5e-5,
    lr_scheduler_type="linear",
    weight_decay=0.01,
    logging_steps=5,
    report_to="none",  # Disable logging to external tools
    fp16=True           # Use mixed precision if supported
)
```

This configuration allows training on limited hardware. The `gradient_accumulation_steps` setting allows for larger effective batch sizes without exceeding memory limits. You can adjust parameters such as `num_train_epochs` or `output_dir` based on the scale of your data and experiment.In the next section, we’ll load the model in 4-bit quantized form, attach LoRA adapters (as introduced in *Chapter 6*), and proceed to initialize the DPO training loop using DPOTrainer method from trl library.

### Training the Preference-Optimized Model

We initialize the DPOTrainer, providing it with the tokenized training and evaluation datasets:

```
from trl import DPOTrainer
trainer = DPOTrainer(
    model=model,
    ref_model=None,
    args=dpo_config,
    train_dataset=tokenized_train,
    eval_dataset=tokenized_eval,
    processing_class=tokenizer
)
```

With the DPOTrainer fully configured and initialized, we begin training by calling `trainer.train()`. The evaluation set is automatically used for logging evaluation metrics during training. The train method launches the training loop over our tokenized dataset. After training completes, we save the adapter weights and tokenizer for later reuse or deployment. Since we used **Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning** (**PEFT**) with LoRA (introduced in *Chapter 6*), we only need to save the adapter layers:

```
model.save_pretrained("qwen2-lora-adapter")
tokenizer.save_pretrained("qwen2-lora-adapter")
```

The `save_pretrained` method stores the model configuration and adapter weights in the `qwen2-lora-adapter` directory. For the tokenizer, the saved files include:

```
tokenizer_config.json
special_tokens_map.json
vocab.json
merges.txt
added_tokens.json
tokenizer.json
```

These files preserve the vocabulary, special tokens, and tokenizer settings, ensuring compatibility with the fine-tuned model. If you need to reload the fine-tuned model later, first load the base model especially if you are running a new session or a new notebook.

```
from peft import PeftModel
from transformers import AutoTokenizer
import torch
# Reload base model (if not already in memory)
base_model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
    "Qwen/Qwen2-0.5B-Instruct",
    quantization_config=bnb_config,
    device_map="auto"
)
# Load LoRA adapter from local directory
model = PeftModel.from_pretrained(base_model, "qwen2-lora-adapter")
# Load tokenizer from saved adapter directory
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("qwen2-lora-adapter")
```

Once training completes or model is reloaded, we evaluate the model's performance on the held-out evaluation data:

```
results = trainer.evaluate()
print(results)
```

The output of the evaluate run is a number of evaluation metrics. These metrics, give insight into how well the trained model aligns with human preferences:

*   `accuracies`: How often the model prefers the “chosen” over “rejected” response.
*   `margins`: The average reward gap between the preferred and rejected responses.
*   `eval_loss`: A proxy for how well the model is learning the preference signal.

Now that we have walked through the implementation of the classic DPO algorithm—including preference data preprocessing, tokenization, and the training loop—we are well-equipped to explore how this direct optimization framework has been extended. Several recent methods build directly on DPO’s foundation, modifying its loss function or regularization strategy to improve sample efficiency, robustness, or alignment quality. Others take inspiration from DPO but propose conceptually distinct alternatives. In the following section, we will examine key variants of DPO alongside emerging direct alignment methods, highlighting their core innovations and practical implications.

## Beyond DPO: Other Direct Alignment Methods

While DPO has become a widely adopted method for aligning language models without reinforcement learning, it represents just one point in a broader and rapidly evolving design space of preference-based alignment. Researchers have proposed both extensions that refine DPO’s internal mechanisms and entirely new frameworks that preserve its core motivation—learning from human preferences—while departing from its contrastive formulation, loss structure, or data assumptions. In this section, we begin by examining prominent variants of DPO that maintain its foundational structure, and then explore alternative direct alignment methods that offer different perspectives on how to train language models from preferences without relying on reward models or full RL pipelines.

#### Variants of DPO

A number of variants have emerged that preserve the core principles of DPO—direct preference optimization via contrastive supervision—while addressing practical limitations. These methods refine specific aspects of DPO such as its loss function, regularization scheme, or optimization dynamics. Below, we group these variants into thematic categories.

##### Regularization and Constraint Variants

These methods modify how DPO constrains the new policy relative to the reference model.**ORPO** (**Odds-Ratio Preference Optimization**):Removes the need for a frozen reference model by embedding the reference into an odds-ratio formulation, reducing compute and architectural complexity. See more here: [https://arxiv.org/pdf/2403.07691 .](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2403.07691)**simPO** (**Similarity-based Preference Optimization**)Uses the model’s own initial predictions as a soft baseline, allowing for reference-free optimization while maintaining conservative updates. See more here: [https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.14734 .](https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.14734)**β-DPO**Dynamically adjusts the KL penalty strength during training, increasing it when preference signals are weak or noisy and reducing it when they are strong. The paper https://arxiv.org/pdf/2407.08639 explains this method in details.

##### Loss Structure Enhancements

These methods modify the DPO loss function to improve optimization behavior.**IPO** (**Implicit Preference Optimization**)Replaces the Bradley-Terry transformation with an identity link function and incorporates regularization implicitly from preference data, reducing overfitting and reducing dependence on a fixed reference model. See more here: [https://arxiv.org/pdf/2310.12036](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2310.12036) .**CPO** (**Contrastive Preference Optimization**)Adds a supervised term to the DPO loss that explicitly increases the likelihood of the preferred output, addressing the concern that contrastive-only optimization may insufficiently reinforce winners. See more here: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2401.08417 .**KTO** (**Kahneman–Tversky Optimization**)Inspired by *Prospect Theory*, KTO introduces asymmetric treatment of gains and losses into the DPO objective, reflecting how humans weigh preference differently in practice (e.g., loss aversion). See more here: https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.01306 .

##### Alignment Robustness and Multi-Objective Optimization

These methods introduce regularization or auxiliary objectives to reduce misalignment artifacts.**R-DPO**Penalizes undesirable behaviors like excessive verbosity or length bias, which arise when models learn to exploit preference signals. See more here: [https://aclanthology.org/2024.findings-acl.297.pdf .](https://aclanthology.org/2024.findings-acl.297.pdf)**LIFT-DPO**Incorporates instruction-based regularization to teach the model when shorter answers are preferable, improving controllability. See more here: Following Length Constraints in Instructions [https://arxiv.org/html/2406.17744v1](https://arxiv.org/html/2406.17744v1) .**MODPO** (**Multi-Objective DPO**)Extends DPO to jointly optimize for multiple preference dimensions (e.g., helpfulness and harmlessness), typically through weighted or Pareto-optimized combinations of separate DPO objectives. See more here: Beyond One-Preference-Fits-All Alignment: Multi-Objective Direct Preference Optimization: [https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.03708.](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.03708)**SafeDPO**Treats safety as a hard constraint in the DPO process, enforcing refusal behavior or content filtering as part of the preference supervision. See more here: SafeDPO: A Simple Approach to Direct Preference Optimization with Enhanced Safety [https://openreview.net/forum?id=MoJSnVZ59d](https://openreview.net/forum?id=MoJSnVZ59d) .All these methods retain the **contrastive preference modeling** framework at the heart of DPO, but they adapt its structure to better suit real-world data, improve alignment stability, and expand its flexibility. They represent the evolution of DPO within its conceptual boundaries—not departures from it.

#### Conceptually Distinct Direct Alignment Methods

Beyond variants of DPO, a growing number of direct alignment methods have emerged that share the goal of learning from human preferences, but do so using different modeling assumptions, feedback structures, or training paradigms. These methods depart from the original DPO framework and define new directions in preference-based fine-tuning.

##### Listwise Preference Optimization

These methods go beyond binary pairwise supervision by learning from ranked lists of responses.**RRHF** (**Reward-Ranked Fine-Tuning**)Trains the model to imitate the top-ranked outputs in a list of completions, using log-likelihood-based imitation rather than contrastive loss. No reference model or KL penalty is used. See here for more information: https://arxiv.org/abs/2304.05302 .**RAFT**Optimizes directly on sorted outputs using a listwise objective. This approach encourages the model to learn global response quality patterns, rather than just local pairwise preferences. See more here: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2403.10131 .

##### Unpaired Preference Learning

Some approaches remove the need for dispreferred comparisons altogether. **Unpaired Preference Optimization** uses only positive (thumbs-up) feedback, either by treating all other outputs as implicit negatives or learning a reward function indirectly. This departs from DPO’s binary structure. See more here: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2310.12036 .

##### Self-Supervised and Self-Play Preference Learning

These methods generate or judge preference data using the model itself. For example, in **Self-Play Optimization** models generate competing outputs, and a separate judge model (or the model itself) selects the better one. Training proceeds by reinforcing the winning strategy. While this may use DPO-style optimization, it is conceptually closer to iterative self-training or comparison-based imitation. See more here: Self-Play Preference Optimization for Language Model Alignment: https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.00675 **.**

##### Fine-Grained or Token-Level Preference Learning

Rather than optimizing on full responses, these methods apply preferences at the token or step level, aligning internal reasoning processes.Stepwise DPO-like trainingUsed in settings like chain-of-thought prompting, where correct intermediate steps are rewarded, rather than just final answers. See more here: sDPO: Don't Use Your Data All at Once: https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.19270.Token-level preference supervisionStill experimental, this approach aligns generation **word-by-word**, often requiring dense human or model-generated feedback. Token-level Direct Preference Optimization: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2404.11999 .These approaches explore new preference formats, non-contrastive objectives, and more flexible or efficient training frameworks. While inspired by DPO’s direct alignment philosophy, they represent conceptual departures that expand what preference-based training can achieve.Together, DPO and its variants—alongside these newer, structurally distinct methods—form a rich and evolving space of direct alignment techniques. Understanding how these methods relate and differ is essential for selecting the right approach depending on available feedback, alignment goals, and resource constraints.

## Limitations and Practical Considerations

Despite its practical advantages, DPO has several important limitations. Its performance relies heavily on the quality and consistency of preference data, since it does not use explicit reward shaping to compensate for noisy or sparse signals. In tasks requiring structured credit assignments, such as multi-step reasoning, long-horizon decision-making, or fine-grained trajectory control, RLHF with explicit reward models may outperform DPO. This is because reward-based methods can provide continuous scalar feedback at each stage, allowing models to learn more nuanced or hierarchical behaviors effectively. Additionally, DPO’s reliance on pairwise comparisons can reduce data efficiency, especially when annotations are limited or costly. Recognizing these constraints is essential when selecting an appropriate alignment strategy.

| Criteria || DPO || RLHF with Reward Models |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Data Type || High-quality pairwise preference data || Preference data + scalar reward signals |
| Feedback Structure || Pairwise comparisons, simpler annotations || Detailed scalar or trajectory-level rewards |
| Task Complexity || Single-turn, local, short-horizon tasks || Multi-step, long-horizon, hierarchical tasks |
| Compute Implementation || Simpler pipeline, no explicit reward model || More complex, requires reward model training |
| Noise Sensitivity || Sensitive to preference quality || Can compensate with reward shaping |
| Sample Efficiency || Less efficient if data is scarce || More flexible with dense feedback |
| Alignment Control || Conservative updates via reference model || Fine-grained shaping via custom reward |

## Summary

This chapter presented DPO as a scalable, reinforcement-free method for aligning language models directly from human preference data. We began by motivating DPO in contrast to traditional RLHF pipelines, highlighting its elimination of the reward modeling stage and its use of a contrastive log-likelihood objective over preferred and dispreferred responses. We examined how DPO models the probability of preference under a Boltzmann-rational assumption and incorporates a reference policy to regularize updates via implicit KL divergence.The chapter then detailed the DPO pipeline, covering preference data collection, log-likelihood-based training objectives, and the intuition behind the DPO loss. We walked through a complete implementation of DPO, from preprocessing triplet data to configuring training with a reference model and tuning hyperparameters like temperature and batch size.We then surveyed a set of DPO variants that preserve its core structure while modifying loss functions, regularization strategies, or optimization dynamics to improve generalization, reduce overfitting, or support multi-objective alignment. We also distinguished these from conceptually distinct direct alignment methods—such as listwise imitation, unpaired preference optimization, and fine-grained feedback models—that reflect the field’s broader shift toward more flexible, preference-driven alignment strategies. Followed by a brief discussion about limitations of DPO and when RLHF might still be a good alternative.Together, these developments position DPO not only as a practical alternative to RLHF, but as a foundational template for future alignment methods grounded in direct optimization from human preferences.While DPO and its extensions offer powerful tools for training alignment-aware models, the question of how well these models or even RLHF reflect human preferences remains open until evaluated rigorously. In the next chapter, we turn to the topic of model evaluation, exploring both automatic metrics and human-centered approaches for assessing alignment quality, generalization, and behavioral fidelity.
