Throughout this book, we have explored **Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback** (**RLHF**) and emerging AI methods using human feedback as essential frameworks for aligning language models with human intentions and preferences. From reward modeling to policy fine-tuning, our discussions centered on text — a domain where misalignment is visible in every sentence and word. Humans interact with the world through more than language alone: we perceive and value images, auditory signals and music, movements, and combinations of these in complex, nuanced ways. The same core idea that drives RLHF in text — iterative learning from explicit or implicit human signals — can align models in these other domains as well.In this chapter, we ask: How can human feedback guide AI systems in tasks where alignment is even more subjective, creative, or physically embodied? We explore examples spanning images, audio, robotics, and multimodal agents. By stepping beyond text, we uncover RLHF’s full potential as a general framework for human-AI alignment.We are going to cover the following main sections.

*   From Words to Pixels — Image and Visual Generation
*   Teaching Machines to Move — Robotics, Autonomous Systems, and Simulation
*   Harmonizing Sound – Audio and Music Generation

## Technical Requirements

*   For this chapter, implementation is guided using snippets within the chapter, where suitable. Code examples and sample notebooks for this chapter are available in the accompanying GitHub repository: [https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback](https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback), after changing directory to `chapter 12`.

## From Words to Pixels: Image and Visual Generation

As discussed throughout this book, RLHF and other human feedback systems enable us to align language models with human preferences. But these alignment techniques are equally powerful in the visual domain. Image generation models, such as diffusion-based systems and large-scale transformers, can produce remarkably diverse visuals. Yet, "quality" and "aesthetics" are deeply subjective and context-dependent.RLHF provides a systematic framework for capturing and modeling human visual preferences—through explicit ratings or pairwise comparisons—and for optimizing image generation policies to reflect those preferences. In practical implementations, this often involves fine-tuning a diffusion or transformer-based image generator using a reward model trained on human or simulated preference data. In this section, we demonstrate this with a detailed example of aesthetic image generation, showing how simulated human scoring can guide a model toward more pleasing or on-brand visual outputs.As we saw in previous chapters, RLHF has dramatically improved the alignment of text generation models with human expectations. A similar revolution is underway in visual generation. Generative image models like Stable Diffusion, DALL·E, and Midjourney can produce images in virtually any style, yet what constitutes a “good” or “beautiful” image is deeply subjective and contextual. Explicitly coding aesthetic or stylistic preferences into a reward function is nearly impossible, but human judgments can fill this gap.Let’s now move from the conceptual overview to a hands-on demonstration. We demonstrate this concretely through a worked example: **Aesthetic Image Generation with Interpretable Preferences**. In this example, preferences such as “minimalist,” “cozy,” or “warm” are translated into an interpretable scoring function, and we illustrate each step of building, training, and applying a reward model to steer image outputs.Key points for readers:

*   Understand pairwise or comparative preference data for images.
*   See how to train a reward model capturing subjective judgments.
*   Learn how to integrate human feedback into image generation pipelines.

Beyond static images, these principles can extend to video generation and scene editing, where continuous feedback can guide style, narrative flow, or composition in richer ways.

#### Aesthetic Image Generation with Interpretable Preferences

Image generation models like Stable Diffusion and DALL·E have made it possible to create high-quality images from natural language prompts. Yet, these models often produce outputs that vary widely in style, composition, or aesthetic appeal — even for the same prompt. What determines whether an image is “better” or more “pleasing”? Human preferences in visual domains are inherently subjective, but they are also rich with structure.By applying the principles of RLHF, we can train a reward model to capture these preferences and guide image generation accordingly. In this section, we walk through an interpretable and replicable workflow using simple preference heuristics like "warm vs. cold colors" or "cluttered vs. minimalist layout" to train a reward model and re-rank images. This hands-on example shows how alignment techniques can extend beyond text — all the way to pixels. A runnable notebook is provided here: [https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback/blob/main/chapter12/aesthetic\_image\_generation.ipynb](https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback/blob/main/chapter12/aesthetic_image_generation.ipynb). We will now walk through the key steps in that process.

##### Introduction to Visual Preference Alignment

To begin implementing the aesthetic image alignment example introduced above, we start by understanding how to represent human preferences in visual form. In language tasks, we often rely on human-labeled preferences or pairwise comparisons to guide model outputs. The same idea can apply to images — if we can simulate or collect human preferences over visual traits, we can learn a reward model to represent these judgments and train models to optimize for them. This simulated labeling step parallels the preference data collection process discussed earlier for text responses, but here applied to visual attributes.In this example, we define interpretable aesthetic dimensions such as:

*   **Color warmth** (warm vs. cool tones)
*   **Visual simplicity** (minimalist vs. cluttered)
*   **Brightness and exposure**

These visual characteristics are easily explainable to humans and can be measured using simple image features. By creating simulated labels based on these traits, we generate pairwise preference data that can be used to train a reward model. We will now walk through the full process step by step. (Full imports and data-loading code are provided in the accompanying Colab notebook. Only the conceptual core is shown here for clarity.)

##### Setup: Installing Dependencies and Loading the Model

To run this example in a Colab environment or fresh setup, install the required libraries first:

```
!pip install diffusers transformers accelerate
!pip install torch torchvision
!pip install matplotlib pillow tqdm
```

Once installed and imported, we load a pre-trained Stable Diffusion model to serve as our image generator:

```
pipe = StableDiffusionPipeline.from_pretrained("CompVis/stable-diffusion-v1-4")
pipe = pipe.to("cuda")
```

##### Generating Prompted Images

We start by crafting a small set of style-rich prompts for living room interiors:

```
prompts = [
    "A cozy minimalist living room",
    "A cluttered bohemian living room",
    "A modern luxurious living room",
    "A warm rustic living room"
]
```

Each prompt is passed to the diffusion pipeline to generate and visualize images:

```
images = []
for p in prompts:
    img = pipe(p).images[0]
    img.save(f"{p.replace(' ', '_')}.png")
    display(img)
    images.append(img)
```

To diversify the dataset, we synthesize 50 additional prompts by mixing stylistic and emotional adjectives:

```
styles = ["minimalist", "modern", "rustic", "bohemian", "luxurious", "industrial"]
feelings = ["cozy", "spacious", "bright", "warm", "inviting", "airy"]
elements = ["living room", "bedroom", "office", "kitchen"]
prompts = []
for _ in range(50):
    style = random.choice(styles)
    feeling = random.choice(feelings)
    element = random.choice(elements)
    prompt = f"A {feeling} {style} {element} interior design, natural light"
    prompts.append(prompt)
```

Then, generate image from each prompt and save as below:

```
os.makedirs("images", exist_ok=True)
for i, p in enumerate(tqdm(prompts)):
    img = pipe(p).images[0]
    img_path = f"images/image_{i}.png"
    img.save(img_path)
    if i < 3:  # Display first few examples
        print(f"\nPrompt: {p}")
        display(img)
```

![Figure 12. 1 An example of a generated image using Stable Diffusion model and the prompt used for generation.](media/file43.png)

Figure 12. 1 An example of a generated image using Stable Diffusion model and the prompt used for generation.

In Figure 12.1, you can see an example of a generated image and the prompt. In the notebook, we display a small sample of images to inspect the generation process. After generating images, we’ll next simulate human preferences to annotate them.

##### Simulating Preferences with an Interpretable Scoring Function

To incorporate human feedback in a reproducible way, we define a simple **interpretable preference function** that simulates a "human aesthetic score." This score is assigned based on common design principles, explained in *Table 12.1*. In a real alignment pipeline, these scores would come from actual human evaluations; here we use a transparent rule-based function to reproduce the structure of human preference data.

| **Keyword** || **Score effect** || **Explanation** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| minimalist || +2 || Clean, simple, uncluttered |
| cozy || +2 || Inviting, warm, comfortable |
| cluttered || –3 || Messy, overwhelming |
| warm || +1 || Welcoming atmosphere |
| luxurious || +1 || Premium, elegant feel |

Table 12.1 Simulated human aesthetic scores based on keyword effects.To simulate human aesthetic judgments, we define a simple scoring function that assigns points based on keywords:

```
def interpretable_aesthetic_score(prompt):
    score = 0
    explanation = []
    if "minimalist" in prompt:
        score += 2
        explanation.append("minimalist: +2 (clean, simple)")
    if "cozy" in prompt:
        score += 2
        explanation.append("cozy: +2 (inviting)")
    if "cluttered" in prompt:
        score -= 3
        explanation.append("cluttered: –3 (messy)")
    if "warm" in prompt:
        score += 1
        explanation.append("warm: +1 (welcoming)")
    if "luxurious" in prompt:
        score += 1
        explanation.append("luxurious: +1 (premium)")
    return score, explanation
```

##### Creating Pairwise Preference Dataset for Training

Like previous chapters on RLHF and DPO, preference pairs differentiating good and bad examples are useful here. We use the scoring function to simulate human preferences across image pairs:

```
pairs = []
num_pairs = 100
for _ in range(num_pairs):
    idx_a, idx_b = random.sample(range(len(prompts)), 2)
    score_a, explanation_a = interpretable_aesthetic_score(prompts[idx_a])
    score_b, explanation_b = interpretable_aesthetic_score(prompts[idx_b])
    preferred = idx_a if score_a >= score_b else idx_b
    if random.random() < 0.05:
        print(f"\nPrompt A: {prompts[idx_a]}\nScore: {score_a}\n{', '.join(explanation_a)}")
        print(f"Prompt B: {prompts[idx_b]}\nScore: {score_b}\n{', '.join(explanation_b)}")
        print(f"Preferred: {'A' if preferred == idx_a else 'B'}")
    pairs.append((idx_a, idx_b, preferred))
```

These pairs represent preference labels used for training a reward model. We then define a custom PyTorch dataset class to load image pairs and their preference labels:

```
transform = transforms.Compose([
    transforms.Resize((224, 224)),
    transforms.ToTensor(),
])
class PreferenceDataset(Dataset):
    def __init__(self, pairs, prompts, transform):
        self.pairs = pairs
        self.prompts = prompts
        self.transform = transform
    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.pairs)
    def __getitem__(self, idx):
        idx_a, idx_b, preferred_idx = self.pairs[idx]
        img_a = Image.open(f"images/image_{idx_a}.png").convert("RGB")
        img_b = Image.open(f"images/image_{idx_b}.png").convert("RGB")
        img_a = self.transform(img_a)
        img_b = self.transform(img_b)
        label = 1 if preferred_idx == idx_a else 0
        return img_a, img_b, label
```

The preference pairs, prompts, and transform can be easily loaded into the dataset, and torch dataloader allows loading them in batches.

```
dataset = PreferenceDataset(pairs, prompts, transform)
dataloader = DataLoader(dataset, batch_size=4, shuffle=True)
```

##### Reward Model Setup and Architecture

For setting up the reward model and the training architecture, we begin by leveraging a pretrained *ResNet-18* as our image encoder. By replacing its final fully connected classification layer with an identity function, we retain the rich 512-dimensional feature representations from its penultimate layer.

```
feature_extractor = models.resnet18(pretrained=True)
feature_extractor.fc = nn.Identity()
```

Unlike traditional feature extraction, we allow the full model to be fine-tuned — enabling our reward model to adapt more directly to aesthetic signals, even in earlier convolutional layers.

```
for param in feature_extractor.parameters():
    param.requires_grad = True
```

Next, we define a small **reward head** that maps the 512-dimensional image embedding to a single scalar reward:

```
reward_head = nn.Sequential(
    nn.Linear(512, 128),
    nn.ReLU(),
    nn.Linear(128, 1)
)
```

The full reward model can be instantiated as shown in the `RewardModel` class. We then stack a small MLP to output a scalar reward.

```
class RewardModel(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, backbone, head):
        super().__init__()
        self.backbone = backbone
        self.head = head
    def forward(self, x):
        features = self.backbone(x)
        return self.head(features)
reward_model = RewardModel(feature_extractor, reward_head).cuda()
```

##### Learning from Pairwise Preferences

The reward model is trained using the pairwise preference loss. For each labeled pair of images (A preferred over B or vice versa), we compute their respective scores and penalize the model if the less preferred image receives a higher score.

```
optimizer = optim.Adam(reward_model.parameters(), lr=1e-4)
epochs = 10
for epoch in range(epochs):
    running_loss = 0.0
    for img_a, img_b, label in dataloader:
        img_a = img_a.cuda()
        img_b = img_b.cuda()
        label = label.cuda().float()
        score_a = reward_model(img_a).squeeze()
        score_b = reward_model(img_b).squeeze()
        pref_diff = score_a - score_b
        loss = torch.mean(torch.relu(1.0 - (pref_diff * (2 * label - 1))))
        optimizer.zero_grad()
        loss.backward()
        optimizer.step()
        running_loss += loss.item()
    print(f"Epoch [{epoch+1}/{epochs}], Loss: {running_loss/len(dataloader):.4f}")
```

This formulation allows the reward model to approximate the underlying aesthetic function used implicitly by human annotators.Once trained, the reward model can evaluate the aesthetic quality of unseen images.

```
for i in range(3):
    img = Image.open(f"images/image_{i}.png").convert("RGB")
    img_tensor = transform(img).unsqueeze(0).cuda()
    score = reward_model(img_tensor).item()
    print(f"Image {i} score: {score:.2f} — Prompt: {prompts[i]}")
    display(img)
```

Higher scores correspond to images that better match the learned aesthetic preferences, reflecting the model’s internalized reward function. *Figure 12.2* shows examples with scores, where the scores are based on trained reward model:

![Figure 12. 2: Generated images of rooms with reward model based scoring. Left showing a luxurious living room while right showing a rustic bedroom.](media/file44.png)

Figure 12. 2: Generated images of rooms with reward model based scoring. Left showing a luxurious living room while right showing a rustic bedroom.

This approach represents interpretability and modularity. Once we have a reward function trained on preferences, we can plug it into downstream image generation pipelines — for ranking outputs, filtering results, or even guiding a generator's latent traversal using RL or optimization algorithms.

#### Beyond static images

The example we discussed, demonstrated how human preferences can train a reward model to evaluate image aesthetics and guide generation accordingly. While we focused on static visual outputs, these principles generalize beyond single images.Feedback-guided generation extends naturally to dynamic domains. For instance, preference models can rank or optimize entire sequences of outputs — making them applicable to video generation, where temporal coherence and visual appeal evolve over time, or to robotic movement, where smoothness and intention alignment matter across action sequences.In the next section, we explore how preference modeling and learned reward functions can support motion generation and alignment in embodied agents — pushing RLHF beyond vision into the realm of movement, behavior, and control.

## Teaching Machines to Move

Unlike text generation, where "good responses" can be partially defined by relevance or correctness, robotic and autonomous systems operate in complex physical environments where desired behaviors often involve qualitative, subjective criteria. How do we encode "safe and comfortable driving" for an autonomous car? How do we teach a robot to pick objects **gently and naturally**, rather than just successfully? How do we balance energy efficiency, safety, and human comfort in a drone's flight path? How does one numerically encode "comfortable driving," "gentle manipulation," or "natural gait"?**Limitations of Traditional Reinforcement Learning:**Traditional reinforcement learning has many challenges, but some of the important ones emanate from intractability of reward design in physical domains.**Sparse Rewards:** Many tasks provide only a terminal signal (e.g., “goal reached”), offering little guidance for learning intermediate behaviors.**Hand-Crafted Rewards:** Designing detailed reward functions is labor-intensive and prone to “reward hacking,” where the agent optimizes the metric rather than the intended behavior. Hand-crafted rewards often miss qualitative preferences (e.g., smoothness, naturalness).**Multi-Objective Conflicts:** Balancing competing objectives (e.g., speed vs. safety, efficiency vs. comfort) is difficult with fixed reward weights.RLHF and human alignment methods address these challenges by letting humans express subjective or multi-objective preferences (e.g., smoothness, safety, aesthetics) through rankings or demonstrations. These judgments are distilled into a learned reward model that augments or replaces hand-crafted rewards, aligning the agent’s behavior with human expectations.

#### Real-World Examples

In physical domains, defining reward functions that capture human notions of safety, style, and naturalness is notoriously difficult. RLHF and preference‐based methods tackle this by treating humans as the ultimate reward engineers by collecting judgments on robot behaviors and shaping policies to align with our subjective criteria. This section surveys concrete implementations across manipulation, language‐driven control, autonomous driving simulation, and human‐interface studies in robotics and related systems.

##### Preference‐Based Dexterous Manipulation

Robust, human‐like manipulation requires on more than task success, it demands motion quality, adaptability, and gentle interaction. Two leading frameworks illustrate how pairwise rankings can yield rich reward surrogates:**Rank-Game (Microsoft, 2023)** combines a handful of scripted door-opening and pen-spinning demonstrations with human pairwise comparisons of rollout videos. Therefore taking advantage of preference data in addition to expert demonstrations. Annotators simply choose “which looks more stable or natural.” A neural reward model learns to predict these preferences and guides policy search toward fluid, human-aligned motions.**RAPL (Representation-Aligned Preference Learning, Tian et al., 2024)** trains a **Franka Panda** grasping policy using sparse human rankings of grasp executions. Instead of dense labels, RAPL aligns internal vision‐encoder features to human judgments, producing a dense reward predictor that matches supervised RLHF performance with far fewer annotations.

##### Natural-Language-Driven Alignment

Language offers an intuitive interface for specifying qualitative desiderata such as “be gentle near fragile parts,” or “slow down before regrasping”. There have been some early works on mapping free‐form instructions to reward signals. **PromptCraft-Robotics & ChatGPT for Robotics (Vemprala et al., 2023)**, which leverage a large language model (LLM) to parse user prompts into structured preference labels. These labels supervise a surrogate reward model, which is used in subsequent RL fine‐tuning of low‐level controllers to respect speed, force, and trajectory style. More recently, **LLM-Personalize (COLING, 2025)** bootstraps a household‐robot planner on expert demonstrations. Then, iteratively generates plans, collects human rankings on overall plan quality, and fine‐tunes the LLM, achieving a 30% success‐rate boost on multi‐room rearrangement tasks versus baseline planners.

##### Autonomous Driving in Simulation

Simulators enable rapid iteration on autonomous vehicle policies, but realism depends on matching human judgments of natural traffic behavior and comfort. Recent works on this such as **TrafficRLHF (NVIDIA, 2024)** use pairwise human rankings of simulated vehicle trajectories for realism. They train a reward model that guides traffic‐agent policies to produce lifelike flows on benchmarks like **nuScenes** with large public datasets on autonomous driving , enhancing downstream AV module evaluation.In another work, **Multimodal Driving RLHF (Rutgers University, 2025)** collects steering, pedal inputs, VR head orientation, and physiological signals during human‐in-the-loop simulation. An LLM fuses these signals into preference labels, training an AV agent to optimize both safety metrics and rider comfort before any road deployment.**Aurora’s Driver** adopts a proposer-ranker architecture inspired by RLHF principles. The system “proposes” multiple trajectory candidates and “ranks” them using a learned reward model trained on expert driver demonstrations and targeted human preference labels (e.g., comfort, predictability). This blend of inverse RL and preference ranking yields behavior that is both safety-constrained by design and aligned with human style. The blog [https://aurora.tech/newsroom/ai-alignment-ensuring-the-aurora-driver-is-safe-and-human-like](https://aurora.tech/newsroom/ai-alignment-ensuring-the-aurora-driver-is-safe-and-human-like) explains this at a higher level and efforts towards human alignment, and futher details in the patent: [https://patents.google.com/patent/US11561548](https://patents.google.com/patent/US11561548) .

##### Human-Interface & Uncertainty-Aware Learning

How you collect feedback can be as critical as what you collect. Two strands of recent work underscore interface design and uncertainty modeling:**UUPL (Uncertainty-Unified Preference Learning, Peng et al., 2025)** introduces a probabilistic reward‐inference framework that jointly models annotator confidence and sensor noise. It produces more stable policies in ambiguous environments by tempering updates when human rankings disagree.**Interface Impact Study (de Heuvel et al., 2025)** compares 2D-screen versus VR headset annotation of robot navigation rollouts. Thereby, finding that VR’s richer spatial cues significantly improve consistency of human rankings, yielding smoother, more predictable learned paths.

##### Industry Practice and Adoption Challenges

While academic prototypes showcase end-to-end RLHF loops, most commercial robotics and autonomous‐systems vendors still embed human feedback upstream:Grasp‐quality labels in warehouse robots (Covariant, Berkshire Grey) and offline reward tuning in automotive‐assembly arms (BMW, Toyota) likely remain batch processes, not continuous RLHF pipelines.Safety certification, annotation cost, and integration complexity delay on-the-fly preference loops in production systems.As regulatory frameworks and tooling evolve, we should expect more actual RLHF deployments, where operators rank live robot behaviors and policies adapt in situ, moving us closer to seamlessly aligned, trusted autonomous machines.

#### Autonomous Driving Trajectory Smoothness with RLHF (Hands-On Demo)

This section details a practical, hands-on demonstration that vividly illustrates the principles of RLHF in a relatable robotic context. Autonomous driving serves as an ideal illustrative example due to its intuitive nature, widespread recognition, and the clear presence of subjective human preferences (e.g., comfort, smoothness, social acceptability) that are difficult to quantify with traditional rewards.To make these ideas concrete, we present a hands-on example using a Jupyter notebook, see supplementary GitHub repository: [https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback/blob/main/chapter12/CARLA\_trajectory\_tuning.ipynb](https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback/blob/main/chapter12/CARLA_trajectory_tuning.ipynb) . The notebook walks through the full pipeline of applying DPO to trajectory optimization in a simulated driving environment. For keeping focus on the concept and installation complexity low, we use a simpler lane changing scenario. We will not use simulation environment directly, but using this example as a starting point, you can adapt to other simulation environments such as **CARLA**, project name for the open-source **Car Learning to Act** simulator. Next we highlight and discuss key steps, with selected code and output included for clarity.

##### Overview and Motivation

We start with motivating the use of DPO for trajectory alignment, outlining the pipeline.For complete DPO introduction and generic pipeline, you can refer to *Chapter 10*. For trajectory smoothening, the pipeline involves following steps.

1.  **Generate synthetic driving trajectories** with different behavioral patterns (smooth, jittery, aggressive, centered)
2.  **Create preference pairs** based on trajectory smoothness and lane centering
3.  **Train a neural policy** using DPO to learn human preferences for smooth, centered driving
4.  **Evaluate the learned policy** on trajectory quality metrics

##### Synthetic Data Generation

To enable rapid experimentation, the notebook generates synthetic driving scenes that mimic CARLA front-camera views. This includes different driving behaviors (smooth, jittery, aggressive, centered), with code for both frame rendering and trajectory generation. Frame rendering is a simple synthetic road frame: road, lane divider, and a car center offset by `x_offset` and `y_offset`.

```
def render_synthetic_frame(x_offset, y_offset, speed=1.0, img_h=IMG_H, img_w=IMG_W):
    img = Image.new("RGB", (img_w, img_h), (100, 120, 120))
    draw = ImageDraw.Draw(img)
    # road rectangle
    draw.rectangle([int(img_w*0.1), 0, int(img_w*0.9), img_h], fill=(40,40,40))
    # lane center line (dashed)
    for i in range(0, img_h, 8):
        draw.rectangle([img_w//2-2, i, img_w//2+2, i+4], fill=(200,200,200))
    # add roadside color
    draw.rectangle([0,0,int(img_w*0.1),img_h], fill=(30,100,30))
    draw.rectangle([int(img_w*0.9),0,img_w,img_h], fill=(30,100,30))
    # draw ego-car marker near bottom, offset by x_offset,y_offset
    cx = img_w//2 + int(x_offset)
    cy = int(img_h*0.8 + y_offset)
    draw.ellipse([cx-5, cy-5, cx+5, cy+5], fill=(255,60,60))
    # Add a debug print to verify x_offset is actually changing
    if abs(x_offset) > 0.1:
        print(f"Car at x_offset={x_offset:.1f}, cx={cx}")
    return np.array(img)
def gen_synth_traj(kind="smooth", traj_len=TRAJ_LEN):
  x = 0.0
  y = 0.0
  frames = []
  actions = []
  for t in range(traj_len):
    if kind == "smooth":
      dx = np.clip(np.random.randn()*0.8, -2,2)  # gentle drift
    elif kind == "jittery":
      dx = np.random.randint(-25, 26)  # large jumps
    elif kind == "aggressive":
      dx = np.random.choice([-8, 8])
    elif kind == "centered":
      dx = int((0 - x)*0.2) + np.random.randint(-1,2)
    else:
      dx = np.random.randint(-4, 5)
    x = float(x + dx)
    action = 0 if dx < -0.5 else 2 if dx > 0.5 else 1
    actions.append(int(action))
    frames.append(render_synthetic_frame(x_offset=x, y_offset=y))
  return frames, np.array(actions, dtype=np.int64)
```

##### Visualizing Trajectories

Visualization is crucial for understanding agent behavior. The notebook provides utilities to display generated trajectories as GIFs, making the effect of different driving styles immediately apparent.Let's generate and visualize examples of different driving behaviors to understand our synthetic data. The below function `show_gif_inline` will help visualizing the trajectory generated by `gen_synth_traj` function. It displays an in-memory list of RGB frames as an inline GIF.

```
def show_gif_inline(frames, fps=6):
  # Ensure uint8 frames
  frames_uint8 = [(f if f.dtype == np.uint8 else (f*255).astype(np.uint8)) for f in frames]
  gif_bytes = imageio.mimsave('<bytes>', frames_uint8, fps=fps, format='GIF', loop=0, return_bytes=True)
  display(IPyImage(data=gif_bytes))
```

You can generate and show one smooth trajectory by calling these utility functions in the notebook cell.

```
frames_smooth, _ = gen_synth_traj(kind="smooth")
show_gif_inline(frames_smooth)
```

##### Preference Scoring

A key step in preference-based RL is defining what makes one trajectory preferable to another. Here, a scoring function combines smoothness and lane centering, using robust red-pixel detection for the car marker.**Preference Scoring: Defining "Good" Driving**We need to define what makes one trajectory better than another. Our scoring function combines:

*   **Smoothness Score (70% weight)**, which measures how consistent the car's lateral position is across frames and penalizes large frame-to-frame movements. A higher smoothness score means smoother driving.
*   **Centering Score(30% weight) m**easures how close the car stays to the lane center, and penalizes deviation from the middle of the road. A higher score means better lane keeping.

These are realistic metrics from autonomous driving perspective. For the scope of this use case, we will just use these and apply a **red pixel detection** to reliably find the car marker. The color pixel detection is a more robust than brightness-based detection which could confuse lane lines with the car.

##### Neural Network Architecture

We use a lightweight CNN as a frame encoder and a simple policy network, first trained via behavior cloning (supervised learning) and then fine-tuned with DPO. Our system includes a frame encoder, `frame_encoder`, a small CNN that converts 64×64 RGB frames into feature embeddings, and a policy network, `policy_ref` that maps these embeddings to action probabilities (left/straight/right).

```
class SmallFrameEncoder(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, out_dim=64):
        super().__init__()
        self.net = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Conv2d(3, 16, 5, stride=2, padding=2), nn.ReLU(),
            nn.Conv2d(16, 32, 5, stride=2, padding=2), nn.ReLU(),
            nn.AdaptiveAvgPool2d((4,4)),
            nn.Flatten(),
            nn.Linear(32*4*4, out_dim), nn.ReLU()
        )
    def forward(self, x):
        return self.net(x)
frame_encoder = SmallFrameEncoder(out_dim=128).to(DEVICE)
# Policy model (reference policy)
class SimplePolicy(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, emb_dim=128, num_actions=3):
        super().__init__()
        self.model = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Linear(emb_dim, 64), nn.ReLU(),
            nn.Linear(64, num_actions)
        )
    def forward(self, emb):
        return self.model(emb)  # logits
policy_ref = SimplePolicy(emb_dim=128, num_actions=3).to(DEVICE)
```

After deciding the network architecture, we'll first train these networks with behavior cloning on our synthetic action data.

##### Behavior Cloning (Supervised Pretraining)

Before DPO, the networks are pretrained to imitate the synthetic action data, providing a strong initialization. We'll train our frame encoder and policy network to predict the correct steering action for each frame using supervised learning. This gives us:

*   A **reference policy** for DPO training – a strong baseline.
*   Pretrained networks that understand basic driving patterns.

The following snippet of code shows pretraining for behavioral cloning based initialization.

```
# Train encoder + policy_ref on frame->action supervised data (behavior cloning)
bc_epochs = 3
bc_batch = 64
dataset = torch.utils.data.TensorDataset(frames_all, actions_all)
loader = DataLoader(dataset, batch_size=bc_batch, shuffle=True)
opt = optim.Adam(list(frame_encoder.parameters()) + list(policy_ref.parameters()), lr=3e-4)
for ep in range(bc_epochs):
    total_loss = 0.0
    for xb, yb in loader:
        xb = xb.to(DEVICE); yb = yb.to(DEVICE)
        emb = frame_encoder(xb)
        logits = policy_ref(emb)
        loss = F.cross_entropy(logits, yb)
        opt.zero_grad(); loss.backward(); opt.step()
        total_loss += loss.item() * xb.size(0)
    print(f"BC epoch {ep+1}/{bc_epochs} loss: {total_loss/len(dataset):.4f}")
# Save a copy of reference policy logits function (we'll use it for KL)
policy_ref.eval()
frame_encoder.eval()
```

In practice, you will have a more elaborate training and possibly use an existing network architecture and pretrained models. Here we are training for capturing some trajectory patterns. You can change the training epochs and batch size to train longer. Output should look converging and appear like this sample output from behavior cloning training:

```
BC epoch 1/3 loss: 1.0846
BC epoch 2/3 loss: 1.0531
BC epoch 3/3 loss: 0.9862
SmallFrameEncoder(
  (net): Sequential(
    (0): Conv2d(3, 16, kernel_size=(5, 5), stride=(2, 2), padding=(2, 2))
    (1): ReLU()
    (2): Conv2d(16, 32, kernel_size=(5, 5), stride=(2, 2), padding=(2, 2))
    (3): ReLU()
    (4): AdaptiveAvgPool2d(output_size=(4, 4))
    (5): Flatten(start_dim=1, end_dim=-1)
    (6): Linear(in_features=512, out_features=128, bias=True)
    (7): ReLU()
  )
)
```

Once the network is pretrained with basic driving patterns, and you see some level of convergence, we move to the next step.

##### Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) Training

**Direct Preference Optimization** (**DPO**) fine-tunes a policy using preference-labeled trajectory pairs. Unlike RLHF approaches that rely on an explicit reward model, DPO directly optimizes the policy to align with human preferences. We covered this in significant depth in *Chapter 10*. Here, we are applying the same for the motion control application.Some important entities in our setup are as follows.Reference policy: The **behavior cloning** (**BC**) model, acting as the baseline as discussed in the last section.Preference source: Trajectory comparisons based on smoothness scores.Loss function: Binary cross-entropy on the probability of preferring one trajectory over another.Regularization: KL penalty to prevent the fine-tuned policy from drifting too far from the reference.The training process follows these steps:

1.  Sample pairs of trajectories from the dataset.
2.  Score each trajectory using the preference function.
3.  Optimize the policy to increase the likelihood of preferred trajectories.
4.  Regularize with a KL term towards the reference policy.

We now walk through the implementation, breaking it into key stages.**Step 1 — Initialize Policy and Hyperparameters**We start with a copy of the reference policy ( BC based model) and prepare the optimizer and DPO hyperparameters.

```
policy_theta = SimplePolicy(emb_dim=128, num_actions=3).to(DEVICE)
policy_theta.load_state_dict(policy_ref.state_dict())
dpo_epochs = 6
dpo_lr = 1e-3
kl_coef = 0.02 # KL penalty towards reference
temp = 1.0 # temperature scaling
opt_theta = optim.Adam(policy_theta.parameters(), lr=dpo_lr)
```

**Freeze the Reference Policy**To keep the reference fixed during fine-tuning, we set `requires_grad` to False.

```
for param in policy_ref.parameters():
    param.requires_grad = False
```

Next, we define two helper functions used in DPO training.**Step 2 — Utility Functions**Compute trajectory log-probabilities under a given policy, and the KL divergence between the trainable and reference policies.

```
def batch_traj_logprobs(policy, frames, actions):
B,T,C,H,W = frames.shape
emb = frame_encoder(frames.view(B*T,C,H,W).to(DEVICE)) logits = policy(emb).view(B, T, -1)
logp = F.log_softmax(logits, dim=-1)
sel = logp.gather(2, actions.unsqueeze(-1).to(DEVICE)).squeeze(-1)
return sel.sum(dim=1) # per-trajectory
def batch_kl_policy(p_theta, p_ref, frames):
B,T,C,H,W = frames.shape
emb = frame_encoder(frames.view(B*T,C,H,W).to(DEVICE)) logp_theta = F.log_softmax(p_theta(emb).view(B,T,-1), dim=-1) p_ref_probs = F.softmax(p_ref(emb).view(B,T,-1).detach(), dim=-1)
return F.kl_div(logp_theta, p_ref_probs, reduction='batchmean')
```

**Step 3 — Training Loop**Each batch contains paired trajectories (A, B) and a label (0 = A preferred, 1 = B preferred). We compute DPO loss and optionally apply a KL penalty.

```
for ep in range(dpo_epochs):
    total_loss = 0.0
    for idx in torch.randperm(len(pairs)).split(BATCH):
        batch = [(pairs[i], labels[i]) for i in idx]
      
        def prep(frames_actions):
            frames, acts = zip(*frames_actions)
            f = torch.stack([torch.tensor(fr/255.0).permute(0,3,1,2) for fr in frames])
            a = torch.stack([torch.tensor(ac, dtype=torch.long) for ac in acts])
            return f.to(DEVICE), a.to(DEVICE)
        A_frames, A_actions = prep([clips[ia] for (ia, ib), _ in batch])
        B_frames, B_actions = prep([clips[ib] for (ia, ib), _ in batch])
        logp_A = batch_traj_logprobs(policy_theta, A_frames, A_actions)
        logp_B = batch_traj_logprobs(policy_theta, B_frames, B_actions)
        logp_A_ref = batch_traj_logprobs(policy_ref, A_frames, A_actions)
        logp_B_ref = batch_traj_logprobs(policy_ref, B_frames, B_actions)
        targets = torch.tensor([1.0 if lab==0 else 0.0 for _, lab in batch], device=DEVICE)
        logits = ((logp_A - logp_A_ref) - (logp_B - logp_B_ref)) / temp
        loss_pref = F.binary_cross_entropy_with_logits(logits, targets)
        if kl_coef > 0:
            loss = loss_pref + kl_coef * 0.5 * (
                batch_kl_policy(policy_theta, policy_ref, A_frames) +
                batch_kl_policy(policy_theta, policy_ref, B_frames)
            )
        else:
            loss = loss_pref
        opt_theta.zero_grad()
        loss.backward()
        opt_theta.step()
        total_loss += loss.item() * len(batch)
    print(f"DPO Epoch {ep+1}/{dpo_epochs}, loss {total_loss/len(pairs):.4f}")
```

#### Evaluation and Results

Once the model has gone through the training loop and converged, it can be saved and evaluated.**Evaluating the Fine-Tuned Policy**After DPO training, the policy can be evaluated both **quantitatively** (loss, preference accuracy) and **qualitatively** (visual smoothness of trajectories). Visual inspection is especially useful for confirming that the learned behavior reflects the intended qualities (e.g., smoothness) rather than overfitting to artifacts in the training data. The *Figure 12. 3* shows the **top** and **bottom** trajectories according to our smoothness metric, along with metadata.

![Figure 12. 3 Snapshots of lane changing behavior: red dot marker representing vehicle shows centered vs aggressive behavior, and smoothness scores for how much top trajectory moves smoothly vs bottom trajectory. (You can also realize this by running the notebook and realizing the motion trajectories.)](media/file45.png)

Figure 12. 3 Snapshots of lane changing behavior: red dot marker representing vehicle shows centered vs aggressive behavior, and smoothness scores for how much top trajectory moves smoothly vs bottom trajectory. (You can also realize this by running the notebook and realizing the motion trajectories.)

Animated GIFs make it easy to spot qualitative differences in motion smoothness. These visualizations are included in the notebook on GitHub referenced at the start of the section. First we capture some motion trajectories by saving top and bottom GIFs. In order to visualize better and see correlation with labels and preference scores, we add GIF with captions, and display it. The code for this is available at: [https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback/blob/main/chapter12/CARLA\_trajectory\_tuning.ipynb](https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback/blob/main/chapter12/CARLA_trajectory_tuning.ipynb) and shows those selective animated GIFs.For quantitative evaluation, using policy preference scores calculated by the `policy_pref_score` function, we can evaluate which trajectory the policy prefers vs ground truth preference labels.

```
def policy_pref_score(policy, frames):
    # log-prob sequence for actions for visual-only clips
    # argmax of policy probabilities at each frame
    t =torch.tensor(
    np.transpose(np.stack(frames).astype(np.float32)/255.0, (0,3,1,2))
    ).unsqueeze(0).to(DEVICE)  # (1,T,C,H,W)
    with torch.no_grad():
        B,T,C,H,W = t.shape
        frames_ = t.view(B*T, C, H, W)
        emb = frame_encoder(frames_)
        logits = policy(emb).view(B, T, -1)
        probs = F.softmax(logits, dim=-1)
        # compute sum of max action log-probs as proxy score
        maxp, _ = probs.max(dim=2)  # (B,T)
        score = torch.log(maxp + 1e-9).sum().item()
    return score
```

Sample trajectories and their preference match accuracy with respect to ground truth can be computed by comparing to the ground truth labels.

```
# Evaluate on some sample pairs
n_eval = min(40, len(pairs))
correct = 0
for idx in range(n_eval):
    ia, ib = pairs[idx]
    label = labels[idx]
    fa, aa = clips[ia]
    fb, ab = clips[ib]
    # If action logs, use sequence logprob else use proxy
    if aa is not None and ab is not None:
        sA = trajectory_logprob(policy_theta, fa, aa)
        sB = trajectory_logprob(policy_theta, fb, ab)
        preferA = (sA.item() > sB.item())
    else:
        sA = policy_pref_score(policy_theta, fa)
        sB = policy_pref_score(policy_theta, fb)
        preferA = (sA > sB)
    truthA = (label == 0)
    if preferA == truthA:
        correct += 1
acc = correct / n_eval
print(f"Preference-match accuracy over {n_eval} pairs: {acc:.3f}")
```

The preference-match accuracy over `n_eval` pairs might not be sufficient, and can be improved. Next, we list possible approaches to improve this.**Experiment and Improve**To improve the preference match accuracy, especially in practice, consider the following strategies.

*   **Increase Training Data**: Generate more synthetic trajectories and preference pairs. More diverse and numerous examples help the policy generalize better to the preference function.
*   **Enhance Preference Scoring**: Refine the `traj_smoothness_score` to better capture human-like preferences. Consider adding more nuanced features (e.g., penalize lane departures, reward smooth corrections, or include speed consistency).
*   **Tune DPO Hyperparameters**: Experiment with DPO training parameters. 
    *   Increase the number of DPO epochs.
    *   Adjust the KL penalty (`kl_coef`) to balance imitation and preference learning.
    *   Try different temperatures (temp) in the DPO loss for sharper preference distinctions.
    *   Use a lower learning rate for more stable convergence.
*   **Improve Model Capacity**: Use a deeper or wider CNN for the frame encoder, or try a pretrained model (e.g., ResNet18) for better visual feature extraction. Increase the size of the policy network.
*   Data Augmentation 
    *   Apply random transformations (e.g., brightness, contrast, small shifts) to frames to make the policy more robust.
*   **Curriculum Learning**: Start training on easier preference pairs (large score differences), then gradually introduce harder pairs (subtle differences).
*   **Better Action Supervision**: If possible, use more accurate or diverse action labels for behavior cloning to provide a stronger initialization.
*   **Regularization**: Add dropout or weight decay to prevent overfitting, especially if the dataset is small.
*   **Evaluate and Debug**: Visualize failure cases where the policy disagrees with the preference function to identify systematic errors (e.g., confusion in certain visual scenarios).

By iteratively applying these improvements and monitoring the preference match accuracy, you should see measurable gains in alignment between your policy and the preference function. Notice that most of these practices are similar to supervised learning practices.**Broader Implications and Future Directions**Having seen how preference-guided fine-tuning improves driving trajectories, we can generalize these principles to other embodied systems. The principles demonstrated in autonomous driving extend to a wide array of physical AI systems, including drone flight path optimization (for smoothness in delivery or cinematography), warehouse logistics (optimizing robot paths for efficiency and safety in human-shared spaces), surgical robotics (refining precision and gentleness), and assistive robotics (aligning with individual user comfort and preferences).

*   **Convergence with Other Alignment Techniques:** RLHF is increasingly integrated with other methods like interpretability, safety constraints, and causal reasoning to build more robust, trustworthy, and human-centric autonomous systems.
*   **RLHF as the Cornerstone of Aligned Physical AI**: RLHF is indispensable for bridging the gap between objective task completion and the subjective, qualitative nuances of human expectations in real-world robotic tasks.
*   **From "Can It Do It?" to "Can It Do It Well and As Desired?":** This framework shifts the focus from mere functional capability to ensuring that autonomous systems perform actions in a manner that is safe, comfortable, efficient, and ultimately, aligned with human intent and values.

This autonomous driving simplified example exhibits how alignment using human feedback on good and bad trajectories could help improving motion trajectories of cars, merging lanes or cars moving in traffic and their behavior with respect to lanes being only one of the many behavioral scenarios. In the next section, we will discuss a more on how to improve another modality: audio generation.

## Harmonizing Sound: Audio and Music Generation

While RLHF has revolutionized text-based AI alignment, its principles extend powerfully to other modalities—most notably, audio and music generation. In this section, we explore how human feedback can guide generative models to produce sound and music that better align with human preferences.

#### Audio Generation with Human Feedback

To demonstrate RLHF in audio, we leverage Hugging Face’s MusicGen model for text-to-audio generation, alongside librosa and soundfile for feature extraction. Ensure transformers>=4.36 and librosa are installed; these examples assume a GPU runtime for MusicGen. This enables us to generate audio clips from text prompts and analyze their properties:

```
import torch, numpy as np, librosa, soundfile as sf
from transformers import pipeline
from IPython.display import Audio, display
# Load once
generator = pipeline("text-to-audio", model="facebook/musicgen-small")
def generate_audio(prompt: str, seed: int | None = None):
    # MusicGen pipeline doesn't take `seed`; seed the RNG directly.
    if seed is not None:
        torch.manual_seed(seed)
        if torch.cuda.is_available():
            torch.cuda.manual_seed_all(seed)
    out = generator(prompt)  # returns dict or list[dict]
    item = out[0] if isinstance(out, list) else out
    audio = item["audio"]
    sr = item.get("sampling_rate", 32000)
    return audio.astype(np.float32), sr
def extract_features(audio: np.ndarray, sr: int) -> np.ndarray:
    # Simple, transparent proxies for calm vs energetic
    centroid = float(librosa.feature.spectral_centroid(y=audio, sr=sr).mean())
    zcr      = float(librosa.feature.zero_crossing_rate(y=audio).mean())
    return np.array([centroid, zcr], dtype=np.float32)
```

Here, we generate audio from prompts and extract interpretable features—spectral centroid (brightness) and zero-crossing rate (energy)—as proxies for musical qualities like “calm” or “energetic.”

#### Modeling Preferences: Bradley-Terry Approach

To simulate RLHF, we collect pairwise preferences between audio clips and use the **Bradley-Terry model** to learn feature weights that best explain these preferences:

```
import numpy as np
from scipy.optimize import minimize
# comparisons: list of (i, j, y) where y=1 if i preferred over j, else 0
def bt_negloglik(w: np.ndarray, X: np.ndarray, comparisons: list[tuple[int,int,int]]) -> float:
    scores = X @ w  # latent score per item
    loss = 0.0
    for i, j, y in comparisons:
        si, sj = scores[i], scores[j]
        diff = si - sj
        # P(i > j) = sigmoid(si - sj)
        p = 1 / (1 + np.exp(-diff))
        loss -= y * np.log(p + 1e-9) + (1 - y) * np.log(1 - p + 1e-9)
    return loss
def train_bt(X: np.ndarray, comparisons: list[tuple[int,int,int]]) -> np.ndarray:
    w0 = np.zeros(X.shape[1], dtype=np.float64)
    res = minimize(bt_negloglik, w0, args=(X, comparisons), method="BFGS")
    return res.x  # learned feature weights
def preference_score(features: np.ndarray, w: np.ndarray) -> float:
    return float(features @ w)
```

This approach allows us to rank future generations according to learned human-like criteria, aligning outputs with desired properties.

#### Candidate Generation and Ranking

We generate multiple candidate audio clips for a prompt, extract their features, and simulate pairwise preferences. The learned preference model is then used to rank the candidates:

```
# --- Create pipeline once (outside loops) ---
gen_pipe = pipeline("text-to-audio", model="facebook/musicgen-small")
def generate_candidates(prompt, n=3):
    outs = gen_pipe([prompt] * n, forward_params={"do_sample": True, "max_new_tokens": 256})
    clips = []
    for i, out in enumerate(outs):
        audio = out["audio"]
        sr = out["sampling_rate"]
        feats = extract_features(audio, sr)
        clips.append((audio, sr, feats, i))
    return clips
# Example pool
candidates = generate_candidates("a calm piano melody", n=3)
# Build feature matrix for BT training
X = np.array([c[2] for c in candidates])
# Synthetic pairwise prefs: prefer lower centroid
comparisons = []
for i in range(len(candidates)):
    for j in range(i+1, len(candidates)):
        yi = 1 if candidates[i][2][0] < candidates[j][2][0] else 0
        comparisons.append((i, j, yi))
# Train BT weights
w_bt = train_bt(X, comparisons)
print("Learned BT weights:", np.round(w_bt, 4))
# Rank candidates (use learned BT model consistently)
ranked = sorted(
    [(audio, sr, feats, preference_score(feats, w_bt), idx)
     for audio, sr, feats, idx in candidates],
    key=lambda x: -x[3]
)
```

This process demonstrates how RLHF can guide generative models in non-language domains, using human feedback to shape outputs.

#### Visualizing Preference Scores

To illustrate the impact of RLHF, we visualize the preference scores assigned to each candidate, showing how the learned model distinguishes between different audio outputs:

```
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
# --- Generate pre-RLHF candidates ---
pre_clips = generate_candidates("a calm piano melody", n=5)
pre_X = np.array([c[2] for c in pre_clips])
pre_scores = np.array([preference_score(feats, w_bt) for _, _, feats, idx in pre_clips])
# --- Rank post-RLHF candidates (simulated by preference model) ---
ranked_post = sorted(
    [(audio, sr, feats, score, idx)
     for (audio, sr, feats, idx), score in zip(pre_clips, pre_scores)],
    key=lambda x: -x[3]
)
# --- Display scores comparison ---
plt.figure(figsize=(6,4))
plt.bar(range(len(pre_scores)), pre_scores, color='skyblue')
plt.xlabel("Clip index")
plt.ylabel("Preference score (BT)")
plt.title("Preference Scores from Simulated Human Feedback (Bradley–Terry Model)")
plt.show()
```

This visualization highlights the effectiveness of human feedback—even when simulated—in aligning generative models with human values.This example demonstrates that RLHF and human feedback methods are not limited to text. By collecting preferences and training simple models, we can align generative outputs in audio and music domains. This approach opens new possibilities for AI alignment across modalities, ensuring that generated sound and music reflect human values and desired properties. Just as text alignment ensures coherent dialogue, audio alignment fosters emotional and perceptual coherence, making RLHF a unifying framework for aligning multimodal intelligence with human intent.

## Summary

In this chapter, we explored how RLHF and related methods can align generative models with human preferences across multiple modalities, extending beyond text to images, motion trajectories, and audio or music. By collecting human (or simulated) preference scores and training alignment models, we demonstrated how generative outputs can better reflect human values and desired qualities. Through visualizations and complete hands-on examples, you gained an in-depth understanding of how feedback-driven training enhances model alignment. These examples underscore the power of human feedback in shaping AI behavior and highlight the broad potential of these techniques for building multimodal, human-aligned systems.
